| DL-042 | `GET /analytics/trends` returns `{"trends": [ {date, tweet_count, average_doubt_rating, total_likes}, … ]}`, bucketed by day over `tweets.created_at`, across a window of `scanner.analytics.trend-window-days` (default 30). No query parameter is introduced. | (a) Accept `start_date` and `end_date` query parameters, as `backend/tests/test_api.py:L55-59` probes. (b) Bucket by week or month. (c) Return every day since the first tweet. (d) Make the window a `Setting` row rather than a property. | `get_trends()` takes no arguments at `backend/app/api/analytics.py:L14`, so adding a query parameter would change the route contract this migration preserves; the contrary test is itself annotated as possibly needing adjustment and cannot execute. A configured window is therefore the only way to bound the series. Day buckets match `documentation/Technical Specifications.md:L440-441`, which describes the interface as trend visualisations and sentiment over time, and the SRS Feature 7 / F007-1 requirement to aggregate tweet data for trend analysis. (c) makes the response size unbounded. (d) is a reasonable future step but the window is an operator-tuning knob, not a product setting, and DL-040 keeps the seeded set to three product keys. | The window cannot be varied per request, so a client wanting 90 days needs a redeploy or a configuration change. Thirty daily buckets is a fixed response size that will not grow, which is the point, but it also means older data is simply invisible through this route. |
| DL-043 | `SettingsService` is an injected singleton `@Service` whose operations are instance methods; it declares no static member other than its logger and its private constants, and controllers reach it by constructor injection. | (a) Keep the source's call shape literally, as Java `static` methods on the class. (b) A static utility holder that receives a repository handed in per call. (c) A `SettingsService` interface plus a `SettingsServiceImpl`. (d) Field or setter injection instead of a constructor. | `backend/app/api/settings.py:L10,L20` invokes `SettingsService.get_all_settings()` and `SettingsService.update_setting(key, new_value)` statically on the class, and that shape cannot carry the three collaborators the behaviour needs — a repository, a mapper and bound configuration. The conversion is not cosmetic: a `static` method cannot be proxied, so `@Transactional` would not apply to it and the transaction boundary this layer owns would not exist, and it cannot be replaced by a mock in a `@WebMvcTest` slice. The injection rule converts every per-request instantiation and every module-level `get_settings()` read in the source to constructor injection, and this class follows the same rule as the four ported services, so the seven service beans are uniform. (c) would add a type with exactly one implementation and no second caller. (d) hides required collaborators from the constructor signature and permits a partially constructed bean. | The call shape differs from the source, so a reader diffing the Python controller against the Java controller sees an injected field where a class-qualified call stood; the method names and parameter order are unchanged. Any future caller must obtain the bean from the context rather than calling through the class, which is a visible constraint on utility-style reuse. |
| DL-044 | The composed term set is additionally validated against a literal grammar, bounded to the provider rule cap in the terms it collects rather than in the rows it reads, and drawn from a projection of the `name` column alone, paged and filtered a page at a time — see DL-254 and DL-257. The streaming rule set is the configured base terms in `scanner.ingestion.stream-base-keywords` — `AI coding tool`, `AI code assistant`, `AI generated code`, `GPT-4` — unioned with every `ai_tools.name` row, and the whole set is overridable by the `stream_keywords` setting row. | (a) Keep the source's empty list. (b) Hardcode a keyword list in Java. (c) Derive the set from `ai_tools` alone. (d) Derive it from configuration alone. | `backend/app/tasks/tweet_monitoring.py:L53-55` leaves `keywords = []` behind a deferred-work comment and then calls `stream.filter(track=keywords)`; an empty track list is invalid, so ingestion could never start. A keyword set therefore cannot be ported and must be designed. Scanning all four documentation artifacts for named AI coding tools returns only "GPT-4" alongside the generic phrase "AI coding tools", so the base terms are traceable to the documentation's own vocabulary rather than invented. Unioning `ai_tools.name` makes the set grow without a redeploy, which is what that table exists for, and the setting override keeps it operator-editable through the existing `PUT /settings/{key}` route. (c) yields an empty set on a fresh database, reproducing the original defect. (d) makes `ai_tools` decorative. | The default terms are broad, so the stream will match posts that are not about skepticism at all; filtering happens downstream through the popularity gate and the doubt rating. Multi-word terms must be quoted per the X rule syntax, so a term added to `ai_tools.name` with unusual punctuation could produce an invalid rule. A rule set assembled from three sources is harder to reason about than a literal list. |
| DL-045 | Rule reconciliation compares each registered rule's tag as well as its expression and sends its mutations in batches — see DL-261 and DL-254. Ingestion consumes the X API v2 filtered stream at `GET /2/tweets/search/stream`, with rules registered and reconciled through `GET` and `POST /2/tweets/search/stream/rules`. `task/TweetStreamClient` recomposes the rule set and reconciles it before every connection, reads the chunked newline-delimited body by re-assembling complete lines across chunk boundaries, and reconnects indefinitely with exponential backoff and jitter capped at five minutes, floored by the `x-rate-limit-reset` instant after HTTP 429 — bounded to a finite reconnect horizon by DL-280, so no answer can suspend reconnection indefinitely — and preceded by discarding the cached token after HTTP 401. A response body that ends is treated as a fault and reconnected; only a listener result of `false` ends the pipeline. The stream opens only in a process the enablement switch of DL-250 designates, and no ownership lease governs it — DL-281 records that withdrawal. This entry supersedes an earlier form that recorded the endpoint choice alone, and a later one that asserted the withdrawn lease as the condition on which the stream opens. | (a) The v1.1 `statuses/filter` endpoint the source's tweepy call targeted. (b) The v2 sampled stream at `/2/tweets/sample/stream`. (c) Polling recent search on a schedule. (d) A bounded retry count, after which ingestion stays down until the service is restarted. (e) A constant reconnection interval with no backoff and no jitter. (f) Reconciling the rules once at startup and never again. (g) Decoding each chunk to text and splitting the text on newlines. (h) Treating the end of the response body as a normal completion. | v1.1 is legacy and no longer updated, and `statuses/filter` is retired, so (a) is not an option however faithful it would be. The filtered stream is the v2 endpoint that delivers posts matching registered rules, which is exactly what `Stream(...).filter(track=[...])` expressed. (b) delivers a sample of all posts and would need client-side filtering of a firehose. (c) would change the ingestion model from push to pull and multiply request volume. Retries are unbounded because `documentation/Code Structure.md:L1426` records that this function runs indefinitely, so (d) would convert a transient network fault into permanent silent data loss — which is also why the rate-limit floor is bounded rather than trusted, since an unbounded floor reintroduces exactly that outcome through a header value. (e) would reconnect at a fixed rate into an outage and, with several instances, synchronise their attempts; backoff with jitter spreads them. (f) would mean an operator's edit to the `stream_keywords` row or a new `ai_tools` row took effect only after a redeploy, which would defeat DL-044's reason for reading both at all. (g) corrupts a multi-byte character that spans a chunk boundary, because the boundary is chosen by the transport and not by the payload; splitting on the line-feed byte first is safe precisely because UTF-8 never emits `0x0A` inside a multi-byte sequence. (h) would leave ingestion silently stopped whenever X closed an idle connection, which it does routinely. | The filtered stream is a paid-tier capability: the Free tier is suitable only for proof-of-concept work and the Basic tier is the practical production minimum. That is an operational precondition rather than a code concern, recorded here so the constraint is visible. Rule reconciliation is a stateful API interaction this project owns, and reconciling on every connection means a reconnect storm also produces rule traffic. Unbounded retries mean a permanently bad credential produces an indefinite `WARN` cadence rather than a hard failure, so the log is the only signal that ingestion is not working. The five-minute backoff ceiling is a judgement, not a documented X requirement. |
| DL-046 | The stream authenticates with an app-only Bearer Token obtained at runtime by exchanging the consumer key and secret at `POST oauth2/token` under the OAuth 2 client-credentials grant. The OAuth 1.0a user-context properties `scanner.twitter.access-token` and `.access-token-secret` are still declared but are not required for the read path. | (a) OAuth 1.0a user-context request signing, as the source's `OAuthHandler` used. (b) A pre-issued bearer token supplied as its own configuration property. (c) Drop the two user-context properties entirely. | App-only authentication introduces no new mandatory secret, because the consumer key and secret are already declared at `backend/app/core/config.py:L5-6`, and it carries no user context — so it *cannot* publish, which reinforces the never-auto-post invariant at the protocol level instead of only by convention. (a) would sign every request for a capability the read path does not need. (b) would add a secret that can be derived. (c) would reopen the configuration drift those two keys represent, since the source referenced them. | Only one app-only token is valid per application at a time, so two instances exchanging credentials can invalidate each other's token; the client must treat a 401 as a signal to re-exchange. The two user-context properties are declared but unused on this path, which a reader could mistake for dead configuration — they exist to close drift. |
| DL-047 | Deferred work uses `@EnableScheduling`, one `ThreadPoolTaskScheduler` and the completion-based trigger registered by `AsyncSchedulingConfig`; there is no broker or queue. The first pass runs immediately at startup and each later pass is spaced from the completion of the previous one, which is the work-then-sleep order of the source loop — see DL-251. | (a) Keep Celery with Redis or RabbitMQ. (b) use `spring-boot-starter-integration`. (c) use fixed-rate scheduling. (d) run a manual loop. | The source performs work and then sleeps, defining an end-to-start interval. The trigger form preserves that pacing and, with DL-227 and DL-228, resolves the settings-backed interval once per pass, at the moment the next instant is computed. | Scheduling remains in-process, so every application instance runs the pass. Cross-instance duplicate rows are prevented by DL-195, but no dead-letter queue or durable task history exists. |
| DL-048 | A path identifier that no row matches — including a value that is not a number at all — yields HTTP 404 with the route's own not-found body. Controllers therefore declare `@PathVariable String`. | (a) Declare `@PathVariable Long` and let the framework answer 400 for a non-numeric value. (b) Declare `Long` and add a handler mapping the type-mismatch to 404. (c) Validate the identifier's shape and answer 422. | Flask's default path converter is a string, so `/tweets/<tweet_id>`, `/tweets/<tweet_id>/analyze` and `/responses/<response_id>` all accepted non-numeric input, passed it to the service, received `None` and returned 404. (a) would answer 400 or 500 for the same input, which is a contract break the wire contract forbids. (b) reaches the right status through a mechanism that is easy to lose in a refactor, and the mismatch exception would have to be distinguished from every other one. (c) invents a status the source never returned. | A caller cannot distinguish "malformed identifier" from "no such row", which is exactly the source's behaviour and therefore intended. Each controller carries an explicit test for the non-numeric case so the behaviour cannot regress silently. |
| DL-049 | `tweets.id` stays a generated surrogate key and the X post identifier is not carried in it. No natural-key column is declared, so ingestion performs no de-duplication. The same rule governs `responses.id`. | (a) Overload the X post identifier onto the primary key. (b) Add a unique natural-key column holding the X post identifier. (c) De-duplicate by querying for an existing row before each insert, without adding a constraint. | `backend/app/db/models.py:L10` declares an autoincrement-shaped `Column(Integer, primary_key=True)`, and the source persists nothing at all — `backend/app/tasks/tweet_monitoring.py:L29` abandons the constructed object at a deferred-work comment — so there is no de-duplication behaviour to preserve, only one that would have to be invented. (a) would change the column's semantics and risk collisions with the generator. (b) is a schema change, and this migration adds no `UNIQUE` constraint and no new column. (c) is a lost-update race without the constraint (b) would add, so it would give the appearance of de-duplication without the guarantee, which is worse than not claiming it. | A post redelivered by the filtered stream — after a reconnect, or because it matches more than one rule — is stored more than once, and the analytics counts of DL-041 include the duplicates. This is an accepted limitation of the parity mandate rather than an oversight. The extension path is a unique natural-key column, which is a schema change and therefore a separate decision. |
| DL-050 | Bean Validation expresses required-versus-optional only. `@NotNull` appears on exactly two request-body components — `CreateResponseRequest.tweetId` and `UpdateSettingRequest.value` — and nowhere else. No `@Size`, `@NotBlank`, `@Min`, `@Max` or `@Pattern` appears on any DTO or entity, and no entity column carries `nullable = false`, `unique` or a bound beyond what DL-068 and DL-069 record. | (a) Add length, pattern and range constraints wherever a value plausibly has bounds. (b) Add `@NotBlank` instead of `@NotNull` on the two components. (c) Drop `spring-boot-starter-validation` and hand-write the two guards. | The Pydantic schemas declare types and optionality only, with no length, pattern or range constraint anywhere, so this is validation parity rather than validation improvement. The two `@NotNull` annotations correspond to the only two guards the source actually had: `backend/app/api/responses.py:L40-41` and `backend/app/api/settings.py:L17-18`, both of which test for absence. (b) would reject an empty string, but `backend/app/api/settings.py:L17` tests `is None`, so the source *accepts* an empty value — `@NotBlank` would be a behaviour change. (c) would remove a starter this migration names and that is genuinely used at those two points. | The API accepts values a stricter contract would reject: an empty setting value, an arbitrarily long content string, a tweet identifier of any shape. That is deliberate parity, and it means input hygiene rests on the persistence layer and on the identifier handling in DL-048. |
| DL-051 | CORS stays fully permissive, reproducing `CORS(app)` with no arguments through a `CorsConfigurationSource` bean. | (a) Restrict allowed origins to a configured list. (b) Restrict allowed methods and headers to what the eleven routes use. (c) Drop CORS handling entirely. | `backend/app/main.py:L20` calls `CORS(app)` with no arguments, which allows every origin, method and header; tightening it would break the frontend, which is out of scope and cannot be adjusted to match, and it is a behaviour change nobody requested. (a) and (b) are what a hardening pass would do — this is not one. (c) would break the browser client outright. The policy is applied ahead of the authorization rules so that a cross-origin preflight is answered before authentication is demanded, which is the order `backend/app/main.py:L20,L22` establishes by calling `CORS(app)` before `JWTManager(app)`. | Any origin can call every route from a browser. The mitigating factor is that authentication is now genuinely enforced (DL-021) and the token is a bearer token rather than a cookie, so a permissive policy does not by itself enable a cross-site request with the user's credentials. Tightening it later is a one-bean change. |
| DL-052 | The stream paths report term counts and never a term value, and the Notion failure record reports the length of the provider explanation and never its text — see DL-257 and DL-269. Logging is the SLF4J and Logback baseline that ships with the Boot starter. Structured `INFO`, `WARN` and `ERROR` records are emitted at each translation point, around every external call and on every stream reconnection. No `logback-spring.xml` is added. Credential-bearing values are rendered as the fixed marker `***REDACTED***`. | (a) No logging at all, matching the source exactly. (b) A custom `logback-spring.xml` with a JSON encoder. (c) An audit store, a Cloud Logging sink or a retention policy. (d) A correlation-identifier filter. | The retired backend had no logging framework at all — not one `logging`, `logger` or `getLogger` reference under `backend/` — so (a) would be faithful but would leave a service with external integrations and a background stream completely unobservable. Because Spring Boot bundles SLF4J and Logback with the starter, a baseline costs zero additional dependencies, which is what makes it a translation rather than a new capability. (b), (c) and (d) are each a new capability with its own configuration surface, and this migration's scope boundary excludes an observability stack. | Log output is plain text on stdout, so a log aggregator has to parse it. Without a correlation identifier, interleaved records from concurrent requests cannot be grouped. The redaction marker is repeated across several types and must stay consistent if it is ever changed — see DL-063 and DL-067. |
| DL-053 | `scripts/deploy.sh` changes **one** line and nothing else: `npm run build`, executed inside `cd backend`, becomes `mvn clean package`. `gcloud builds submit --tag gcr.io/code-skeptic-scanner/backend` is left standing on the following line, and every other line — the `gcloud run deploy`, the Cloud Function, the Pub/Sub topics, the Firestore rules and the services-describe call — is left exactly as it is. | (a) Rewrite the script to match the Java build end to end. (b) Leave the script untouched, including the npm command. (c) Delete the script. (d) Change `npm run build` and also replace `gcloud builds submit` with the `docker build -f …` and `docker push` pair that `cd.yml` uses, converging the two mechanisms. | The agreed carve-out confines this file to its backend-build line — "Line 18 only … Nothing else changes" — and `npm run build` inside `backend/` is precisely such a reference: it was wrong for a Python backend and is wrong for a Java one. `gcloud builds submit` is a separate line implementing a different mechanism, so replacing it is a second edit the carve-out does not authorise, however sensible convergence would be; (d) was implemented and then withdrawn for that reason, and the asymmetry it leaves standing is argued in DL-215. (a) exceeds the carve-out by a wide margin. (b) leaves a Node command as a Java backend's build step. (c) removes an operational file this migration keeps. | The script still cannot build an image, because `gcloud builds submit` runs from `backend/`, which holds no Dockerfile — a pre-existing defect deliberately left standing, of the same family as the unresolved `var.backend_image` this migration leaves broken (DL-215). A reader could mistake that restraint for an oversight, which is why it is recorded. The changed line assumes Maven is present on the deploying host, where the retired command assumed npm. |
| DL-054 | `scripts/setup_environment.sh` is left untouched, including the `pip install -r backend/requirements.txt` line at `:L12` and the `cp .env.example .env` line at `:L26`. | (a) Change the `pip` line to a Maven command. (b) Delete the script. (c) Replace the whole script with a Java-oriented equivalent. (d) Add the missing `.env.example` the script copies. | The in-scope operations files are named explicitly and scripts are otherwise left structurally alone except where they reference the backend build or image; this script references neither an image nor the build the CD path uses, so no line of it falls inside that carve-out. Both files it names — `backend/requirements.txt` and `.env.example` — were already absent on the source branch, so the script could not run before this work and its failure mode is unchanged. Alternatives (a) to (c) are edits this migration's scope does not authorise, and (d) would add a configuration file this migration does not list. | A reader could reasonably expect the `pip` line to change alongside the Dockerfile and the CI workflow, and its retention reads as an oversight unless this row is consulted. The script remains non-functional. |
| DL-055 | `backend/.gitignore` and `backend/.dockerignore` each hold exactly one line, `target/`, and no comment. The Maven build directory is the only path either file excludes: it is the only build output the Java module produces, the repository has no root `.gitignore` to absorb it, and `./backend` is the Docker build context `.github/workflows/cd.yml` passes. | (a) Carry an explanatory comment in each file recording that the file is net-new and why, which is what an earlier revision delivered. (b) Exclude more — `*.class`, `.idea/`, `.mvn/`, `*.iml` — anticipating developer tooling. (c) Add a root `.gitignore` instead of two module files. (d) Rely on Docker's own layer caching and add no `.dockerignore` at all. | The migration boundary states the content of both files as `target/` only, so a comment is content beyond it — which the review that found this named explicitly. Rule 1 places rationale in this log and forbids it in code, and an explanatory comment inside an ignore file is rationale living outside this log, so removing it satisfies both the boundary and the rule at once. (b) excludes paths nothing in this repository produces. (c) is a repository-wide change the boundary does not admit. (d) would send `target/` into the build context, where it is copied and then overwritten by the build stage. | Neither file records why it exists, so a reader who finds `backend/.gitignore` must come here to learn that it is net-new and that the retired Python tree carried no ignore file of any kind. That is the intended division of labour under Rule 1. A developer whose tooling writes into `backend/` must extend the file or configure the tooling elsewhere. |
| DL-056 | The Docker build context stays `./backend` and the Dockerfile is named out of context with `-f infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend`. The `COPY` instructions address `pom.xml` and `src` relative to that context. | (a) Move `Dockerfile.backend` into `backend/` so no `-f` is needed. (b) Widen the context to the repository root and address `backend/pom.xml` and `backend/src`. (c) Keep the context at `./backend` and copy the whole tree with `COPY . .` as the source Dockerfile did. | `infrastructure/docker/` is where both Dockerfiles live on the source branch and `cd.yml:L36` already passes `./backend` as the context, so naming the file out of context is the smaller of the two possible edits and leaves the frontend Dockerfile's location undisturbed. Alternative (a) moves a file the frozen inventory lists at its existing path. Alternative (b) enlarges the build context to the whole repository, which would send `frontend/node_modules` to the daemon. Alternative (c) would copy `target/` into the image, which is what `backend/.dockerignore` exists to prevent (DL-055). | The Dockerfile path and the build context are specified in two different places, so a future move of either has to update both. A build run from `backend/` without `-f` finds no Dockerfile. |
| DL-057 | The `flake8` and `mypy` steps are removed from the CI backend job rather than replaced by a Java linter, and `pytest` and `python -m build` are replaced by the single command `mvn -B clean verify`. | (a) Add Checkstyle, SpotBugs or PMD as the Java equivalent of `flake8` and `mypy`. (b) Add `mvn -B compile` as a separate lint-like step before the test step. (c) Keep the Python steps and let them fail. | Java compilation is itself the type check that `mypy` approximated, and `mvn clean verify` runs compilation, tests and packaging in one lifecycle, so a separate step would re-run work already done. Adding a static-analysis plugin would be a new capability with its own rule set to curate, which this migration's scope boundary excludes. (c) would keep the pipeline red for a tree that has no Python left in it. | Style and bug-pattern analysis is not enforced in CI, so a convention drift is caught only in review. `mvn clean verify` is a single opaque step: when it fails, the log has to be read to learn whether compilation, a test or packaging failed. |
| DL-058 | Rule 1 is complied with in three concrete ways, and this entry owns all three. **(i) Comments carry facts and a pointer only:** the source path and line range, a faithful-port or net-new marker, and `see docs/DECISION_LOG.md` with the governing identifier where one is assigned. No comment states a reason, an alternative, a trade-off or a risk. A contrast that *is* the contract is stated as two claims and not as a preference between two options: `service/ResponseService` reports the literal of `backend/app/api/responses.py:L65`, and it does not report the one at `:L31`. `rather than` is consequently **inside** the wording set, and the delivered tree carries none of it: the forty-four comment runs that used that construction were rewritten into the two-claim form, and a re-scan after the scope-restoration round rewrote a further seventeen runs across fifteen files, `because` included. A scan of `backend/src/**`, `backend/pom.xml` and the two resource documents for the set now matches no comment line, and `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` matches none either. this file is the one artifact outside that scan, since it is where the reasoning is required to live. The guard decides wordings, not meaning, so it is a floor and not a proof: a comment that argues for a choice in none of those ninety-three wordings passes it, and only review closes that gap. The claim this entry makes is therefore the narrower one — every comment in the delivered tree was read against this rule and rewritten where it argued, and the guard holds the line against the wordings that recur. **(ii) This log carries a leading row-key `ID` column** in addition to the four content columns Rule 1 names, so every decision has an address. **(iii) Entries live in one table ordered by identifier**, with the subject-area grouping delivered as the index above rather than as separate tables per area. A corollary of (ii) is that an identifier's subject is fixed by whatever cites it: `DL-053` owns the one-line `scripts/deploy.sh` change and `DL-056` owns the `.github/workflows/cd.yml` build-context decision, because `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` cites them in that sense. | On comments: (a) Follow the translation-point comment instruction literally and let each comment explain its own choice. (b) Put rationale in code and reduce this log to an index. (c) Omit the provenance markers entirely, since Rule 1 makes this log the source of truth. (h) Leave the comment prohibition to review alone, with no mechanical guard. (i) Keep the guard line-based over `backend/src` and `backend/pom.xml` alone, which is the form the guard first took. (j) Keep the thirty-seven-wording flat list, which is the form a security review found underinclusive. (k) Keep `rather than` outside the set as an admitted contract contrast. (l) Add `, so `, which appears in 224 comment runs, and `since`, `the reason`, `rejected` and `otherwise`. (m) Classify comment sentences with a natural-language model instead of matching wordings. (n) Hold the wording set to comments alone and leave `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` to review, which is how it stood until a review counted seventy occurrences of these wordings in that file. On the `ID` column: (d) Carry only the four columns Rule 1 names and address rows by their Decision text. On layout: (e) Split the entries into one table per subject area. (f) Keep one table but reorder it so areas are contiguous. On the two placements: (g) Move `DL-053` and `DL-056` onto the subjects an earlier reading of the inventory suggested. | Rule 1 forbids rationale in code comments and declares this log the single source of truth for "why", while a comment is separately required at every translation point marking whether a construct is a faithful port or net-new. Both are satisfiable because they govern different content: provenance is a fact, rationale is reasoning. A source pointer plus an identifier is metadata that makes this log *reachable from the code*, which serves Rule 1's own purpose — a reader who lands on a class can find the reasoning — rather than undermining it. (a) would violate Rule 1 outright. (b) inverts it. (h) is how the prohibition was held until a review found rationale in the comments of thirty-six changed files at once: a rule every one of seventy-seven classes must observe, and that is decidable by reading a line, belongs in the suite where it fails the build rather than in a reviewer's attention. (j) and (k) were then withdrawn together by a second finding, which recorded the flat list as underinclusive and named eight sites it passed: a set organised by the kind of claim it forbids can be extended one group at a time and states what it is a proxy for, and closing the `rather than` hole cost forty-four rewrites, each of which turned an ambiguous comparison into two checkable claims. (l) is refused on measurement, not on principle: `, so ` marks a mechanism-to-outcome statement, which is the behavioural contract Rule 1 admits, and forbidding it would strip 224 factual sentences; `since` names a repository or query parameter, or reads as time, in seven of its ten occurrences; `the reason` matches the domain nouns `finish reason` and `reasoning effort`; `rejected` occurs 92 times, every one of them behavioural; and `otherwise` introduces a conditional outcome. (m) would make the build depend on a model's judgement, which is neither reproducible nor offline. (n) is withdrawn on the same measurement that withdrew (h): a rule decidable by reading a line belongs in the suite, and the mapping artifact states that it carries mappings only, so the claim and the check are now the same thing. (c) would discard the traceability this migration requires and that `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` is checked against. (d) is not workable: 193 distinct identifiers are cited by name from 81 files outside `backend/docs` — the 77 source and test files under `backend/src`, plus `backend/pom.xml`, `backend/.gitignore`, `backend/.dockerignore` and `backend/src/main/resources/application.yml`, with `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` alongside them — and `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` names 147, so the identifier *is* the addressing mechanism and removing the column would strand every citation; the column is a row key, not a fifth rationale dimension. (e) and (f) both move rows: an identifier is never renumbered, but splitting or reordering breaks the one property that makes the log auditable — that `DL-n` is found at position n — and 236 of the 296 entries belong to no area the original inventory named, so any grouping of them would be invented rather than recorded. The index above delivers the review-by-area benefit at no such cost. (g) was rejected on evidence: `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` already cites `DL-056` for the `cd.yml` decision and `DL-053` for the `deploy.sh` line, so reassigning either would falsify a live citation and turn a compliant file into a non-compliant one. | Reasoning is one indirection away from the code, so a reader has to open this file to learn why a surprising line exists — which is the trade the rule makes deliberately. The mechanical guard matches wordings, not meaning: a comment that argues for a choice without using one of the ninety-three wordings passes, and a factual sentence that happens to contain one fails and has to be reworded, so the wording set is a proxy for the rule and not the rule itself. The five excluded wordings named in the Rationale column are the widest holes that remain, and `, so ` is the widest of those: a comment may still explain the purpose of a construct in a consequence clause, and only review distinguishes a delivered mechanism from an argument for it. Extending the set to `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` has its own cost: a mapping row that needs a contract contrast now states it as two claims, and a factual sentence carrying one of the ninety-three wordings has to be reworded there as well. Closing the `rather than` hole has its own cost: a contract contrast now takes two sentences where it took one, and a reader who wants the distinction has to read both. The guard also polices itself — every wording it declares is absent from its own comments, so the class that describes the policy cannot state the policy's category nouns in prose and names them in code identifiers instead. Widening the set is not free either — `the reason` was tried and withdrawn, because it also matches the domain noun `finish reason` that `service/LlmService` carries and the word `reasoning` this file's own guard describes itself with. An identifier cited in a comment and never populated here is a defect, which is why the completeness invariant at the top of this file exists. The identifiers are a coupling surface: renumbering or reassigning one silently breaks every citation that names it, and nothing in the build detects that, so the never-renumber policy has to be enforced by review. A single table of 296 rows is long to read front to back, and a reader who ignores the index may not find the area they want; a reader who trusts the index for `DL-061` and above will find no grouping there and must use the matrix instead. The class this entry once named as its covering test is not in the delivered tree: the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary declares nineteen test classes and none of the guard classes that assertion relied on, so they were deleted under DL-216 and this claim is held by review against the delivered artifacts rather than by an assertion in the build. |
| DL-059 | Every pre-existing divergence between the React frontend and the backend contract is left exactly as it is, and the full set is inventoried here rather than partly. **Twenty-two items are confirmed**, each read from a `frontend/**` file that this migration must not edit, and `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` rows A15–A32 carry the same twenty-two so the two artifacts state one inventory. **Five recorded from the start.** (1) The Java service serves the eleven routes unprefixed while `frontend/src/services/api.ts:L25` calls `/api/tweets?page=&perPage=`. (2) It names the page-size parameter `per_page` where that call names it `perPage`. (3) It emits `snake_case` member names where the Zod schemas type `likeCount`, `createdAt`, `doubtRating`, `quotedTweetId`, `userId`, `aiToolsMentioned`, `generatedAt`, `isApproved` and `tweetId`. (4) `frontend/src/services/api.ts:L43` calls a `generate-response` route no backend route declares; the correct operation is `POST /responses` with the body `{tweet_id}`, answering 201 and a `ResponseDto`, so the path, the method's body, the status and the response shape all differ. (5) `frontend/src/utils/api.ts:L2` imports `getAuthToken` from `../services/authService`, a module that does not exist — the import is on **line 2**; `:L13-16` is where the resolved token is written into the `Authorization: Bearer` header, which is the separate piece of evidence that the client expects a bearer JWT. **Seven confirmed by a later review.** (6) **Transport signature** — `frontend/src/utils/api.ts:L8,L19-23` declares `fetchWithAuth(url, method, data?)` with a required `Method`, while `frontend/src/services/api.ts:L26` omits it and `:L34-36,L44-46` pass an Axios-style configuration object in its place, so no call site binds under strict TypeScript. (7) **Double unwrap** — the helper already returns `response.data` at `frontend/src/utils/api.ts:L25-26` and each caller reads `.data` again at `frontend/src/services/api.ts:L27,L37,L47`; no backend body carries a top-level `data` member, so a successful call evaluates to `undefined`. (8) **Unresolved modules** — `axios` and `zod` are imported by `frontend/src/services/api.ts:L1-2` and by all four schema files at `:L1` but are declared in neither `frontend/package.json:L6-28` nor installed, and the `app/utils/api` specifier resolves under no configured alias. (9) **Analyze shape** — `frontend/src/services/api.ts:L11-16,L32-37` types the analysis as `{sentiment: string, keywords: string[]}` while `POST /tweets/{tweetId}/analyze` answers `{tweet_id, analysis_result}`; the members and their types are unrelated, so this is not the naming divergence DL-022 records. (10) **Error key** — `frontend/src/utils/api.ts:L30-31` reads `error.response?.data?.message`, while every backend error body carries the single key `error`, so each approved literal is discarded for a generic fallback. (11) **Date and nullability semantics** — `frontend/src/schema/tweet.ts:L7,L10` and `frontend/src/schema/response.ts:L6` type the timestamps `z.date()`, which rejects the ISO-8601 string Jackson writes; `tweet.ts:L10` uses `.optional()`, which admits `undefined` but not the JSON `null` written for `quoted_tweet_id`; and `frontend/src/schema/setting.ts:L6` and `frontend/src/schema/aiTool.ts:L4-6` require strings where `dto/SettingDto.description` and every `dto/AiToolDto` component may be `null`. (12) **Absent backend origin** — neither `frontend/src/utils/api.ts:L12-23` nor `frontend/package.json:L1-54` supplies a `baseURL`, a `proxy` or any API origin, while the deployment puts the backend on Cloud Run and the frontend behind a storage bucket and CDN. **Ten confirmed by the client/API seam review, which an earlier revision of this row omitted.** (13) **Notion routes with no backend counterpart** — `frontend/src/services/notionService.ts:L16` posts `/api/notion/sync` and `:L37` gets `/api/notion/data`; no controller declares either path, and the Notion adapter is a secondary mirror the backend drives itself with no HTTP surface (DL-088). (14) **Notion module and type resolution** — `notionService.ts:L1` imports `fetchWithAuth` from `'app/services/api'`, which exports only `getTweets`, `analyzeTweet` and `generateResponse`; `TweetData` and `NotionData` are referenced at `:L10,:L34,:L49` and declared nowhere; and `:L40` calls `response.json()` on a value the Axios helper has already decoded. (15) **Five client exports its own consumers import and `services/api.ts` does not declare** — `getTweet` (`frontend/src/pages/TweetView.tsx:L4,L21`), `getResponse` (`frontend/src/pages/ResponseView.tsx:L4,L15`), `getSettings` and `updateSetting` (`frontend/src/components/Settings.tsx:L2,L18,L27`) and `getAnalytics` (`frontend/src/components/Analytics.tsx:L3,L20`); the first two address routes that do exist, `GET /tweets/{tweetId}` and `GET /responses/{responseId}`. (16) **Settings consumer shape** — `Settings.tsx:L9,L18-19` treats the reply as a `Record<string, any>` map and `:L40` iterates its entries, while `GET /settings` answers a JSON array of `{key, value, description}` objects (DL-039); `:L27` calls `updateSetting(key, value)` with a bare value where `PUT /settings/{key}` requires the body `{value}`. (17) **Analytics consumer shape** — `Analytics.tsx:L20` makes one `getAnalytics()` call against two routes, `GET /analytics/trends` and `GET /analytics/summary`; `:L36,L40` read `data.labels` and `data.values`, and `:L65-68` render `totalUsers`, `activeUsers`, `totalRevenue` and `averageOrderValue`. None of those six members appears in `dto/TrendsDto` or `dto/SummaryDto`, and the last four are leftovers from the `financial-dashboard` scaffold `frontend/package.json:L2` still names. (18) **List-envelope drift at the caller** — `services/api.ts:L24` types `getTweets` as returning `TweetData[]` while `GET /tweets` answers the `{tweets, pagination}` envelope of `api/tweets.py:L18-21` (DL-038), so the caller must unwrap once and read `tweets`; `store/tweetSlice.ts:L24` and `services/twitterService.ts:L26-27` both consume it as a bare array. (19) **Caller argument shapes** — `components/Dashboard.tsx:L8` hands `getTweets` itself to `useQuery`, so it is invoked with react-query's context object instead of `(page, perPage)`; `store/tweetSlice.ts:L24` calls `getTweets(params)` with a single object; and `components/TweetAnalysis.tsx:L15` passes the whole tweet to `analyzeTweet(tweetId: string)`. (20) **Field name at a consumer** — `services/twitterService.ts:L27` filters on `tweet.popularity`, a member neither `dto/TweetDto` nor `frontend/src/schema/tweet.ts` declares; the sanctioned client spelling of the backend's `like_count` is `likeCount`. (21) **TypeScript base configuration and further unresolved modules** — `frontend/tsconfig.json:L25-27` extends `tsconfig.base.json`, which is not in the repository, and states it as a one-element array where this TypeScript version requires a string; `@reduxjs/toolkit` is imported by `store/tweetSlice.ts:L1` and `dayjs` by `utils/formatters.ts:L1`, and neither is declared in any manifest section; and `components/Dashboard.tsx:L4-5` imports `./TweetList` and `./PerformanceMetrics`, neither of which exists as a file. (22) **Export shape and router version** — `pages/ResponseView.tsx:L3` imports `{ ResponseManagement }` as a named export while `components/ResponseManagement.tsx:L64` exports a default only, and `src/app.tsx:L2,L18-24` imports `Switch` and uses `Route component={…}`, both removed in the `react-router-dom` v6 that `frontend/package.json:L12` declares. A read-only `tsc --noEmit` over the delivered client reports **67** errors, which is the aggregate of items 6, 8, 14, 15, 19, 21 and 22 together with the `unknown` catch bindings of `frontend/src/utils/api.ts:L30-31`. | (a) Repair the frontend in this change. (b) Move the backend to meet the frontend wherever the frontend is wrong: adopt `/api`, accept `perPage`, emit camelCase, add a `message` alias to the error body, add the `generate-response` route, add `/notion/sync` and `/notion/data`, answer `{sentiment, keywords}` from analyze, return `GET /settings` as a map, add a combined `GET /analytics`, and make `SettingDto.description` and the three `AiToolDto` components non-null. (c) Record the set as one line — that the client does not work — without itemising it. (d) Leave the inventory at the five items it originally carried, or at the twelve a later revision carried. (e) Declare `axios`, `zod` and `@reduxjs/toolkit` in `frontend/package.json` only, on the grounds that a manifest edit is smaller than a source edit. (f) Add `frontend/tsconfig.base.json`, on the grounds that a missing file is an omission rather than a divergence. | All twenty-four `frontend/**` files are entirely out of scope, and this log names the prefix, the parameter name, the camelCase members, the absent `authService` and the absent `generate-response` route as divergences that are explicitly not fixed; every review that confirmed the remaining seventeen read those same files as evidence that must not be edited. (a), (e) and (f) therefore all edit files no scope admits, and (e) additionally buys nothing that works — it would leave the signature, the unwrap, the error key, the schema semantics, the missing exports, the consumer shapes and the origin unrepaired, so the client would resolve three imports and still return `undefined`. (b) is worse than (a): each item changes a contract endpoint parity freezes, three of them are the very divergences this migration must not close, two of them would add routes to a service whose route table is frozen at eleven plus the token endpoint, and for the analyze shape the backend is the source-faithful side — `backend/app/api/tweets.py:L52-55` returns exactly `tweet_id` and `analysis_result`, which is what DL-037 records — so meeting the frontend would break parity to satisfy a scaffold leftover from the `financial-dashboard` project the manifest is still named after. The `setting.ts` and `aiTool.ts` halves cannot be closed from the backend either: neither DTO derives from a Pydantic schema — they are transcribed from `backend/app/db/models.py:L40-44` and `:L33-37` — so the record-mapping rule supplies no required-ness to enforce, and the frozen schema forbids adding the `NOT NULL` that would justify inventing one, which is precisely why the record-mapping alignment DL-080 records reaches `TweetDto` and `ResponseDto` and stops there. (c) is what Rule 1 forbids: a deferral with no inventory cannot be checked against the tree. (d) is the state this rewrite corrects twice over — five of twenty-two, and then twelve of twenty-two, each read as a complete list while omitting items that independently stop the client working, and a review recorded the second of those as a Rule 1 failure. The `authService` line citation is corrected in the same rewrite: the import is at `frontend/src/utils/api.ts:2`, and `:L13-16` is retained only as the Bearer-header evidence it actually is. | None of the client's operations is usable end to end, and each fails for at least three independent reasons, so repairing any single item changes nothing observable: the repair has to be one coordinated frontend change covering the helper signature, the single unwrap, the three dependency declarations, the missing TypeScript base configuration, the import specifiers, the five absent exports, the two absent components, the named-versus-default export, the router version, the error key, the four schema contracts, the analyze type, the two Notion operations, the four caller argument shapes and the backend origin. The failures divide by when they surface. The unresolved modules, the absent base configuration, the absent exports and components, the export shape, the router API and the helper signature stop the client compiling at all — 67 `tsc` errors in total — so they are the loudest and the least likely to reach a user. The double unwrap, the error key and the caller argument shapes are silent at build time and wrong at run time: a successful call yields `undefined`, a failed one reports a generic message in place of the backend literal, and a paged read is issued with no page. The date and nullability mismatches surface as Zod validation failures on bodies the backend considers valid. The two Notion calls and the `generate-response` call would answer 404 against the delivered route table. The absent origin is a transport risk rather than a correctness one: once a token provider exists, the `Authorization` header would be sent to the frontend origin instead of the service, and it is latent only because `authService` does not exist and nothing calls `POST /auth/token` (DL-019). The `aiTool.ts` mismatch is latent for a second reason — no route exposes `AiToolDto`. Every item predates this work, since the same divergences hold between the retired Flask handlers and the same frontend, so leaving them is preservation rather than neglect; but a reader who assumes the two halves interoperate will be wrong, and this inventory and the matrix rows it mirrors are the only places that say so. No backend file carries a comment about any of it, because comments in this tree state provenance and point here. Nothing in the build detects an item added to the client, so a future frontend change can widen this set without this row noticing. |
| DL-060 | The twenty Python files under `backend/app/**` and `backend/tests/**` are deleted, not retained beside the Java tree, and they are deleted in the same change that creates the Java module. | (a) Keep them until the Java tree is complete, then delete. (b) Move them to an `archive/` or `legacy/` directory. (c) Keep the three test files as behavioural documentation. | The Python tree and the Java tree occupy the same directory, and each operational file can reference exactly one of them at any moment: the Dockerfile copies either a Python package or a Maven project, the CI job runs either `pytest` or `mvn verify`. Applying only part of the change would leave a repository where neither backend builds, which is strictly worse than the starting state. The files are also not merely superseded but non-importable — there is no `__init__.py` anywhere, the test files import `fastapi.testclient` against a Flask application and reference modules and symbols that do not exist — so (c) would preserve documentation that never described working behaviour. (b) keeps dead code discoverable at the cost of a directory this migration does not contemplate. | While the migration was in flight the repository held a Java tree that did not yet compile into a runnable jar, with no Python fallback; at this revision `mvn clean verify` succeeds from a clean checkout and `spring-boot:repackage` produces the runnable jar. Every construct the deleted files carried is accounted for in `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` and recoverable from git history, which is what makes the deletion reversible in practice. |
| DL-061 | `entity/Setting.java` maps the two `settings` columns whose names are reserved SQL words with JPA-standard quoted identifiers — `@Column(name = "\"key\"")` and `@Column(name = "\"value\"")` — keeping the physical names exactly as `backend/app/db/models.py:L42-43` declared them. `settings.description`, the table name `settings` and every `ai_tools` column stay unquoted. `spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.auto_quote_keyword: true` is additionally set in both `src/main/resources/application.yml` and `src/test/resources/application-test.yml`; the mapping does not rely on it, and no artifact — the test profile's H2 URL included — carries a vendor-specific keyword escape. | (a) Plain `@Column(name = "key")` / `@Column(name = "value")`, relying on `auto_quote_keyword` alone. (b) Rename the columns, e.g. `setting_key` / `setting_value`. (c) Set `hibernate.globally_quoted_identifiers=true` for the whole application. (d) Remove `auto_quote_keyword` and rely on the explicit mapping alone. (e) Append `NON_KEYWORDS=KEY,VALUE` to the H2 JDBC URL. (f) Backtick quoting, ``@Column(name = "`key`")``. | `KEY` is reserved in H2 2.x and in MySQL, and `VALUE` is reserved in the SQL standard and in H2, and (a) fails **even with `auto_quote_keyword` enabled** — measured, not assumed: with the property set to `true`, Hibernate 6.6.53 emits `create table q_plain (key varchar(255) not null, "value" clob, primary key (key))` on H2 and the same shape on PostgreSQL, quoting `value` but leaving `key` bare, and real H2 2.3.232 then rejects that statement with `Syntax error … expected "identifier"`. Only MySQL's dialect quotes both under the property. The explicit JPA-standard form produced correctly quoted identifiers in all six measured combinations of three dialects and the property on and off, and combining it with the property causes no double quoting. (b) breaks the column-name parity mandate (the data-model parity boundary and its entity-mapping rule) and would falsify the traceability matrix. (c) changes identifier case-sensitivity for all four tables and twenty columns to solve a two-column problem. (d) is defensible on the strength of the measurements, but the property is what covers any future keyword-named column and the mapping is unaffected by it either way, so both are kept. (e) is vendor-specific, fixes only H2, and would have to be threaded through a `DATABASE_URL` that is kept opaque and vendor-neutral. (f) is MySQL-flavoured, whereas Hibernate renders the JPA-standard form with each dialect's own quote character — measured as `"key"` on H2 and PostgreSQL and `` `key` `` on MySQL — so one mapping covers all three supported vendors. | Quoted identifiers are case-sensitive: on H2 the columns exist as lower-case `key` and `value` while unquoted identifiers are folded to upper case (measured: the created `settings` table has columns `DESCRIPTION`, `key`, `value`). Hand-written native SQL must therefore quote them — `select "key", "value" from settings` — and schema-metadata assertions must expect the lower-case names. Nothing inside the application is affected, because Hibernate emits the quoted form consistently in DDL and DML. Two mechanisms now guard the same two columns, so someone removing `auto_quote_keyword` as redundant would be right about these columns and wrong about any future one. The `not null` that appears on the primary key in the generated DDL is JPA's own `@Id` semantics, not an added constraint. |
| DL-062 | `service/SentimentAnalysisService.calculateDoubtRating` returns `10.0` for a `Double.NaN` score through an explicit guard placed ahead of the transcribed expression; the transcribed lines themselves stay byte-for-byte `(1 - sentimentScore) * 5` followed by `Math.max(0.0d, Math.min(10.0d, doubtRating))`. | (a) Keep `Math.max`/`Math.min` alone, letting NaN propagate. (b) Replace the clamp with ordered conditional expressions that reproduce Python's comparison fall-through across the whole domain. (c) Reject a NaN score with an exception. (d) Substitute a neutral `5.0`. | Parity is measured against the source expression `max(0, min(10, doubt_rating))` at `backend/app/services/sentiment_analysis.py:L32`. Python's built-ins return their first argument when a comparison is false, so `min(10, nan)` is `10` and `max(0, 10)` is `10`: the source yields `10` for a NaN score, confirmed by executing the expression. Java's `Math.min`/`Math.max` instead propagate NaN, so (a) returns NaN from a method contracted to return `0.0`–`10.0` and lets a non-finite value reach `tweets.doubt_rating`. NaN is the *only* input where the two differ — ±0.0 and ±∞ agree — so one guard restores bit-identical behaviour over the full `double` domain (verified against a python3 reference for 18 inputs). (b) is equally correct but discards the two transcribed source lines that are fixed verbatim (min nested inside max, no `Math.clamp`, no reordering), which makes the port harder to diff against its source. (c) and (d) invent behaviour the source does not have. | The bound `10.0` now appears twice in the method — as the upper bound and in the guard — and both must move together if the scale ever changes. A NaN score is mapped to the maximum doubt rating rather than reported as an anomaly; the score returned by the Natural Language API is logged at `INFO` by `analyzeSentiment`, so the input stays observable. |
| DL-063 | `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator.TranslatedDatabaseUrl.toString()` renders all three components — `jdbcUrl`, `username` and `password` — as the single fixed marker `***REDACTED***`, returning the same text for every instance. | (a) The compiler-generated record `toString()`. (b) Redact only the password, printing the URL and username in clear. (c) Emit a sanitised URL with the user-info component and credential-bearing query properties stripped. (d) Render the markers tri-state, distinguishing an absent value (`null`) and an empty value (`""`) from a present one. (e) Drop the override and forbid logging the record by convention. | A value that arrives already in JDBC form is passed through exactly as supplied (DL-064) and may embed credentials in its user-info component or in query properties such as `?user=…&password=…`, so any rendering of the URL text can disclose a credential (CWE-532); (a) and (b) both do, and (b) additionally printed the username, which is credential material. (c) would require a second security-critical parser and would still reveal host and database name for no diagnostic gain. (d) still discloses whether a credential is configured at all, which is the same disclosure that was removed from `config/ScannerProperties` for its fourteen redacted components; using one marker keeps a single convention across both types and cannot leak by construction. (e) depends on every present and future caller remembering, and the record is the natural thing to interpolate into a startup log or an assertion failure. | Someone diagnosing a connection failure cannot read the URL out of a log record that interpolates this record, and cannot tell from that record whether a credential was configured. The values remain available through the `jdbcUrl()`, `username()` and `password()` accessors for code that genuinely needs them, and `translate` still logs the resolved vendor at `INFO` on the translation path. |
| DL-064 | `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator.translate` tests the value exactly as supplied for the literal lower-case prefix `jdbc:` using `String.startsWith`, and returns that same value unchanged. No value is trimmed, case-folded or otherwise normalised anywhere in the class, including on the parse path. | (a) Trim first and match the prefix case-insensitively, returning the trimmed value. (b) Trim for detection only but return the original value. (c) Keep trimming and document it as intended behaviour. | The specified contract is that a JDBC value is returned unchanged and is not parsed, normalised or stripped (DL-027 and DL-064), which a trimmed or case-expanded return value does not satisfy — and the class Javadoc already claimed that behaviour while the code trimmed, so the two disagreed. (b) would hand a whitespace-padded URL to the DataSource it had just recognised, converting a clear startup failure into a driver-level one. (c) makes silent correction part of the contract and keeps the class free to alter a value it must not touch. Consuming the value exactly as supplied also matches SQLAlchemy, which rejects a padded URL rather than trimming it, so failing fast is the source behaviour as well. | A `DATABASE_URL` padded with whitespace, or written with an upper-case `JDBC:` prefix, now fails at startup instead of being silently corrected: a leading pad raises `DATABASE_URL is not a parseable URL (Illegal character in scheme name)` and an upper-case prefix raises the unsupported-scheme error naming the supported schemes. Where a pad falls decides the outcome, and the case matrix measured the asymmetry rather than assuming symmetry: a *leading* pad displaces the prefix, so `String.startsWith` does not match and the value goes to the parse path and fails there, while a *trailing* pad leaves the prefix intact, so a `jdbc:` value padded only on the right matches and is returned with its trailing whitespace still attached, exactly as supplied. That is the specified behaviour — a recognised JDBC value is returned unchanged and is never normalised — and the padded value then fails, if it fails at all, in the driver rather than in this class. Both fail fast at boot with an actionable message and neither can produce a half-configured DataSource, but an operator who previously relied on the trim will see a new startup failure. |
| DL-065 | `exception/BadRequestException`, `exception/NotFoundException` and `exception/ResponseGenerationException` each declare `private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;` and each is `final`. | (a) Carry `@SuppressWarnings("serial")` and declare no identifier. (b) Leave the `javac -Xlint:all` `[serial]` warning in place. (c) Avoid `RuntimeException` so the types are not serializable. | No instance of these types crosses a serialization boundary — each is created, thrown, caught by the error-handling advice in the same JVM and rendered as a JSON body — so any of (a) and a declared identifier are equally sound on the merits, and the tree must be clean under `-Xlint:all`, which rules out (b). A declared identifier was chosen because it is the resolution the review named first, because it removes the warning at its cause rather than hiding the category, and because these three types now expose a closed set of client-visible messages through static factories (DL-066): a fixed identifier makes the wire-literal contract explicit rather than leaving it to a compiler-generated value that changes whenever the class shape does. (c) is not available — an unchecked exception has to extend `RuntimeException`, which is `Serializable`. | The literal `1L` is a value no consumer checks today, so it carries no information; if one of these types is ever genuinely serialised across versions the identifier must be maintained deliberately. Nothing in the service serialises an exception, and introducing that would be an architectural change carrying its own entry. |
| DL-066 | `exception/BadRequestException` and `exception/NotFoundException` declare a private constructor and expose their approved wire literals only through public constants and one static factory per literal (`tweetIdRequired()`, `updateDataRequired()`, `noValueProvided()`, `tweetNotFound()`, `responseNotFound()`, `responseNotFoundOrUpdateFailed()`, `settingNotFound()`); `exception/ResponseGenerationException` carries one fixed message and exposes only `()` and `(Throwable)`. A triggering throwable attaches through `withCause(Throwable)` or the cause constructor and is reachable only through `getCause()`. | (a) Keep a public `String` constructor, as a literal reading of the error-envelope requirement implies, and rely on call sites passing only the approved literals. (b) Validate a supplied message against an allow-list at construction time. (c) Map arbitrary messages to a generic body inside the error-handling advice. | A public `String` constructor lets any caller — including one relaying a database driver message, a vendor API response or a value read from configuration — put arbitrary server-side text into the `error` value of a client response (CWE-209). (a) makes that a convention rather than a guarantee. (b) adds a runtime check for something the type system can make unrepresentable. (c) moves the guarantee into a component that does not exist yet and would still let the wrong text reach a log-and-rethrow path. Closing the constructor makes the eight wire literals the complete client-visible message set by construction, and each factory carries its literal unaltered so every one still round-trips through `getMessage()` character for character, which is what the preserved error bodies actually require. | `new NotFoundException("…")` and `new BadRequestException("…")` no longer compile; call sites must use the factories. No source in the module constructs these types today, so nothing needed changing, but a later checkpoint adding controllers or services must use the factory names. Adding a new client-visible 4xx literal now requires editing the exception type as well as its call site. |
| DL-067 | `dto/LoginRequest` overrides `toString()` to render **both** components as `***REDACTED***` and binds its `password` member write-only (`@JsonProperty(access = WRITE_ONLY)`); `dto/TokenResponse` overrides `toString()` to render `accessToken` as `***REDACTED***` while rendering `tokenType` and `expiresIn` as they are. Deserialization and serialization are otherwise untouched: `LoginRequest` still binds both JSON members and `TokenResponse` still emits `access_token`, `token_type` and `expires_in`. | (a) Rely on a documented "instances of this type are never logged" invariant, which is what the records originally carried. (b) Redact the credential component only, leaving the submitted principal name in clear. (c) Redact without the write-only binding. (d) Omit the credential component from the record and pass it separately. | A record's compiler-generated `toString()` renders every component, so a plain interpolation into a log line, an assertion message or an exception message discloses a submitted password or a live bearer token (CWE-532); (a) makes that a convention that every present and future caller has to remember, which is the fragility the review flagged. (b) still discloses the principal name, which identifies the single configured account. (c) leaves the plaintext able to reach a response body if the record is ever serialised, so the write-only binding is defence in depth; it was measured not to affect deserialization. (d) changes the wire contract of an endpoint this migration fixes. `tokenType` and `expiresIn` stay visible because they are not secret and are what makes a token response diagnosable. | A rendered `LoginRequest` no longer identifies which principal a failed authentication attempt used, so diagnosis relies on the authentication layer's own logging. The marker text `***REDACTED***` is repeated across `ScannerProperties`, `TranslatedDatabaseUrl` and these two records and must stay consistent if it is ever changed. |
| DL-068 | Ten of the eleven character columns carry `@JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.LONGVARCHAR)` and no other facet: no `length`, no `columnDefinition`, no `@Lob`, no `nullable = false` and no `unique`. They are `tweets.content`, `tweets.media`, `tweets.quoted_tweet_id`, `tweets.user_id`, `tweets.ai_tools_mentioned`, `responses.content`, `ai_tools.name`, `ai_tools.description`, `settings.value` and `settings.description`, and each dialect renders them as its widest character type that needs no declared capacity — `text` on MySQL, `varchar(32600)` on PostgreSQL and H2. The eleventh, `settings.key`, carries no JDBC type code at all because it is the indexed primary key; DL-069 owns it. No `columnDefinition` appears anywhere in the delivered main sources. | (a) `columnDefinition = "varchar"`, which an earlier revision of this entry recorded as delivered. (b) A bare `@Column` naming the column and nothing else, taking Hibernate's undeclared-length default of `varchar(255)`. (c) `@JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.LONG32VARCHAR)` or `@Lob`. (d) `length = Integer.MAX_VALUE`. (e) An explicit portable length such as 65535 or 768. (f) A Hibernate `TypeContributor` registering a capacity-free DDL type for `SqlTypes.VARCHAR` globally. | The schema must be creatable by `spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto` on every vendor the migration declares a driver for, because that is the only schema-bootstrap mechanism in the tree and no requirement admits pre-created tables or a manual step. The mapping was chosen by measurement, not by reading: the generated DDL of all four candidates was produced for the MySQL, PostgreSQL and H2 dialects and then **executed** against a real MySQL 8.4 server, a real PostgreSQL 16 server and H2 in memory. `columnDefinition = "varchar"` emits a bare `varchar` on MySQL, and `create table probe_bare (c varchar)` fails there with `ERROR 1064`; no facet at all emits `varchar(255)` on all three, which truncates a 280-character post and a several-hundred-character generated reply; `@JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.LONG32VARCHAR)` emits `longtext`, `text` and `clob`, and is disqualified by Hibernate's own type system rather than by any vendor — an attribute carrying that code is not string-like, so `like` raises `SemanticException` and `lower()` and `length()` raise `FunctionArgumentException`, on every dialect and on the converted `List<String>` attributes too. `@JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.LONGVARCHAR)` emits `text` on MySQL and `varchar(32600)` on PostgreSQL and H2, passed every HQL operation on all three engines, and `ddl-auto=update` was idempotent across two runs. (a) is the form this entry carried before and is withdrawn: it is not valid MySQL DDL, and this entry's own risk column previously admitted that a MySQL deployment would need pre-created tables — which is the defect, stated in the log rather than fixed. (b) and (e) add a bound the source did not have, and (b)'s 255 is narrow enough to reject a post `tweets.content` can legitimately carry. (c) and (d) move the columns onto a long or large-object family and, as measured, break string operations at the HQL level. (f) reaches the same rendering but moves the contract out of the entity into a Hibernate SPI registration that carries no compatibility guarantee across releases. Note that `SqlTypes.LONGVARCHAR` was never evaluated in this entry's earlier forms at all, which is why the defect survived three revisions. | PostgreSQL and H2 render a capacity of 32,600 characters, which is a bound the mapping does not declare but the dialect does; a value longer than that is rejected on those vendors and accepted on MySQL, so the three are not byte-identical. 32,600 is two orders of magnitude above the longest value any write path in this service produces. An existing database created under the withdrawn mapping keeps its own column types, because `update` never alters an existing column, so a redeployed instance diverges from a freshly created one. Every generated column type is asserted for all three dialects, and three further assertions hold the generated statements to the vendor invariants that made the earlier mapping unusable — no large-object character type, every capacity-requiring type carrying a capacity, and every primary-key column declared in an indexable type. |
| DL-069 | `settings.key` is the quoted natural primary key and is the one character column that carries no JDBC type code: `@Id @Column(name = "\"key\"")` and nothing else, which every supported dialect renders as `varchar(255)`. It is therefore indexable on MySQL, PostgreSQL and H2 alike. It declares no `length`, no `columnDefinition`, no `nullable = false` and no `unique`; the 255 is Hibernate's own default for an undeclared length and is not a value this migration chose. | (a) `columnDefinition = "varchar"`, which an earlier revision recorded as delivered. (b) `@JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.LONGVARCHAR)`, the mapping the other ten character columns carry. (c) `length = 768` — the widest a `utf8mb4` column can be while fitting InnoDB's 3072-byte index-key prefix. (d) An unbounded LOB or TEXT mapping. (e) Replace the natural key with a surrogate. (f) Rename the column. (g) A narrower portable bound such as 191. | This is the only character column that is also an index, and that single fact decides it: MySQL cannot index a `text` column without a prefix length, which was verified by execution — creating this table with the key mapped `LONGVARCHAR` fails on a real MySQL 8.4 server with `ERROR 1170 (42000): BLOB/TEXT column 'key' used in key specification without a key length`. So (b), which is right for the other ten columns, is unavailable here, and (d) fails for the same reason. (a) is withdrawn: a bare `varchar` is not valid MySQL DDL, and this entry previously stated that MySQL 'cannot express this column at all' and that a MySQL deployment must carry a pre-created `settings` table — an admission of the defect rather than a resolution of it. (c) and (g) add a capacity the migration invented, where 255 is the framework's own undeclared-length default. (e) and (f) change the source's primary key, which the data-model parity clause freezes. | A settings key is bounded at 255 characters. The three keys the seeding pass writes are at most 26 characters and it is the only writer of new keys, because `PUT /settings/{key}` answers the source's `Setting not found` literal for a key that does not already exist — so nothing in the application can reach the bound. This is a departure from the other ten character columns and from this entry's earlier claim of a capacity-free rendering, and it is the price of a schema that MySQL can actually create. A database created under the withdrawn mapping keeps its own column type. |
| DL-070 | `entity/AiTool.id` is typed `Integer` and `repository/AiToolRepository` is declared `JpaRepository<AiTool, Integer>`, matching `entity/Tweet` and `entity/Response` under DL-138. | (a) `Long`, the identifier type the literal prose signature states for this repository and which this entry authorised in an earlier form. (b) `Long` on the field with `@JdbcTypeCode(SqlTypes.INTEGER)` to keep a 32-bit column. (c) `Long` plus `columnDefinition = "integer"`. | `backend/app/db/models.py:L35` declares `Column(Integer, primary_key=True)`, and because `ddl-auto=update` generates the schema the Java type decides the physical column type. The migration requires identical column types and the entity-mapping rule admits `Integer` and `Long` alike, so the narrower goal governs and `Integer` is the mapping: a `Long` identifier was measured to generate `bigint` on H2 2.3.232, `int8` on PostgreSQL 16.14 and `BIGINT` on MySQL 8.4, none of which is the 32-bit column the source declared. This makes all four identifier columns in the schema — the three primary keys and the `responses.tweet_id` foreign key — uniform, so DL-023's conversion to a `String` at the DTO boundary is uniform too, and `AnalyticsService` reads only `count()` from this repository, so no arithmetic depends on the width. (b) reaches the right column type but leaves the field and the column disagreeing and adds a Hibernate-specific annotation to an entity that otherwise uses only `jakarta.persistence`. (c) is not portable across the three vendors. | `ai_tools` is capped at 2^31−1 rows, which is what the source declared. Because `ddl-auto=update` never narrows a column, a database created while this entry authorised `Long` keeps `bigint` and diverges from a freshly created one. The wire contract is unaffected: the identifier serialises as a `String` either way (DL-023). |
| DL-071 | `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` holds one `Vendor` constant per JDBC driver the executable jar carries, and emits that vendor's JDBC authority prefix: `jdbc:postgresql://`, `jdbc:mysql://` and `jdbc:h2://`. There are three, matching the three drivers AAP section 0.5.1.1 declares at `runtime` scope, and `VENDOR_BY_SCHEME` maps five scheme spellings onto them — `postgresql` and `postgres` onto PostgreSQL, `mysql` and `mariadb` onto MySQL, and `h2` onto H2. | (a) Two vendors only, omitting H2 on the ground that no deployment uses it, which an earlier revision of this entry recorded as delivered. (b) One vendor constant per scheme spelling, so `postgres` and `mariadb` each carry their own. (c) No vendor enumeration at all: rewrite the scheme in place and let the driver decide. (d) Add vendors the jar carries no driver for, so a URL naming one fails in the driver rather than in this class. | AAP section 0.6.5.1 states the translation matrix, and it includes `h2 -> h2`; omitting it made a documented input an unsupported-scheme failure. That the literal transcription is also executable was verified rather than assumed: H2's own driver accepts `jdbc:h2://localhost/db` and `jdbc:h2://mem:name`, and `java.net.URI` parses `h2://sa:pw@localhost:9092/scanner` with the user-info split correctly, so the generic authority-prefix reassembly needs no special case for it. (b) would state one vendor twice and lose the point that `mariadb` deliberately resolves to the MySQL driver (DL-187). (c) discards the fail-fast on an unrecognised scheme, which is the class's main diagnostic. (d) promises support the class path cannot honour. | The supported-scheme set is now a three-vendor, five-spelling contract, and a deployment naming any other scheme fails at startup with the supported set in the message. An H2 URL is a supported production input even though no production deployment should use one — which is the cost of transcribing the matrix literally, and is preferred to silently refusing a documented input. |
| DL-072 | `TranslatedDatabaseUrl.jdbcUrl()` never carries credential material **on the parse path**, and carries whatever the operator supplied on the `jdbc:` pass-through path. On the parse path the user-info component of the authority is split into the username and password and omitted from the reassembled URL, and a query property named exactly `user`, `username`, `uid`, `password`, `passwd`, `pwd`, `password1`, `password2` or `password3` is removed from the query and fills whichever credential the user-info left unset. **Every other query property is retained verbatim**, including one whose name happens to contain `password`, `secret`, `token` or `credential`, because AAP section 0.6.5.1 reassembles the query as supplied. On the `jdbc:` path the value is returned character-for-character with both credentials null, so a `jdbc:` URL carrying `?user=` and `?password=` is used exactly as written. | (a) The two-class taxonomy an earlier revision of this entry recorded as delivered: a **primary credential** property extracted on the parse path and rejected on the pass-through path, and a **secondary secret** property — any name containing `password`, `passwd`, `pwd`, `passphrase`, `secret`, `credential` or `token` as a substring — rejected on **both** paths. (b) Extract the primary credentials on both paths, parsing the `jdbc:` value to do it. (c) Retain everything on both paths, extracting nothing, and let the driver read the credentials from the query. (d) Reject any URL carrying credentials anywhere, requiring separate username and password properties. | (a) is withdrawn because it contradicted the specification it claimed to implement: section 0.6.5.1's own worked example preserves the query — `postgresql+psycopg2://u:p@localhost/scanner?sslmode=require` becomes `jdbc:postgresql://localhost/scanner?sslmode=require` — and the substring rule rejected `?sslpassword=x`, a PostgreSQL property that is part of a legitimate TLS configuration, so a correct deployment failed at startup. The pass-through half of (a) was worse: it made a `jdbc:` URL carrying credentials a startup failure, when the same section says to pass through unchanged anything already beginning with `jdbc:`. That was verified by booting the delivered jar with a `jdbc:postgresql://` URL carrying `?user=USERNAME&password=PASSWORD`, which now starts and serves and previously did not. (b) parses a value the specification says not to parse. (c) loses the guarantee that a translated URL is safe to log. (d) invents an input contract the source did not have. | A `jdbc:` URL is logged only as the fact that a JDBC URL was supplied, never as its text, so credentials in one are not exposed by this class — but they are not extracted either, so they reach the driver in the URL and may appear in a driver-level diagnostic this service does not control. On the parse path a property whose name suggests a secret is retained and reaches the reassembled URL, which is the behaviour the specification requires; the reassembled URL is therefore not unconditionally free of secret material, only of the nine primary credential names and of user-info. That is a narrower guarantee than this entry's earlier form claimed, and it is stated plainly here. |
| DL-073 | `service/SettingsService` tolerates degenerate input rather than letting a framework exception escape: a `null` key given to `updateSetting` is reported as absent through `NotFoundException.settingNotFound()` without reaching the repository.
