(b) Reject a `null` key with `BadRequestException`. (c) Assert the key with `Objects.requireNonNull`, raising `NullPointerException`. (d) Seed `stream_keywords` with the configured terms rendered and normalised here, which is what this entry previously recorded. (e) Normalise the terms by reusing `util/DelimitedStringListConverter`. | Spring Data asserts a non-`null` identifier, so (a) turns a `null` key into `InvalidDataAccessApiUsageException`, which the error-handling advice renders as HTTP 500 — a key that names no row is exactly the 404 branch of `backend/app/api/settings.py:L21-22`, and the Flask route could never produce a `null` key because its path converter always yields a string, so 500 would be a status the source can never return. (b) would emit the wrong literal, since `No value provided` describes the body rather than the key. (c) would surface as 500 as well. On the seed value, (d) is withdrawn: a seeded copy of the configured terms is a second declaration of the rule set, and an operator who then changed `scanner.ingestion.stream-base-keywords` would see no effect while the stale row overrode it — a blank seed leaves the override inert and keeps one declaration site (DL-044). It is unreachable through `PUT /settings/{key}`, whose path converter always yields a string, so it is defence for direct callers only and costs a branch no route exercises; a caller that passes `null` by mistake sees `Setting not found` instead of a null-pointer diagnostic, which is less pointed about the real fault. A reader following this identifier from an older commit for the seed rendering will not find it: `DL-044` states the blank seed and `DL-257` the grammar the terms are held to. |
| DL-074 | The `actions/setup-node` pin in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` stays at `'14'`, exactly as the pre-refactor workflow declared it, and no frontend step in that workflow is altered. | (a) Raise it to `22.23.2`, the version installed on the build host. (b) Raise it to the lowest release satisfying the platform's Node floor, `20.20.2`. (c) Remove the pin and let the runner default decide. | The frontend steps of this workflow are deliberately left untouched and 0.2.3 places `frontend/**` entirely out of scope, so every line of the Node.js step is frozen; the frozen frontend carve-out is the authoritative reference and it carves out no exception for the runtime pin. The platform's Node floor governs the toolchain this agent runs while building, not the contents of a job this migration declares untouchable — the backend job reads nothing from the Node toolchain, so the floor and this pin never meet. (a) was delivered first and changed a line the refactor had no authority over, which made it a deviation with no authorising requirement rather than a decision. (b) has the same defect at a lower version. (c) makes the toolchain version implicit and unreproducible. | The workflow's frontend job stays exactly as broken as it was before the refactor — `npm ci` with no committed lock file, and `npm run lint` and `npm run type-check` naming scripts `frontend/package.json` does not declare — which this migration explicitly requires be left that way. A reader may reasonably take the `14` pin for an oversight; this entry is the record that it is not. |

(d) Omit the key entirely when the aggregate is null. | Zero is a value the data genuinely holds — a doubt rating of 0 is the score of a maximally positive post — so (a), which was delivered first, made "no rated tweet" indistinguishable from "every tweet rated 0", and it contradicted both records' own documentation, which declares the mean components nullable. `TweetRepository` documents the null case and assigns it to the caller; the caller's job is to report it, not to erase it. | DL-076 | `POST /responses` reports exactly two outcomes and never 404. `service/ResponseService.generateResponse` rejects a `null` or empty `tweet_id` with `BadRequestException.tweetIdRequired()` and reports every other failure through the single `ResponseGenerationException` literal: an identifier carrying no number, an identifier naming no `tweets` row, a failure raised inside `LlmService` and a failure raised while saving the row. (b) Report an unparseable `tweet_id` as 400, since the value arrives in the request body rather than in the path. (c) Use `isBlank()`, so a whitespace-only identifier is rejected as 400. (d) Back both 404 messages with one constant and emit it from both routes. | `backend/app/api/responses.py:L46-49` branches on the truthiness of the value the service returned and has exactly two arms, 201 and 500. The imported module defined none of the four methods, so the only value it could yield for an identifier it failed to resolve was `None`, which reached `:L49`; the route has no 404 arm to port. (a) and (b) would emit statuses that route can never produce, and (a) would additionally emit a literal belonging to `backend/app/api/tweets.py:L32`. (c) is ruled out by the guard's own semantics: `if not tweet_id` at `:L40` is falsy for the empty string alone, and `'   '` is truthy, so a whitespace-only value passed that guard and failed later as an unresolvable identifier; `isBlank()` would move it from 500 to 400. A reader may see the two near-identical 404 constants as duplication and merge them, silently changing the `PUT` body — the class documentation and this entry record that they are deliberate. | DL-077 | **Superseded by DL-048, DL-123, DL-217, DL-225 and DL-232.** This identifier originally carried three unrelated subjects at once — pagination-argument normalisation, `generated_at` minting and raw-identifier parsing — and every code site that cited it now cites the entry that owns its own subject: query-parameter binding and the substitution of a default for an absent, blank or unconvertible value in DL-217; the lower bounds the two list services apply and the absence of any `per_page` ceiling in DL-123; a `page` whose first row lies beyond the offset `PageRequest` can express in DL-225; `generated_at` minting in DL-232; and the 404 a path identifier matching no row receives in DL-048. (b) Retire this identifier and renumber the successors into the gap. (c) Delete the row. (d) Leave the pointer naming DL-200, DL-201 and DL-202. | A citation is only checkable if it resolves to an entry about the one thing the reader is looking at, and an identifier covering three subjects cannot be checked — which is how the false pagination statement recorded here survived review. (b) would break every citation in version history that names DL-077. (c) would leave a dangling identifier in the commits that cite it. Rewriting in place, as DL-099 directs, keeps the identifier resolvable and says where each subject went. | A reader following a DL-077 citation from an older commit takes one extra hop to reach the subject they need, and this row can only point — it states none of the five contracts itself. No artifact in the delivered tree cites this identifier: the ten sites that did now cite DL-123, DL-217 or DL-186's neighbours directly, so the pointer serves version history alone. DL-099 and DL-232 name it as context and resolve here rather than to substance; because this row names the owner of each subject, that hop is informative rather than misleading. |
| DL-078 | `api/AuthController.issueToken` catches a credential rejection inside the handler and returns 401 with an empty body, rather than letting the exception propagate out of the handler method. Which exceptions count as a credential rejection is DL-117's subject; this entry decides only that they are answered inside the handler. | (a) Let the exception propagate, as the literal reading prescribes. (b) Add an `@ExceptionHandler(AuthenticationException.class)` to `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` returning 401. (c) Return the 401 with a `dto/ErrorResponse` envelope such as `{"error": "Unauthorized"}`. (d) Clear the security context and re-enter the filter chain so `HttpStatusEntryPoint` handles it. | Propagation was measured, not assumed: because `POST /auth/token` is the chain's one `permitAll` matcher, the request reaches the `DispatcherServlet`, which resolves the exception through `ExceptionHandlerExceptionResolver` into `GlobalExceptionHandler`'s `@ExceptionHandler(Exception.class)` before `ExceptionTranslationFilter` can convert it — so a wrong password, an unknown principal, an empty JSON object and an absent body each returned **500 `{"error":"Internal server error"}`**, reporting a client credential error as a server fault. (a) ships the observed 500. (b) is asserted against by `api/GlobalExceptionHandlerTest.declaresNoHandlerForAnAuthenticationOrAccessDeniedFailure`, and would change the status of every route rather than this one. (c) would invent a twelfth wire literal. (d) would require unwinding into the filter chain from inside a handler. | The 401 carries no diagnostic body, so a client cannot tell an unknown principal from a wrong password or an absent body — deliberate, and identical to the 401 of every other route. The catch names three types only — `BadCredentialsException`, `UsernameNotFoundException` and `AccountStatusException` — so an infrastructure fault such as `AuthenticationServiceException` propagates and is answered 500 `Internal server error` rather than 401; that narrowing is DL-117's subject, and it is what keeps a misconfigured credential store from presenting as a rejected credential. The three that are caught are indistinguishable to the client by design. Because only the exception's simple class name is logged and never the submitted values, that case has to be diagnosed from the WARN record rather than from the response. |
| DL-079 | The `sub` claim of a token minted by `api/AuthController.issueToken` is `Authentication.getName()` of the authentication the `AuthenticationManager` returned, not `dto/LoginRequest.username()` as submitted. | (a) `request.username()`, the raw submitted string. (b) The `UserDetails` principal cast out of `Authentication.getPrincipal()` and read through `getUsername()`. (c) A claim value derived from `scanner.auth.username` read straight from configuration. | The returned authentication is the credential store's own answer, so its name is the resolved principal and is non-null on every authentication the manager completes — which matters because `security/JwtService.generateToken` rejects a null subject with `NullPointerException`. Against the current `InMemoryUserDetailsManager` the two values coincide, so this is a robustness choice and not an observable change: the minted subject is `admin` either way. (a) would carry a caller-controlled string into a signed claim and would put a `null` subject one absent JSON member away from an unhandled `NullPointerException`. (b) reaches the same value through a cast that couples this class to the store's principal type. (c) would ignore the authentication entirely and mint the same subject for any credential the manager happened to accept. | If the credential store is ever replaced by one that normalises names — lower-casing, or trimming — the `sub` claim will carry the normalised form rather than the submitted form, so a client that compares the claim against what it typed can see a difference. `security/JwtAuthenticationFilter` resolves the claim back through the same store, so the round trip stays consistent. |
| DL-080 | The wire records enforce the required-versus-optional split their source schemas declare. `dto/TweetDto` rejects a `null` `id`, `content`, `likeCount`, `createdAt`, `doubtRating` and `userId`, leaving `quotedTweetId` the sole nullable component; `dto/ResponseDto` rejects a `null` value for all five of its components. Each rejection is raised by the record's canonical constructor as a `NullPointerException` whose message names the wire key, not the component — `like_count must not be null.`, `created_at must not be null.` — so the diagnostic reads in the vocabulary of the contract. `likeCount` and `doubtRating` stay boxed so the rejection is expressed uniformly. The two list components of `TweetDto` are normalised: a `null` list becomes an empty unmodifiable list and a `null` element is dropped. The requirement is declared in exactly one place: `service/mapper/TweetMapper` and `service/mapper/ResponseMapper` carry every column value through unexamined — the withdrawn `required(value, column)` guard that duplicated the check and raised `IllegalStateException` is gone, and neither mapper dereferences an absent `tweet` association — so a row that leaves a required column empty is named by the record's constructor rather than by a second, differently-typed guard one layer earlier. The entity columns stay nullable. The two consumers that could otherwise present an incomplete record are aligned to the contract rather than left to trip over it: `service/NotionService.toTweetDto` skips a mirrored page carrying no value for a required component (DL-219), and the mirror call sites `task/TweetStreamListener.mirrorGeneratedResponse` and `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler.generateAndMirror` no longer test the generated reply's content for `null`, because a `ResponseDto` that reached them cannot carry one. This row owns the null policy of both records; the withdrawn form of DL-139 is a pointer to it. | (a) Accept `null` for every scalar and carry it to the wire as JSON `null` — the contract this entry authorised in an earlier form and the one DL-139 carried. (b) Substitute a default for a null column — `0`, `0.0`, the empty string, the epoch. (c) Add `nullable = false` to the columns. (d) Serialise a null list as JSON `null`. (e) Declare the components non-null but keep the two mirror call sites' `null`-content branches, so the same condition is tested in two places. (f) Express the required-ness with `@NotNull` on the record components instead of in the constructor. | The record-mapping rule maps a required Pydantic field to a non-null contract and `Optional[...]` to a nullable component. `backend/app/schema/tweet.py:L6-14` declares eight fields required and `quoted_tweet_id` the sole `Optional[str]`; `backend/app/schema/response.py:L5-9` declares all five required. (a) inverted that map and let a source-required field serialise as `null`. Rejecting instead reproduces the source's own behaviour exactly: `TweetSchema(**tweet_data)` and `TweetSchema(...)` at `backend/app/services/twitter_service.py:L37` and `backend/app/services/notion_service.py:L44-50` raise Pydantic `ValidationError` for a null required field, which Flask answers with 500 `{"error":"Internal server error"}` — the same status and body the catch-all advice produces here. (b) reports a measurement that was never taken, the same fault as substituting zero for a null aggregate (DL-075). (c) is forbidden: the frozen four-table, twenty-column schema prohibit adding a `NOT NULL` constraint, which is why the required-ness lives at the wire boundary and not in the schema. (d) contradicts DL-024. (e) leaves dead code that reads as though the condition were reachable, and a reader cannot tell from the call site which of the two guards is the contract; the constructor is the one that cannot be bypassed, so it is the one kept. (f) is forbidden by validation parity and DL-050, which hold Bean Validation to what Pydantic declared — two `@NotNull` annotations on request bodies and nothing else — so the enforcement has to be constructor logic rather than a new annotation. | A `tweets` or `responses` row that carries `null` in a required column has no wire form, so `GET /tweets`, `GET /tweets/{id}`, `GET /responses` and `GET /responses/{id}` answer 500 for it rather than returning a sparse body. Only ingestion and generation write these rows and both populate every required column, so the case arises from data written outside this service. The failure names the column in the log, so the gap is diagnosable rather than silent. Removing the two mirror branches means a defect that did produce a contentless reply now surfaces as a `NullPointerException` at construction inside the generating service rather than as a skipped mirror, which is louder but is raised before anything is persisted or mirrored. Three tests that encoded the permissive contract were rewritten to assert the rejection instead — two `api/TweetControllerTest` cases now address a row carrying a doubt rating, and one case in each of `task/TweetStreamListenerTest` and `task/ResponseGenerationSchedulerTest` now asserts the rejection and that the mirror is never called — so the suite states the delivered contract rather than the withdrawn one. The frontend Zod contracts type `likeCount` and `doubtRating` as non-nullable numbers, so this contract is closer to what that client expects than (a) was. |
| DL-081 | `service/LlmService.generateResponse(TweetDto)` returns the generated text as a `String`. A `ResponseDto` is built only from a stored `responses` row, by `service/mapper/ResponseMapper`, after the insert has assigned an identifier. | (a) Return a `ResponseDto` assembled before persistence, with a `null` `id`, `is_approved` `false` and `generated_at` minted in the adapter. (b) Let `LlmService` persist the row itself and return the mapped record. (c) Introduce an intermediate record carrying the text plus generation metadata. | `dto/ResponseDto` requires a non-null `id` (DL-080) and an identifier exists only once the database has assigned one, so (a), which was delivered first, can be satisfied only by weakening the wire contract — which is the finding that produced DL-080. An integration adapter's contract is what the vendor produced; the component that owns the transaction owns the row and therefore the record built from it. (b) would put a repository inside an adapter whose responsibility boundary the adapter rule limits to the external system, and would give `LlmService` a transaction. (c) adds a class the frozen file inventory does not contain, for a value one `String` already carries. | The returned text carries no metadata, so the caller must supply `is_approved` and `generated_at`; `service/ResponseService.store` does, at one site. A caller wanting the raw vendor payload rather than the trimmed text has no accessor for it. |
| DL-082 | `dto/UpdateResponseRequest` holds its two components as `com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.JsonNode` and exposes `writesContent()`, `contentValue()`, `writesApproval()`, `approvalValue()` and `carriesNoUpdatableMember()`. Presence of a key decides whether its column is written; the carried value decides what is stored. `responses.content` is written only from a JSON string, `responses.is_approved` only from a JSON boolean, and either key may instead carry an explicit JSON `null`, which is a write of `null` (DL-244); a carried key holding any other type is refused while the body is bound (DL-231). An omitted key is not writable and never writes `null`. This entry owns the third outcome: when neither component is carried — no body at all, `{}`, or a body whose only members are keys this route does not write, such as `{"isApproved":true}` or `{"id":"9"}` — `service/ResponseService.updateResponse` raises the transcribed 400 literal `Update data is required` of `backend/app/api/responses.py:L57` before it reads or mutates a row. DL-082, DL-231 and DL-244 are the whole contract for this route's body and nothing else constrains it: the record shape and the no-writable-member outcome here, the type gate in DL-231, the explicit-`null` semantics in DL-244. | (a) Coerce every carried value to its column's type — `asText()` for `content`, `asBoolean()` for `is_approved` — which is the form this tree carried before, so `{"content":123}` stored `"123"`. (b) Use `String content` and `Boolean isApproved` components, where an absent key and an explicit JSON `null` are indistinguishable. (c) Use `Optional<String>` and `Optional<Boolean>` components with `jackson-datatype-jdk8`. (d) Accept a `Map<String, Object>` body. (e) Widen the `Update data is required` literal to cover a body whose carried member cannot be written. (f) Move the route to `PATCH`. | Keeping `JsonNode` preserves the three-way distinction the partial-update contract needs — omitted, explicit `null`, wrong type — without adding a DTO or a Jackson module, and the four accessors state exactly which values the two target columns can hold. `Update data is required` is the route's own transcribed invalid-body outcome, produced by the `if not update_data` guard at `backend/app/api/responses.py:L56`, so applying it before any persistence access keeps the response envelope closed and keeps the three literals this route may answer closed at three. (a) is the defect DL-231 corrects and is recorded there. (b) collapses `{}` and `{"content":null}` into the same request, which removes the outcome this entry owns. (c) was tested and does not work: Jackson delivers both an absent key and an explicit `null` as `Optional.empty()`. (d) discards the two known target types, so nothing in the DTO states what each column can hold. (e) would make a literal transcribed from a presence guard cover a type condition that guard never described. (f) changes the HTTP method endpoint parity freezes. | The DTO stays coupled to Jackson's tree model, so a future change of body binding touches this record. A body carrying one writable and one omitted member commits the writable one, so a client must read the returned row to see which column changed. Empty text and `false` are writable values: `{"content":""}` stores an empty string rather than being treated as absent. `carriesNoUpdatableMember()` is presence-only, so `{"isApproved":true}` is reported as carrying no writable value rather than as naming an unknown member — the source route behaved the same way, and this is parity rather than an improvement. Both writable columns stay nullable because this migration adds no constraint, so a row written outside this service can still carry a `null` that `dto/ResponseDto` cannot render (DL-244). |
| DL-083 | `service/LlmService.firstChoiceContent` accepts non-blank content from the first choice whatever finish reason accompanies it, and reports **three** unusable outcomes as `IllegalStateException` with a fixed message code: `NO_CHOICE`, `REFUSAL` and `BLANK_TEXT`. A finish reason other than `stop` that accompanies accepted content is not a failure: it is recorded once at `WARN` under the prefix `INCOMPLETE:` followed by the provider's own reason token, by `reportIncompleteFinish`. The adapter logs the fixed code and a fixed description of the observed state, and never the refusal or the reply text. `service/ResponseService` catches that unchecked failure and converts it to `exception/ResponseGenerationException`, whose fixed message is the wire literal of `backend/app/api/responses.py:L49`; nothing is persisted first. An earlier revision of this row named `INCOMPLETE:<finish_reason>` as a fourth raised code; it never was one in the delivered tree. | (a) Return empty or blank text and let the caller store it. (b) Raise `ResponseGenerationException` directly from the OpenAI adapter. (c) Return `null` or `Optional<String>` and leave the decision to each caller. (d) Retry the provider call. (e) Include refusal or generated text in the failure. (f) Raise on a non-`stop` finish reason as a fourth code, which this row previously described. | The source's `choices[0].text.strip()` already fails when no choice or text exists, and the route's 500 branch covers generation that produced no usable reply. A fixed adapter-local code keeps provider state diagnosable without giving the integration adapter ownership of a wire exception or exposing model output. `ResponseService` is the layer that owns the route contract and therefore performs the one conversion to `ResponseGenerationException`. (a) puts an empty draft into the human-review queue. (b) couples the adapter to the API's error envelope. (c) makes every caller repeat the same required check. (d) adds a retry policy excluded by scope. (e) violates the logging policy for provider-controlled content. (f) is what this row asserted and the code never did, and it is the wrong contract as well as a false statement: 150 completion tokens truncate a long reply by design of DL-202, so `finish_reason` `length` beside usable text is the expected outcome for a verbose model and discarding that text would turn a working generation into a 500. | A model that returns no text, refuses, or returns only whitespace makes the route answer 500 rather than 201; a model that returns usable text and stops for any other reason still answers 201, with the reason in a `WARN`. The three codes and the `INCOMPLETE:` prefix are an internal contract asserted by `LlmServiceTest`; changing one affects diagnostics but not the wire literal. A caller other than `ResponseService` would receive `IllegalStateException` and must choose its own boundary conversion. This row and DL-178 both describe the check order, so a change to it has to be applied in both places or one will go stale — as the fourth-code claim did. |
| DL-084 | Each failure path has exactly one component that writes its error record. That record carries the business operation, the exception class name, and — for a failed HTTP call — the provider's status value together with the provider's own machine-readable error identifiers: for OpenAI the `type`, `code` and `param` members `OpenAIServiceException` exposes, and for Notion the `code` member of the error body, the `x-request-id` header and the number of characters the provider explanation carries, each under the shape checks DL-153 states. The Notion `message` member itself is not logged: an earlier revision of this row admitted it cut to 200 characters, and DL-153 withdrew that admission while this row was left stating it — the explanation reaches the record as a length under DL-269 and never as text. The response body itself is never logged, nor is any prompt, credential, page URL or request payload. `service/ResponseService` owns the generation path, `service/NotionService.logFailure` owns all three mirror paths, and `service/LlmService` writes one record naming the provider rejection at the adapter boundary before propagating it. | (a) Log the exception with its message and stack trace at both the adapter and its caller. (b) Log at the adapter only, carrying the status value and nothing further, which is what this entry authorised before it was rewritten. (c) Log nothing and let the handler's 500 record be the only trace. (d) Keep the whole provider message but redact it by pattern. (e) Log the full provider response body at `DEBUG` only. | A vendor exception message is not ours to store: the OpenAI request carries the tweet text as prompt content, the Notion request carries a database identifier and a page URL, and an HTTP client's message quotes the request and often the response body — so (a) writes third-party payloads and our own request content into a plain-text log, beyond the DL-052 baseline, which excludes an audit facility precisely because logs are not a record store. Duplication was also real: one failure produced two `ERROR` records from two layers. The caller knows the business operation and the adapter knows the transport, so the reporting layer names both. (b) was measured to be unactionable, which is why it was withdrawn: both providers answer a rejected request with a status that is identical across unrelated causes — every malformed Notion page write is `400 validation_error` and every rejected OpenAI parameter is `400 invalid_request_error` — so an operator reading `failed: BadRequest after HTTP 400` learns only that something in the request was wrong, and the one field that names which thing was discarded. Both providers publish that field as a short, enumerated, machine-readable token that is not derived from request content, which is exactly the part worth keeping and the part (d) would have to guess at. Notion's `message` was the one free-text member ever admitted, on the ground that it names the offending property; DL-153 withdrew it, because a length cut bounds a free-text member's size and not its content. What remains of it in the record is its character count (DL-269), which distinguishes an empty explanation from a long one without carrying any of it. (c) removes the signal entirely. (e) puts the body one log level away, where a routine `DEBUG` build would capture it. | The permitted detail set is now defined per provider, so adding a fifth external system means deciding again which of its error fields are content-free — the rule is not mechanical. A provider that ever moves request content into its `code` or `param` field would leak it; both are documented as enumerated tokens, and nothing in this repository re-verifies that. Notion's explanation reaches the record as a count alone, so an operator has no in-service source for the offending property name and must reproduce the call outside this service — DL-152 records that the start-up check which was once claimed to supply it was never delivered and is withdrawn; a provider that returned a body larger than the client's buffer yields no count at all rather than a partial one. |
| DL-085 | `service/LlmService` closes the cached `OpenAIClient` in an `@PreDestroy` method, clears the field under the same monitor that creates it, and refuses to create a replacement afterwards — a call after destruction raises `IllegalStateException`. The method marks the bean destroyed before it waits and awaits a completion already in flight, which DL-266 owns. A failure to close is logged at `WARN` by exception class and is not propagated. | (a) Leave the client to garbage collection. (b) Declare the client as a `@Bean` and let the container's destroy callback close it. (c) Create a client per call and close it in a try-with-resources. (d) Close it without a destroyed flag. | `com.openai:openai-java` 4.49.0 holds an OkHttp connection pool and a dispatcher executor, and those threads keep the JVM alive until they are shut down, so under (a), which was delivered first, a stopped context leaks a pool and delays shutdown. (b) is the idiomatic form, but the client is created lazily on first use precisely so a missing API key does not fail startup — the key is optional in every profile — and a `@Bean` is created eagerly. (c) discards connection reuse on every request. (d) leaves a race: a request already inside the service can create a replacement after `@PreDestroy` has closed the first, and the replacement is then never closed. | A request in flight during shutdown fails with `IllegalStateException` rather than completing; the container is stopping, so the alternative is a request completing against a closed client. The destroyed flag makes the bean single-use, so a container that destroyed and then reused the same instance would find it unusable — Spring does not. |
| DL-086 | No transaction is open while the OpenAI call runs. `service/ResponseService.generateResponse` reads and maps the subject in one repository-demarcated unit, calls `LlmService` with no transaction open, then persists inside an explicit `TransactionTemplate` unit that re-reads the parent row before inserting. The template is injected rather than obtained by annotating a private method. | (a) One `@Transactional` method spanning the whole flow. (b) `@Transactional(propagation = NOT_SUPPORTED)` around the external call. (c) Annotate the private persistence helper `@Transactional`. (d) Move generation to an asynchronous worker so the request never holds a connection. | A chat completion takes seconds, and under (a), which was delivered first, a pooled JDBC connection is held for all of it — the HikariCP default pool is ten, so ten concurrent generations exhaust it and every other route waits on a database connection for a reason that has nothing to do with the database. (b) still needs an outer transaction to suspend and leaves the connection bound to the thread. (c) is inert: Spring's transaction advice is a proxy, so a self-invocation never passes through it and the annotation silently does nothing — which is why the template is injected. (d) needs a broker or a queue, which 0.2.3 excludes. | The flow is no longer atomic: the parent row can be deleted between the read and the insert, which is why the persistence unit re-reads it and reports the generation-failure literal instead of inserting an orphan. When that happens the generated text is lost and the paid call is not refunded. Reading and writing in separate units also means the mapped subject is a detached entity — only basic and converted attributes are read from it, never the `responses` collection. |
| DL-087 | **Superseded by DL-245 and DL-249.** `repository/ResponseRepository` no longer overrides `findAll(Pageable)` and declares no entity graph. A response page is now read as the closed `ResponseRow` projection of DL-245 — the five response columns plus `tweet.id`, resolved from the owning foreign-key column without joining the parent row — through `findAllRows(Pageable)` with an explicit count query, and in bounded chunks through `findRowChunk(Pageable)` per DL-249. No entity is materialised for a listing, so there is neither a secondary select per parent nor a fetch of parent columns the wire contract never carries. This identifier is retained so a citation from any commit resolves; `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest` and `service/ResponseServiceTest` remain the covering classes. | (a) Delete this row and renumber. (b) Leave the row describing the entity-graph override and let it contradict the code. (c) Rewrite it as the new decision under this identifier rather than pointing at successors. | The entity-graph override this row authorised still loaded every response entity and every parent tweet column to serve a page whose wire shape needs five response fields and one parent identifier, which a review then raised as a finding: the fix was to stop materialising entities for a listing rather than to tune how their parents are fetched. Two successors were opened rather than one because the projection and the chunked page read are separate subjects with separate alternatives, and this log's ownership rule allows one subject per entry. (a) breaks every citation in an earlier commit. (b) is the failure Rule 1 exists to prevent. (c) would give one identifier two subjects. | A reader who arrives here from an older comment must follow two pointers rather than one. The projection is a closed interface, so adding a field to the wire contract now requires editing both the projection and its JPQL rather than only a mapper — the cost DL-245 accepts. |
| DL-088 | `service/NotionService` writes exactly seven Notion properties and no others: `Content`, `Author`, `Timestamp`, `Doubt Rating`, `Engagement` and `Tweet Id` on page creation, and `Response` on the reply update. `TweetDto.media`, `TweetDto.aiToolsMentioned` and `TweetDto.quotedTweetId` are not mirrored — no property is written for them, and a read leaves the two list components empty and the quoted identifier `null`. A component the post does not carry has its property omitted rather than written as null. | (a) Mirror nine properties, adding `Media`, `Quoted Tweet Id` and `AI Tools Mentioned`, each list written as a single comma-delimited rich-text value — the form this entry authorised before it was rewritten. (b) Write each list as a Notion multi-select property. (c) Write one rich-text property per list element. (d) Write an explicitly null property for an absent component. (e) Keep writing the three extra properties but tolerate the resulting rejection by retrying without them. | The agreed mirror property set is these seven, and media, aiToolsMentioned and quotedTweetId are not mirrored (the agreed mirror set and the requirement table), so the nine-property form was a specification breach rather than a richer copy. It was also inoperative, which is what makes this a correction and not a preference: Notion answers `POST /v1/pages` with HTTP 400 `validation_error` — "… is not a property that exists" — for any property the target database does not define, and the rejection covers the whole request, so against a database built to the seven-property contract every single mirror write failed and the secondary mirror was not over-populated but absent. The completeness argument the withdrawn form rested on — that a copy omitting three of nine columns lets a reader mistake an absent value for an unmirrored one — does not survive that measurement, and it is answered instead by this entry and the class Javadoc, which state the mirrored set explicitly. The relational store remains the system of record, so the unmirrored components are never unavailable, only absent from the secondary copy. (b) requires the target database to declare a multi-select schema with pre-registered options, which this adapter neither manages nor can create. (c) invents property names that depend on the element count. (d) writes a shape the Notion API rejects for several property types and would make an absent value indistinguishable from an empty one. (e) is a retry policy, which this migration places out of scope, and it would double every write. | The seven property names are part of an implicit contract with a Notion database this code does not create; a database missing any of them still rejects the write. A reader of the Notion database sees no media, no quoted-post identifier and no AI tool names, and cannot tell from Notion alone that those columns exist in the system of record — the mirror is deliberately narrower than the row. `util/DelimitedStringListConverter` is no longer reached from this class, so the delimited codec is exercised only by the persistence layer. A future decision to widen the mirror has to move the stated requirement, this entry, the class Javadoc and `service/NotionServiceTest` together. |
| DL-089 | A 2xx Notion response whose body is empty, or which lacks the structure the call requires, is reported as a failure. `requireResults` raises for an empty body and for an absent or non-array `results`; page creation raises for an empty body and for a created page carrying no identifier. An empty `results` array is not a failure — it is a genuine absence of a match. | (a) Normalise every unexpected shape to a benign value — the empty string, no match, an empty list. (b) Retry the call. (c) Return an `Optional` or a result object from each call so every call site distinguishes the cases itself. | An answer of 200 whose body is not what the API documents is an integration fault, and under (a), which was delivered first, it is indistinguishable from a correct negative answer: `findPageIdByTweetId` reported no match for a broken response, so `updateTweetResponse` then wrote nothing and reported success — a mirror that silently stopped mirroring, which is worse than one that fails loudly. Separating the two is precisely what makes it safe to keep reporting the empty-array case as no match. (b) retries a well-formed exchange that will return the same body. (c) spreads the decision across every call site of an adapter whose purpose is to hide the transport. | A Notion API change that wraps `results` in a new envelope turns a working mirror into a failing one at the first call rather than degrading quietly — intended, and contained: the mirror is secondary (0.6.5.12), so the relational write has already succeeded before the mirror is attempted. |
| DL-090 | Reading a Notion page substitutes exactly one value and no others, and reads a title or rich-text property by **concatenating every item in order**. Each item's `text.content` member is read first and its `plain_text` member second, every item contributes, and the property yields `null` only when the whole array carries no text — so a value the write side carried as consecutive items is reassembled exactly. `DL-292` owns the matching split and the two are a pair: the read side is the inverse of `textItems(String)`. | (a) Return the first non-empty item and ignore the rest, which an earlier revision of this entry recorded as delivered. (b) Concatenate with a separator between items. (c) Return the items as a list and let the caller join them. (d) Read only `plain_text`, which the provider populates for every item. | (a) is withdrawn because it is not the inverse of the delivered write side: once a value longer than the provider's per-item ceiling is carried as several items, reading the first returns a 2,000-character prefix of the stored value and silently discards the rest — a data-loss path through a read that reports success. (b) inserts characters the value never had, so the round trip is not exact. (c) changes the DTO shape, which the wire contract freezes. (d) is nearly equivalent and was not taken because `text.content` is the member this service writes and `plain_text` is the provider's rendering of it, so preferring the written member keeps the read closest to the write. | Concatenation assumes the items are in the order the write side produced them, which is the order the provider returns them in; a provider that reordered items would return the value scrambled rather than truncated. The round trip is asserted at eight lengths from 1 to 12,345 characters against text whose every 2,000-character window differs, so a reordered or dropped window is detected rather than merely a shortened result. |
| DL-091 | **Superseded by DL-180.** This entry and DL-180 recorded the same subject — the isolation level `service/AnalyticsService.getSummary()` declares and the one-snapshot guarantee it buys. Both also miscounted the statements: the method issues **three** aggregate statements, not six — `TweetRepository.findAggregates()`, `ResponseRepository.findApprovalCounts()` and `AiToolRepository.count()`, one per table — and `pending_responses` is arithmetic over two of them. DL-180 owns the subject and states the delivered count. | (a) Leave both rows standing on one subject, as delivered. (b) Delete this row. (c) Make DL-180 the pointer and keep this row as the owner. (d) Correct the statement count here as well as in DL-180, leaving two current rows. | The log's ownership rule is that one subject is owned by exactly one entry, and its revision rule (DL-099) is that a superseded entry is rewritten in place as a pointer rather than deleted. DL-180 is the fuller statement — it also records that `getTrends()` keeps the default isolation — so it is the entry kept. (b) and (c) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier. (d) is the defect being closed: two rows on one subject let a later correction be applied to one and missed on the other, which is exactly how the six-statement figure survived two reviews here while DL-158 already recorded the correct figure of three. | A reader arriving from an older commit takes one extra hop to DL-180. The two citations in `service/AnalyticsService` that named this identifier alongside DL-180 now name DL-180 alone, so no artifact in the delivered tree cites this row. DL-165 previously listed this identifier among its successors and now names DL-041 and DL-180 only. |
| DL-092 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded an explicit `@ExceptionHandler` for each framework-reported client failure on `api/GlobalExceptionHandler`, so the catch-all answered server failures only — among them 400 `Bad request` for an unreadable body, and the companion literals for the other client statuses. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` declares handlers for the application's own exceptions and one catch-all, and writes exactly the source's two envelopes and the per-route literals the source declared. The catch-all resolves a framework failure by rule rather than by a handler per type: an exception implementing `org.springframework.web.ErrorResponse` whose status is not 5xx is answered with that status and **no body**, carrying the exception's own headers so a 405 keeps `Allow` and a 415 or 406 keeps `Accept`; an `HttpMessageConversionException` that is not an `HttpMessageNotWritableException` is answered 400 with no body; everything else is 500 with `{"error": "Internal server error"}`. | (a) Keep a handler per framework exception type, with a literal for each. (b) Keep the handlers but reuse the 500 literal for all of them. (c) Resolve by rule and write no body for a framework-reported client failure, which is what is delivered. (d) Answer every framework failure 500 with the 500 literal, which would turn a 405 into a server error. | The wire-contract parity clause freezes the status codes and the error envelopes to what the retired Flask application served: only `@app.errorhandler(404)` and `(500)` produced JSON, so a framework-reported client failure came from Werkzeug's own page. 'The framework's own status with no envelope of ours' is therefore the parity behaviour, and every additional status literal is an invention. The rule replaces fourteen handler methods across eighteen exception types with three branches, and it was derived by probing the real class path rather than from documentation: each candidate exception was asked whether it implements `ErrorResponse` and what status it reports, which is what established that `MissingServletRequestParameterException`, `HttpRequestMethodNotSupportedException`, `HttpMediaTypeNotSupportedException`, `HttpMediaTypeNotAcceptableException`, `MaxUploadSizeExceededException`, `NoHandlerFoundException`, `NoResourceFoundException` and `MethodArgumentNotValidException` all do, while `HttpMessageNotReadableException`, `TypeMismatchException`, `MultipartException`, `IllegalArgumentException` and `AccessDeniedException` do not. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | A client that sends a wrong method, an unsupported content type or an unreadable body receives a bare status with the RFC-mandated headers and no JSON body, so a caller that expects every response to carry a JSON envelope must tolerate an empty one. `POST /settings` was exercised against the running service and answers 405 with `Allow: GET` and an empty body. The source imposed the same split. |
| DL-093 | **Superseded by DL-111.** This entry stated that a failed token verification is logged at `WARN` as the simple class name of the exception jjwt raised, one record per rejected token. The delivered `security/JwtService` writes neither: the record is at `DEBUG` and carries a fixed sentence with no exception type in it. DL-111 owns the whole token-rejection and authentication-filter logging contract and states what the tree does. | (a) Leave this entry describing a `WARN` record the code does not write. (b) Delete the row. (c) Change the code back to match this entry. | (c) is the change DL-111 examined and rejected: an unauthenticated caller chooses how many failures arrive, so a `WARN` record enabled by default hands that caller log volume, and a jjwt message quotes the presented token. The entry is therefore the stale side and is rewritten. (b) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier; DL-099 directs a rewrite in place. | The reader needs DL-111 for the delivered contract; this row carries none of it. No artifact in the delivered tree cites this identifier. |
| DL-094 | The record written when a request authenticates is a fixed sentence naming neither the principal, the request method nor the request URI: `security/JwtAuthenticationFilter` writes `Authenticated a request through a bearer token` and nothing else, and sets the principal on the security context without ever writing its name. An earlier form of this entry stated that the record carries the request method and URI; it never did in the delivered filter, and the sentence is fixed text with no interpolated argument at all. DL-111 owns the full set of sentences this filter and `security/JwtService` write; this entry owns the narrower decision that the principal name is not among them. | (a) Log the token subject. (b) Log a non-reversible identifier such as a salted hash of it. (c) Log nothing on success. (d) Log the subject at `TRACE`. (e) Add the request method and URI to the sentence, which this entry previously described as delivered. | A principal name in a log is an access record, and the scope line is drawn at a logging baseline with no audit facility (DL-052) — an access record is the first half of one, and it accumulates on stdout in plain text with no retention policy to govern it. With a single configured principal (DL-020) the name is also constant, so (a), which was delivered first, distinguished nothing while creating exactly that record. (b) still stores a per-principal identifier and adds a salt to manage, for no diagnostic gain. (c) removes the signal that the filter ran and authenticated. (d) leaves the value in the code, one configuration change away from being emitted. (e) is what this entry claimed was delivered, and it is excluded on the same ground as (a) at one remove: a method and URI pair per authenticated request is a request log, which the baseline does not undertake to keep, and the container already records the request line. Correcting the claim rather than adding the fields keeps the record a constant sentence, which is also what makes it free of any value a caller supplies. | A successful request cannot be attributed to a principal, a method or a path from the logs alone; the sentence confirms only that the filter authenticated something. Acceptable while the credential store holds one principal; if DL-020's extension path to a persistent user store is taken, this is the decision to revisit. Because the sentence is fixed text, a reader who expects the fields this entry once described will find none — which is why the superseded claim is stated here rather than silently dropped. |
| DL-095 | An in-scope test that asserted defective behaviour is rewritten to assert the corrected contract rather than preserved. Four were: the `length() == 255` assertion in `JpaMappingIntegrationTest`, the six-named-properties and six-field round-trip assertions in `NotionServiceTest`, the pre-persistence `ResponseDto` and empty-text expectations in `LlmServiceTest`, and the exactly-six-handlers, seven-handled-types and only-400-404-500 counts in `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest`. | (a) Keep the assertions and shape each fix so they still pass. (b) Delete the assertions without replacement. (c) Mark them `@Disabled` with a comment. | A test encoding an outdated or incorrect value is updated to match the agreed contract, never the reverse, and each of these four assertions pinned exactly the behaviour a finding required changed — the handler-count assertion in particular made adding the 405, 415 and 406 handlers impossible, so (a) would have preserved a defect to keep a test green. (b) would leave the corrected behaviour unguarded, so each assertion was replaced by one over the new contract: physical column type and capacity read from JDBC metadata, one property per mirrored component plus whole-record equality, a failure for absent or blank model output, and a containment assertion over the handled types with the emitted status set widened to six. (c) leaves dead code and no guard. | The count-based assertions did catch an accidentally added handler or property, and the containment assertions that replace them tolerate an unintended handler as long as its status is one of the six. Test-only, and the exchange is what the findings require. |
| DL-096 | A test class may exist only if the frozen test inventory names it or an entry in this log authorises it by name for a subject that inventory never contemplated. That is the rule; the delivered enumeration under it is owned by DL-216, so the two are never stated twice. DL-183 now serves the envelope from an `ErrorAttributes` bean inside the planned `api/GlobalExceptionHandler`, so that controller and its test are deleted and this row no longer authorises either; Contracts whose owning class the inventory does not reach — the presence semantics of `dto/UpdateResponseRequest`, the log content of `security/JwtAuthenticationFilter` — are covered by the classes that do exist. | (a) Create a test class for every contract, regardless of the inventory. (b) Fold an authorised addition's assertions into an unrelated existing class. (c) Leave a new subject unasserted. (d) Assert a new subject only through the classes the inventory already names. (e) Keep the enumeration in this row as well as in DL-216. (f) Keep authorising `api/ErrorDispatchControllerTest` so the identifier's history stays intact. | The frozen inventory enumerates the constructs it planned, and a construct delivered after it — a container-dispatch path, a context-lifecycle listener — has no entry there at all, so the inventory cannot be read as forbidding a companion class for a subject it never contemplated; each such class is admitted by name here and by its own decision. (a) would repeat the root cause of two of the review's findings, a change outside the authorised file set. (b) would put an assertion about one component inside another component's test class, where nobody looking for it would find it. (c) would leave a servlet-container dispatch and a lifecycle listener — neither reachable from a slice test — with no assertion at all. (e) is the drift this rewrite removes: two enumerations of one set is how this row came to authorise a file the tree no longer has. (f) confuses history with authority — the withdrawal is stated in this row and in DL-183, so the history is legible, but a rule that authorises a deleted file is a rule a reviewer cannot check. | The authorised set now lives in one place, so an addition has to be recorded in DL-216 and admitted by its own entry; an unrecorded addition is exactly the drift these two rows exist to prevent. Because this row states a rule rather than a count, it no longer goes stale when a class is added or withdrawn — which the earlier form did twice, first by fixing the additions at "exactly two" and then by asserting that six planned classes were still undelivered after DL-216 recorded all of them as delivered. The class this entry once named as its covering test is not in the delivered tree: the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary declares nineteen test classes and none of the guard classes that assertion relied on, so they were deleted under DL-216 and this claim is held by review against the delivered artifacts rather than by an assertion in the build. |
| DL-097 | `service/SettingsService.seedDefaultSettings` is not itself transactional. Each key is seeded inside its own `TransactionTemplate` unit configured `PROPAGATION_REQUIRES_NEW` and `ISOLATION_REPEATABLE_READ`, and the insert is flushed inside that unit. | (a) One `@Transactional` method enclosing all three keys. (b) A native `INSERT` with a vendor conflict clause. (c) Make `Setting` implement `Persistable` so Spring Data issues `persist` rather than `merge`. (d) Keep one transaction and catch the conflict inside it. | `SimpleJpaRepository.save` resolves to `EntityManager.merge` for an entity with an assigned `String` identifier and no `@Version`, and `merge` issues its own select — under read-committed that select can see a row another replica committed after the presence test and turn the insert into an update that overwrites an operator's value. That matters here specifically because these rows exist to be edited through `PUT /settings/{key}` (DL-040). Repeatable read puts the merge select on the presence test's snapshot, so `merge` always schedules an insert and every conflict is a duplicate key rather than a silent overwrite. Per-key units also mean a conflict on one key cannot mark the transaction rollback-only and take the other two with it, which (a), delivered first, and (d) both allow. (b) is not vendor-neutral and would hand-write SQL beside reserved column names each vendor quotes differently. (c) changes an entity the review passed and affects every other write to it. | Three transactions at startup instead of one. On PostgreSQL a repeatable-read write transaction can raise a serialization failure; it is caught as a concurrency conflict and reconciled by DL-098. Measured honestly: with read-committed and twelve concurrent replicas, PostgreSQL produced no overwriting update either, because the primary key blocks the second inserter first — so this level buys a property that holds by construction rather than by timing, not a fix for an observed overwrite. |
| DL-098 | A duplicate key or a lock conflict raised while seeding a key is reconciled by re-reading that key: present means another instance inserted it first and the stored row is left exactly as it stands; still absent means the write failed for another reason and the exception propagates. | (a) Let every write failure propagate. (b) Swallow every write failure. (c) Retry with backoff. (d) Insert, then compare the stored row against the default and repair it. | Several replicas start at once and all three keys are seeded on `ApplicationReadyEvent`, so a duplicate key is the expected outcome for all but one replica — measured on live PostgreSQL, (a) makes eleven of twelve replicas log an error for a database that is in exactly the intended state. (b) would hide a genuine outage behind the same path, which is why the re-read rather than the exception type alone is the discriminator. (c) retries a row that is already there. (d) is the overwrite this design exists to prevent: a value an operator changed must survive every subsequent start. | One extra presence read, on the conflict path only. A row another instance inserted with a different value — a different software version's default, say — is accepted and never corrected, which is the same guarantee an operator's own edit relies on. |
| DL-099 | This log is maintained as part of any change that alters a decision, and a superseded decision is rewritten in place under its own identifier rather than appended as a second entry. Seven entries were rewritten this way after the first code review — DL-015, DL-017, DL-025, DL-068, DL-070, DL-074 and DL-075 — and DL-059, which an existing entry cited but which had never been populated, was written so that the completeness invariant holds. A second review found further entries describing an earlier state of the tree, and these were rewritten in the same way: DL-053, DL-061, DL-096, DL-139, DL-170, DL-180 and DL-181 were corrected against the delivered artifacts, and DL-077, DL-140, DL-160, DL-161, DL-163, DL-165, DL-167, DL-168, DL-172, DL-173 and DL-179 became pointers to the entry that now owns their subject. A third review found two rows describing the wire records' null policy differently and three describing constructs the tree no longer holds: DL-059, DL-080, DL-096, DL-183 and DL-216 were corrected against the delivered artifacts, DL-139 became a pointer to DL-080, and DL-219 was allocated for a rule the delivered code raised. A fourth review found four subjects carrying more than one current row and two rows asserting a schema the migration forbids: `DL-028`, `DL-072`, `DL-087`, `DL-162` and `DL-166` were corrected against the delivered artifacts, `DL-179` became a pointer to `DL-159`, `DL-125` became a pointer to `DL-180`, `DL-229` became a pointer to `DL-270` and `DL-271`, and `DL-240` through `DL-245` were allocated for decisions that remediation made. A fifth review found a further eleven rows describing an earlier tree and two more subjects held by two current rows each. `DL-083`, `DL-084`, `DL-178`, `DL-180`, `DL-202`, `DL-216`, `DL-226` and `DL-239` were corrected against the delivered artifacts — the unusable-output codes and their check order, the permitted Notion failure fields, the statement count of `getSummary()`, which branch records a finish reason, the test inventory, the two distinctly named generate-if-absent methods and the case count of `ScannerApplicationTests`; `DL-091` became a pointer to `DL-180` and `DL-124` the pointer to `DL-159` that this row had already claimed it was; and `DL-158` and `DL-165`, already pointers, were rewritten because their own redirection text had gone stale. `DL-058` records the mechanical comment guard that review's Rule 1 finding produced, and `DL-275` and `DL-276` were allocated for the stream-rule refusal reporting and the yamllint policy. A sixth review — the client and API seam checkpoint — found nine defects of which seven had a half in this file, and the corrections are the widest single pass this row records. Six entries became withdrawals, because the mechanisms they authorised were operations changes no scope admitted and the tree was restored to the line-level carve-out `DL-215` states: `DL-102`, `DL-103`, `DL-104`, `DL-105`, `DL-136` and `DL-276` now each state what they used to do, what the delivered tree carries instead, and which pre-existing defect that leaves standing. Seven more were corrected against the delivered artifacts: `DL-215` for the exact four-file operations delivery, `DL-059` for a client-seam inventory that stated twelve items while twenty-two were confirmed and cited the absent `authService` import at the wrong line, `DL-058` for a Rule 1 enforcement claim the guard did not yet support, `DL-082`, `DL-231` and `DL-244` for three mutually inconsistent accounts of one update-response body contract, and `DL-183` for an error-dispatch strategy the tree had quietly departed from a second time. `DL-135` became a pointer to `DL-074` and `DL-103`: once its action-reference pin was withdrawn, the only statement left in it was DL-074's own subject, so the operations withdrawal had produced a duplicate-ownership defect as a side effect and the matrix citation naming it was moved to the owner in the same change. `DL-279` was allocated for the build-scoped `test`-profile guard, because four citations named `DL-244` — which owns an unrelated subject — for it. Two of those corrections are worth naming as kinds rather than instances. `DL-183` had been rewritten once already, after the first controller was withdrawn, and the tree then reintroduced the same construct as a nested class inside a file the inventory does declare, so the row was true of the package listing and false of the package: a claim can be defeated by a construct moving rather than by the claim changing. And `DL-082`, `DL-231` and `DL-244` were each internally coherent while contradicting one another on the same body, which no single-row review can catch — the ownership rule stated in this file's header is what makes that findable, because exactly one of the three may own each outcome. The claim this row previously made about `DL-124` is the sharpest form of the defect it exists to record: this row asserted the rewrite while the row itself still carried the withdrawn mechanism, so the file contradicted itself. Its allocation sentence for `DL-240` through `DL-245` named six subjects that are not the six those identifiers carry, and it no longer names them. Three of those six existed in the code as citations before they existed here, which is the defect the completeness invariant catches and the reason the invariant is checked mechanically rather than by reading. | (a) Freeze the file and record remediation rationale elsewhere. (b) Append a new entry for each changed decision and leave the superseded one standing. (c) Keep both forms in one row, marked superseded and current. (d) Delete a superseded entry and renumber its successor into the gap. | Rule 1 makes this file the only place rationale may live and forbids it in code, so a decision that changes must be recorded here or nowhere — (a) makes the rule unsatisfiable for any later change and would strand the identifiers already cited from code. (b) is what produced the defect the second review found: two entries on one subject, with nothing to tell a reader which describes the tree. (c) doubles the length of a row whose earlier form is already in version history. (d) breaks every citation in version history that names the deleted identifier, which is why a superseded entry becomes a pointer instead. | A rewritten entry loses its earlier wording from the file, so reconstructing the history of a decision means reading `git log` on this file. A pointer row costs a reader one extra hop. The convention only works if it is applied every time: an entry left describing a superseded state is indistinguishable from an entry describing the tree, which is exactly the defect four reviews have now had to find by hand — twice for a row that contradicted another row, once for a row that authorised a file the tree does not hold, and once for six rows that authorised operations mechanisms no scope admitted. A row correcting itself is not enough on its own: `DL-183` declared the withdrawn controller deleted while the file was still on disk, and then, once both were true, the tree reintroduced the same construct nested inside another file while the row still read correctly against the package listing — so a claim in this log is only true while the tree matches it, the matching change belongs in the same commit, and a claim stated in terms a reader can check by listing files can be defeated by a construct that moves. Nothing in the build reads a prose claim, so the mechanical guards cover the shape of this file — population, four populated columns, resolvable citations, the pointer enumeration — and not whether a sentence is true of the tree; that remains a reading task, and six reviews have found something for it each time. |
| DL-100 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded an override of the `<netty.version>` property the Spring Boot parent exposes, raising the Netty version to 4.1.136.Final above the 4.1.135.Final the 3.5.16 BOM resolves, on the ground that the raised release carried a published security fix. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** The property is not declared. `backend/pom.xml` declares exactly one property, `<java.version>21</java.version>`, and the Netty version resolves to the BOM's 4.1.135.Final, which was verified by resolving the dependency tree after the override was removed. | (a) Keep the override. (b) Pin the Netty version on the coordinate instead of through the property, which is the same drift by another route. (c) Raise the parent version, which the frozen contract fixes at 3.5.16. (d) Take the BOM version, which is what is delivered. | AAP section 0.5.1.2 lists the versions the parent BOM resolves and states that they must not be pinned in the POM, because pinning them creates drift the moment the Spring Boot version moves. The frozen dependency contract is seventeen coordinates with exactly five explicit pins, and an override property is a sixth, seventh, eighth and ninth pin by another name. A locally raised patch release also decouples this service from the Spring Boot release train: the next parent upgrade would silently keep the local value if it were lower, or be overridden by it if it were higher, and neither is what a BOM-managed coordinate is for. The CVE posture is now inherited from the Spring Boot release line, which is the posture the frozen contract chooses. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | the Netty version is whatever the 3.5.16 BOM resolves, so an advisory against 4.1.135.Final is answered by upgrading the Spring Boot patch release rather than by a local property — which is a larger change but the only one the frozen contract admits. The applicability triage that justified the raise was a judgement made at one cutoff and is not carried forward. |

| DL-102 | **Withdrawn.** This entry set the CD `workflow_run.workflows` value to the exact CI workflow name `Code Skeptic Scanner CI`. The delivered `.github/workflows/cd.yml` carries the source-branch value `["Continuous Integration"]`, which matches no workflow name in this repository, so the deploy job stays unreachable — a pre-existing defect this migration retains. The image reference is likewise unchanged and carries **no tag at all**: `BACKEND_IMAGE` is `gcr.io/$GCP_PROJECT_ID/code-skeptic-backend`, so `docker build -t`, `docker push` and `gcloud run deploy --image` all address the registry's implicit `latest`. `backend/pom.xml` names this row for that fact where it records that no operational file reads the module version. | (a) Keep the rename this entry made, on the grounds that a workflow that cannot trigger is worse than an unauthorised edit. (b) Rename the CI workflow instead. (c) Trigger CD directly on pushes to `main`. | The agreed operations carve-out authorises exactly one change to this file, its backend build line, and this entry changed a second line. The mismatch predates this work: the source branch already carried `Code Skeptic Scanner CI` as the CI name and `Continuous Integration` as the CD trigger — both values were verified against the pristine upstream commits of the two files — so the deploy job was unreachable before the migration and is unreachable after it, unchanged. It belongs to the same family as the undeclared `var.backend_image` the plan leaves broken on purpose, and repairing it here would be the scope breach a review recorded as critical. (a) states the engineering case and is exactly the reasoning the carve-out exists to overrule; (b) and (c) are larger edits to the same protected surface. A second review has since re-raised the same defect and asked for the workflow name to be matched; The frozen operations carve-out DL-215 records states this file's delivery as "Line 36 only … Nothing else changes" and enumerates this file's remaining lines as unchanged, so the exclusion decides it again and this row is the record of that, not a new decision. | Continuous deployment does not run. Nothing in the repository reports that: both workflows are individually valid YAML, and the chain simply never fires. The unreachability is what bounds the exposure of every other retained CD defect — DL-104's long-lived service-account key and DL-105's untagged deploy are reached by no execution, and `google-github-actions/setup-gcloud@v0.2.1` declares `runs.using: node12`, a runtime GitHub runners no longer provide, so the job could not complete even if it did fire. Repairing the trigger without also repairing those would turn three dormant defects into live ones in one commit, which is the order any widening of the carve-out has to respect. The class this entry once named as its covering test is not in the delivered tree: the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary declares nineteen test classes and none of the guard classes that assertion relied on, so they were deleted under DL-216 and this claim is held by review against the delivered artifacts rather than by an assertion in the build. |
| DL-103 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a full-commit-SHA pin on the one GitHub action reference this migration authored: the JDK toolchain step inside the authorised `ci.yml` edit at `L16-19` was written as `actions/setup-java@<40-hex>` with a comment naming the release the SHA resolved to, while every other action reference was left as the source branch wrote it. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** The step reads `uses: actions/setup-java@v4` and carries no resolved-release comment. That is the bare-tag form every other action reference in the file uses — `actions/checkout@v2` and `actions/setup-node@v2` — so the file is uniform and the only change inside `L16-19` is the toolchain substitution the carve-out names. | (a) Keep the SHA pin and the resolved-release comment. (b) Keep the SHA without the comment. (c) Pin every action in the file, which would edit lines outside the carve-out. (d) Reference by tag, which is what is delivered. | AAP section 0.4.1.8 states the `ci.yml` edit as replacing `actions/setup-python@v2` with `actions/setup-java` plus Temurin 21 and Maven caching, and nothing more; a supply-chain pin is content beyond that, and the review that found it named the action content explicitly among the unapproved changes. This entry had already been recorded as withdrawn once, in the operations restoration that withdrew `DL-102`, `DL-104`, `DL-105`, `DL-136` and `DL-276` — while the SHA pin was still live in the file. So the log said withdrawn and the workflow said pinned, which is exactly the class of contradiction this review round exists to remove, and the correction is now made in both places in the same change. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | A tag reference is mutable: `actions/setup-java@v4` resolves to whatever the `v4` branch points at when the workflow runs, so a compromised or regressed release reaches the build with no recorded decision. That is a real supply-chain exposure and it is the exposure the source branch already carries on all three of its own action references, so the workflow is no weaker than the branch this migration started from. (c) is where a future change belongs if the carve-out is ever widened to the whole file. |
| DL-104 | **Withdrawn.** This entry added a `permissions` block to both workflows — `contents: read` for CI, plus `id-token: write` for CD — set `persist-credentials: false` on every checkout, and moved Google Cloud authentication onto Workload Identity Federation. The delivered workflows carry none of it: neither declares `permissions`, both checkouts use the repository default, and `cd.yml` authenticates with `google-github-actions/setup-gcloud@v0.2.1` reading `secrets.GCP_SA_KEY`, exactly as the source branch wrote it. | (a) Keep the least-privilege blocks as delivered. (b) Add the `permissions` block to CI only, since the backend job is the one this migration edits. (c) Keep WIF but drop the `permissions` blocks. | A `permissions` key, a checkout input and a change of cloud credential mechanism are three edits outside the one build line `cd.yml` is authorised to change and outside the five backend steps `ci.yml` is authorised to change. The frozen plan states the `ci.yml` delivery as "Backend job only … All frontend steps untouched" and the `cd.yml` delivery as "Line 36 only … Nothing else changes"; a workflow-level key belongs to neither, and an input added to the pristine checkout step at `L14` belongs to neither. Each of (a) to (c) is a defensible hardening and each is a scope breach; the carve-out decides the question, not the merit. A second review has re-raised all three and asked for them again — least privilege, `persist-credentials: false`, and Workload Identity Federation in place of the long-lived key — and the exclusion is explicit enough to decide it a second time, so this row records the repeat rather than reversing. What that review asked for is not reached by this migration at all: the action-reference pin it proposed is withdrawn (DL-103), and there is no Node.js toolchain floor — DL-285 records that the `node-version` input is `'14'`, byte-identical to the source branch. Both are operations changes the line-level carve-out of DL-215 does not admit. | Both jobs run with the repository's default token permissions, the checkout token stays in the work tree for later steps, and production deployment depends on a long-lived service-account JSON key in `secrets.GCP_SA_KEY`. That is the posture of the source branch, unchanged by this migration and unrepaired by it. The key's practical exposure is bounded by the workflow never running (DL-102), which is a property of a defect rather than a control: repairing the trigger makes this credential live in the same commit, so the two belong in one change. Widening the carve-out to admit `permissions: contents: read` is a two-line edit to `ci.yml` and needs no other change, which is the cheapest of the three to take up first. |
| DL-105 | **Withdrawn.** This entry checked out `workflow_run.head_sha`, tagged the image with that SHA, resolved the pushed digest, deployed the digest, and serialised production deployments through a `concurrency` group. The delivered `.github/workflows/cd.yml` carries the source-branch job: a default-ref checkout, `docker build -t $BACKEND_IMAGE`, `docker push $BACKEND_IMAGE` and `gcloud run deploy --image $BACKEND_IMAGE`, with no tag, no digest and no concurrency group. Only the `-f infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` flag is added (DL-056). | (a) Keep the digest deploy and the concurrency group. (b) Keep the commit-SHA tag alone, as the smallest of the three. (c) Keep serialisation alone. | Every element of this entry is a line the carve-out does not open. The image reference is a mutable tag with no version at all, which is the mechanism the source branch chose; changing it, adding a digest lookup and adding a `concurrency` key are three separate unauthorised edits, and a review recorded the delivered form of them as a critical scope breach. (a) to (c) are the same breach at three sizes. The frozen `cd.yml` carve-out is explicit about the same lines — it enumerates `BACKEND_IMAGE` at `L12`, `docker push` at `L37` and `gcloud run deploy … --image $BACKEND_IMAGE` at `L41-45` as unchanged — so a second review asking for a commit tag and a digest deploy is answered by the same exclusion rather than by a new judgement. The one place the mutable reference does get written down is the immutability the *container* achieves by other means: both base images in `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` are digest-pinned (DL-106), so the bytes the build starts from are fixed even though the bytes it publishes are addressed by a movable name. | The deployed bytes are whatever `$BACKEND_IMAGE` resolves to at deploy time, not necessarily the commit CI tested; two runs can interleave; and a re-push under the same reference silently changes what a later deploy pulls. All three are properties of the source-branch workflow, and none is introduced by this migration. All three are also unreached, because the workflow never triggers (DL-102) — which means the first commit that repairs the trigger inherits every one of them at once, and the serialisation is the element that matters soonest, since two concurrent `workflow_run` completions on `main` would race with no lock of any kind. |
| DL-106 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded base-image digest pinning: both stages of `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` named a patch tag and a `@sha256:` digest — `maven:3.9.16-eclipse-temurin-21` and `eclipse-temurin:21.0.11_10-jre` — so a rebuild resolved byte-identical images, together with an applicability triage of the advisories the pinned images carried. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** Neither stage carries a digest or a patch tag.
