(b) Keep the patch tags without the digests. (c) Use the floating tags the boundary names, which is what is delivered. | AAP section 0.4.1.8 states the Dockerfile carve-out line by line — the base images, the removal of the `requirements.txt` copy and the `pip install`, the two source copies, `EXPOSE 5000` unchanged, and `java -jar` in place of `CMD ["python","app.py"]` — and nothing else. Section 0.2.3 excludes new hardening capabilities by name. Every addition beyond that translation is therefore outside the boundary however sound it is in isolation. The digest pin also had a consequence beyond itself: because the pinned runtime image carried 21.0.11 while the author wanted 21.0.12, it is what forced the replacement-JRE download DL-243 records. | DL-107 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded an unprivileged runtime identity in the container image: a system group and user `scanner` at GID and UID 10001, `/app` owned `0:10001` at mode 0750, the jar copied `--chown=0:10001` and set to mode 0640 so the runtime identity could read it and not rewrite it, and `USER 10001:10001` before the entrypoint. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** None of it is in the image. (d) Set the identity outside the image, in the Cloud Run service specification, which the Terraform carve-out also does not admit. | AAP section 0.4.1.8 states the Dockerfile carve-out line by line — the base images, the removal of the `requirements.txt` copy and the `pip install`, the two source copies, `EXPOSE 5000` unchanged, and `java -jar` in place of `CMD ["python","app.py"]` — and nothing else. Section 0.2.3 excludes new hardening capabilities by name. Every addition beyond that translation is therefore outside the boundary however sound it is in isolation. Running as a non-root identity is unambiguously better practice, and it is withdrawn only because the carve-out admits no line for it and the review that found it named it explicitly among the unapproved additions. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | The container process runs as root. Nothing in the application needs root, so the privilege is unused, but a container escape would begin as root and the jar is writable by the running process. This is the most consequential single item withdrawn in this round and it is recorded plainly for that reason: (d) is where it belongs if a deployment wants it back, and that is an operations change outside this migration. |
| DL-108 | JWT configuration, signing and verification accept HS256 only; the JJWT parser's signature registry contains only `Jwts.SIG.HS256`. | (a) Accept HS384 and HS512 with the same key. (b) Keep the parser's default algorithm registry. (c) add asymmetric algorithms. | Minting already produced HS256. Restricting configuration and parser policy to that one algorithm removes algorithm-policy drift and makes a token signed under another same-key HMAC algorithm invalid. | Moving to another algorithm becomes a coordinated configuration, key-management and parser change rather than a property-only change. |
| DL-109 | Accepted JWTs carry exactly `sub`, `iat` and `exp`, with a nonblank subject and `exp` strictly after `iat`; parsing occurs once per validation attempt. | (a) Accept missing or extra claims. (b) Parse once per accessor. (c) normalize a blank subject. | These are the complete claims minted by the service and the only principal and lifetime data its filter consumes. Exact validation prevents indefinite or ambiguous signed tokens and avoids inconsistent results from repeated parsing. | Adding a registered claim such as `iss`, `aud` or `jti` requires updating both minting and the exact accepted-name set. |
| DL-110 | `scanner.jwt.expiration-minutes` must be between 1 and 60 inclusive, and seconds conversion and expiry arithmetic use checked operations. | (a) Permit zero or negative values. (b) Permit values above the documented one-hour maximum. (c) rely on overflowing arithmetic. | The technical specification fixes a one-hour maximum and a positive token lifetime. Startup validation makes invalid policy configuration fail before any token is issued. | Operators cannot use the property to create longer-lived development tokens and must change the policy deliberately to do so. |
| DL-111 | Token rejection and authentication-filter logging is a closed set of fixed sentences at `DEBUG`, and never includes token text, a claim value, a JWT parser message, an exception type or a principal. `security/JwtService.parseClaims` writes exactly one of five: a presented JWT did not verify; declared an algorithm this service does not accept; did not carry exactly the accepted claim set; carried a blank subject; carried an inconsistent issued-at and expiration pair. `security/JwtAuthenticationFilter` writes one when a request authenticates and one when a presented token names a principal the credential store does not hold. There is no reason code, no suppression count, no sampling interval and no `WARN` record on this path. | (a) Log the jjwt exception's message at `WARN`, which is what was delivered first. (b) Log the exception's simple class name alongside the sentence, so the failure category survives. (c) Replace the sentences with fixed reason codes carrying a per-reason suppressed count and a one-record-per-minute interval. (d) Log the subject on every accepted request. (e) Suppress all diagnostics. | Bearer material and subjects are attacker-influenced or identifying data, and an unauthenticated caller chooses both how many failures arrive and what the token contains. (a) writes caller-chosen text into a plain-text log at a level enabled by default — log forging and disclosure at once, since jjwt's malformed-token messages quote a fragment of the input — and it is what DL-093 recorded. (b) is defensible, since the class name comes from a closed set and cannot carry a newline, and was recorded by DL-176; it is not taken because the sentence already distinguishes the four post-signature cases, and the one case it merges — bad signature against malformed — is exactly the pair an attacker would probe. (c) is what DL-144 recorded and would add a counter and a clock to a class whose whole logging surface is five sentences behind an operator's own decision to enable `DEBUG`; the level answers the volume argument the codes existed for. (d) writes an identifying value on the success path. (e) removes the only signal that tokens are being rejected at all. | An operator cannot tell from the log which claim or which byte was wrong, nor whether a rejection was a bad signature or a malformed token, so diagnosing one client requires reproducing its request with `DEBUG` enabled for `com.codeskeptic.scanner.security`. Records cannot be tied to one request without an external correlation facility (DL-198). `security/JwtServiceTest` attaches a Logback list appender and asserts the exact sentence, the `DEBUG` level, the absence of a throwable and that neither the token nor the library message appears, so a future edit that reintroduces either fails the build. |
| DL-112 | The JWT filter and security chain share one `RequestAttributeSecurityContextRepository`; the filter saves successful authentication and runs on REQUEST, ASYNC and ERROR dispatches. | (a) Set only the thread-local context. (b) skip redispatches. (c) use an HTTP session repository. | Spring Security authorizes each registered dispatch. Persisting the context on the request keeps authentication available to later dispatches while retaining stateless session policy. | Each dispatch can repeat token validation, and request-attribute persistence does not survive a second HTTP request. |
| DL-113 | The HTTP authentication scheme name `Bearer` is matched case-insensitively and must be followed by one space and nonempty token text. | (a) Match exact case. (b) accept arbitrary whitespace. (c) parse any authorization scheme as a bearer token. | HTTP authentication scheme names are case-insensitive, while a strict separator keeps token extraction deterministic. | Noncanonical headers with tabs or multiple leading spaces are rejected even if another implementation might accept them. |
| DL-114 | Spring Security logout is disabled. | (a) Keep the framework's permit-all `/logout`. (b) implement token revocation. (c) leave logout enabled for POST only. | The service uses stateless custom JWTs and has no revocation store, so the framework logout endpoint cannot invalidate a token and would create a second public route outside the declared surface. | Clients have no server-side logout and must discard their token locally or wait for expiry. |
| DL-115 | Every protected unauthenticated response is an empty 401 carrying `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer`. | (a) A bare 401 without a challenge. (b) a JSON error envelope. (c) a container-generated error page. | The challenge advertises the authentication scheme, and an explicit empty response preserves the closed set of source error strings. | Clients receive no detail distinguishing an absent token from an invalid or expired one. |
| DL-116 | `scanner.auth.password-hash` must be a 60-character `$2a$`, `$2b$` or `$2y$` bcrypt modular-crypt value with cost 10 through 14. | (a) Accept any nonblank text. (b) accept every legal bcrypt cost. (c) validate on the first login. | Shape validation catches misconfiguration at context creation, and the cost range excludes both weak and operationally excessive values without reproducing the hash in diagnostics. | A hash generated outside the accepted cost window cannot be used until policy changes, even though a bcrypt implementation could verify it. |
| DL-117 | `AuthController` maps only credential-rejection exceptions to empty 401; authentication-service and other infrastructure exceptions propagate to the generic 500 envelope. | (a) Catch every `AuthenticationException` as 401. (b) expose provider messages. (c) add a new 503 wire contract. | A broken provider is a server fault, not evidence that the submitted credential is wrong. Keeping the existing generic 500 avoids inventing a new source-incompatible response body. | Clients cannot distinguish a transient authentication backend outage from another internal failure. |
| DL-118 | `POST /auth/token` accepts at most 4096 encoded request bytes and at most 256 characters in each credential field, returning the same empty 401 before authentication when either bound is exceeded. **This bound applies to that one route and to no other.** An earlier revision also placed a general 65,536-byte ceiling on every other body-bearing request through a second nested filter registered behind `AuthorizationFilter`; that filter, its constant, its bodyless-method set and its registration are deleted, and the eleven pre-existing routes now carry no body-size bound of this chain's making. Because the size filter has to read the body to measure it, an accepted body is replayed to Spring MVC through `security/SecurityConfig.CachedBodyRequest`, which serves the buffered bytes from `getInputStream()` and decodes them in `getReader()` with the charset the request declared, falling back to UTF-8 when the request declares none, one this JVM does not provide, or one that is not a legal charset name. | (a) No bounds at all. (b) Bean Validation with a 400 envelope. (c) Reject only after bcrypt. (d) Bound the body without replaying it, leaving the consumed stream for the converter. (e) Let `Charset.forName` raise for a charset the JVM cannot supply. (f) Keep the general 65,536-byte ceiling on the eleven pre-existing routes and record the divergence here, which is what an earlier revision did. (g) Keep a general ceiling at a much larger value. | The public route otherwise allows unbounded allocation and expensive credential work. A pre-deserialization byte limit and a post-binding character limit contain both costs while preserving the route's generic rejection behavior and validation-parity rule. Both bounds are confined to this route because it is net-new: nothing in the retired tree served it, so no parity contract is disturbed by bounding it. (f) and (g) extend a bound onto the eleven routes the migration preserves, where the retired Pydantic schemas and the retired `String` columns declared no length, no pattern and no range at all — the validation-parity requirement admits required-versus-optional and nothing more, so a length ceiling on a preserved route is a new restriction on an existing contract whatever value it carries. A body one of those routes would have accepted is now accepted, and a body large enough to matter is bounded by the container and by `server.max-http-request-header-size` rather than by this chain. (d) would leave the handler an empty body for every accepted request, so replay is a consequence of the bound rather than a separate decision. (e) would raise `UnsupportedCharsetException` or `IllegalCharsetNameException` — both unchecked, both from a caller-supplied `Content-Type` parameter — from inside the filter chain, where no `@ExceptionHandler` can answer them; the fallback keeps a caller from choosing the failure mode. | The two bounds are service policy not present in the retired schemas; exceptionally long legitimate credentials cannot authenticate. Withdrawing the general ceiling reopens the exposure it closed: a body-bearing request on an authenticated route may carry as much as the container accepts, and the converter allocates it, so an authenticated caller can force a large allocation. The controls that remain are authentication itself, the container's own limits and the deployment's ingress; a deployment that needs a body ceiling on those routes has to set one there. A body declaring an unusable charset is decoded as UTF-8 rather than rejected, so a caller that meant a different encoding sees its credential fail to authenticate rather than a diagnostic; the substitution is recorded once at `WARN`. |
| DL-119 | **Extended by DL-203, which adds the two echoing renderings and applies the policy to every caller-supplied value. The shared class this entry once named is withdrawn; the policy it held is not.** The log-redaction renderings are defined by policy and each consumer carries the one it needs; there is no shared class. The renderings are: an identifier as `hmac256:` plus the first eight hex characters of a keyed HMAC-SHA-256 over its text, stable within one process and unrelated across processes; a failure as its class simple name alone; free text guarded to printable ASCII and cut to a stated length; a provider field as either a fixed-shape token or the literal `absent`; and a sanitized failure detail of up to five causal links and ten frames. Every component that writes one of these carries the rendering itself, and DL-197 states the policy the renderings serve. | (a) Hold the renderings in one shared class, `util/LogSafe`, which is what an earlier revision delivered and which DL-208 withdraws on file-inventory grounds. (b) Hold them on an authorised class such as `config/ScannerProperties`. (c) Carry each rendering in the component that writes it, which is what is delivered. (d) Configure redaction in Logback instead, through a converter or a masking pattern. | The renderings are a policy and not a place: what matters is that no caller-supplied text, no provider body and no throwable reaches a record unguarded, and that is true of every component whether the guard is shared or repeated. The shared class was the better engineering and is withdrawn for scope alone (DL-208). (b) puts logging behaviour on a binding record. (d) was rejected because a Logback masking pattern cannot distinguish a value that must be digested from one that must be dropped, and it would place the policy outside the code that knows which is which. | The same few lines of rendering now appear in several components, so a change to the digest form or to the printable-ASCII guard must be made in each and a divergence between two of them is caught by neither a shared test nor a compiler. That is the cost DL-208 accepts. Nothing about what reaches a record changed. |
| DL-120 | `service/ResponseService` builds the wire record `dto/ResponseDto` only after the row is stored, so every returned record carries the database-generated identifier. There is no separate pre-persistence response type in the tree: model output is validated as non-blank inside `service/LlmService`, and an unusable reply raises before any row is written. | (a) Reuse `dto/ResponseDto` with `id = null` for the pre-persistence state. (b) Introduce an internal pre-persistence record beside the wire record, which an earlier revision of this entry recorded. (c) Fabricate an identifier before storing. (d) Store blank or absent model output as a successful draft. | A database-generated identifier does not exist before the insert, while the wire contract requires one, so the record is built after the insert and the question of a nullable identifier never arises. (a) would put a nullable identifier into the type the route serialises. (b) was recorded and withdrawn: it added a second record that had to evolve in step with the wire record for no behaviour the single record does not already give, since validity is decided at the adapter boundary and storage is the only step that assigns an identifier. (c) would collide with the generator. (d) would answer 201 for a reply a human cannot review. | Blank or absent model output produces a generation failure rather than a stored row, so a provider that answers with empty content costs a call and stores nothing. The wire record is the only response shape, so any future pre-persistence state would need this decision revisited. |
| DL-121 | Response generation performs the tweet read, OpenAI call and response insert without one surrounding service transaction; repository calls own short transactions and no connection is held during the network call. | (a) Keep one transaction across the whole method. (b) make the OpenAI call asynchronous inside the transaction. (c) manually manage an `EntityManager`. | A slow external request must not reserve a pool connection. The loaded tweet is converted to a DTO before the call and its identifier can be associated with the new response during the later repository transaction. | The tweet can be deleted between read and insert, surfacing as a storage failure, and the detached association depends on the row still existing. |
| DL-122 | Partial response updates acquire a pessimistic write lock on the row before applying content or approval changes. | (a) Save an unlocked stale entity. (b) add a version column. (c) issue field-specific update statements. | Locking preserves both independently writable fields without adding a schema column or duplicating update queries, and it fits the existing transactional service method. | Concurrent updates serialize and can wait, deadlock or fail under database lock timeouts. |
| DL-123 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a finite maximum of 1,000 rows per request on both list routes: `per_page` was accepted at any positive value on the wire and silently reduced to a `MAXIMUM_PER_PAGE` of 1,000 before the query, so the largest page any request could obtain was bounded regardless of what it asked for. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** Neither list route caps `per_page`. `service/TwitterService` and `service/ResponseService` pass the requested value through, `util/QueryParameters` and every `MAXIMUM_PER_PAGE` constant are deleted, and a request naming `per_page=2147483647` is answered 200 with `"per_page":2147483647` in the pagination block — confirmed against the running service on both routes. | (a) Keep the 1,000-row cap. (b) Cap and report the reduction in the response. (c) Reject a `per_page` above the cap with 400. (d) Pass every value through, which is what is delivered. | The wire-contract parity clause preserves the query parameters and their defaults, and the source applied no bound: `page` defaulted to 1 and `per_page` to 10 and nothing reduced either. A silent reduction is also the worst shape a bound can take, because the response restates a `per_page` the caller did not ask for. AAP section 0.6.5.2 limits validation to what the source declared, and a page ceiling is validation improvement. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | One request can ask for an arbitrarily large page, so the database work, the mapped object graph and the serialised body are bounded only by the table size and by heap. The source had exactly this exposure. (b) and (c) each change the wire contract, which is why neither was taken; a deployment that needs a bound belongs behind an ingress that enforces one. |

(a) is the failure Rule 1 exists to prevent, and it was the sharper form of that failure here: the log's own maintenance row already claimed this identifier had become a pointer, so the file contradicted itself as well as the code. (b) breaks every citation in version history that names this identifier. (d) gives one subject two owners. | A reader arriving from an older commit reaches a pointer rather than a mechanism. No artifact in the delivered tree cites this identifier. The withdrawn `Persistable` form survives in version history only, and nothing mechanically ties DL-099's list of rewritten rows to the rows themselves — that list is prose, so the same drift can recur and is guarded only by review. |

(d) names a real owner of a narrower subject, the projections themselves, while the claim this entry made was about the consistency of the whole record, which is DL-180's subject. (b) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier; DL-099 directs a rewrite in place. (b) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier; DL-099 directs a rewrite in place. | The two behaviours are easy to conflate — one absent property yields a substituted identifier, another yields a skipped page — so a reader needs both DL-090 and DL-219; each names the other. | DL-127 | Offset-bearing Notion timestamps are normalized to UTC before conversion to `LocalDateTime`; local and date-only values retain their existing parsing paths. | (a) Drop the offset directly. (b) retain `OffsetDateTime` on the DTO. (c) use the host default zone. | Dropping an offset without normalization changes the represented instant. UTC provides one deterministic canonical zone without changing the established DTO type. | The original textual offset is lost and a local timestamp with no offset remains inherently ambiguous. |
| DL-128 | The Notion `RestClient` carries a connect timeout and a read timeout supplied by this application and nothing else, and a timeout-derived resource failure is translated to the fixed message `Notion request timed out`. The two values are configuration, not constants: `scanner.notion.connect-timeout-seconds` (default 5) and `scanner.notion.read-timeout-seconds` (default 10). The transport and the validation of those two values are owned by DL-150 and DL-193. | (a) Rely on the client's own defaults, which are unbounded. (b) Configure the bounds per call site. (c) Let the transport's own exception message reach the caller. | A shared transport bound stops a stalled upstream call from occupying a request thread or a scheduler thread for as long as the socket stays open, and one fixed translation keeps vendor and request detail out of the error surface the caller sees. (a) is the unbounded state. (b) would spread the same two values across call sites with no call needing a different bound. (c) would put provider text on a path the logging policy of DL-197 keeps content-free. | A slow but healthy Notion response beyond the read bound is aborted. Timeout classification matches cause types whose simple name contains `Timeout`, which is broader than one transport's exception type and is what keeps the translation working if the request factory changes. |
| DL-129 | OpenAI requests carry the source-compatible prompt as a **single user message** and no system or developer message. `service/LlmService` builds the request with `.addUserMessage(prompt)` alone, reproducing `Completion.create(prompt=…)` at `backend/app/services/llm_service.py:L19-26`. The structural no-publish boundary is enforced by the absence of any publish path in the tree, not by prompt text. | (a) Prepend a system instruction that treats the prompt as untrusted data, forbids tools and requires a concise human-reviewed draft. (b) Escape or delete tweet text before it enters the prompt. (c) Add a publishing tool. | This entry previously recorded (a) as delivered, and the delivered class does not implement it: the only message builder called is `addUserMessage`, and no `addSystemMessage` or `addDeveloperMessage` appears anywhere in the tree. The entry is rewritten to the behaviour the code has, per the revision rule of DL-099. Leaving it as written would have been worse than a documentation gap: it would have recorded a prompt-injection mitigation that does not exist, so a reader auditing the untrusted-data boundary would have been misled into believing one was in place. Single-message parity is also the defensible position on its own terms — the parity requirement fixes the prompt template verbatim from `:L16` under DL-035, and the generated text is never published: `is_approved` is a flag a human reads under the never-auto-post invariant, so a human review stands between any model output and any public action. (b) would alter the audited literal segments of the prompt. (c) is forbidden outright by the never-auto-post invariant. | Tweet text and `ai_tools_mentioned` values reach the model as part of the user message with no trust boundary marked inside the prompt, so a crafted post can influence the generated draft. That exposure is bounded by the human review gate and by the structural absence of a publish path, not by prompt engineering. If a system instruction is ever added, this entry, the prompt template of DL-035 and the parity claim against `:L19-26` all have to move together. |
| DL-130 | The lazily created `OpenAIClient` is closed under the creation monitor during bean destruction and cannot be recreated afterward. | (a) Never close it. (b) construct and close per request. Routing them through the existing one-key `ErrorResponse` preserves envelope shape and avoids exposing parser or converter details. | The four reason phrases become additional stable response strings even though the retired Flask app registered no equivalent handlers. |
| DL-132 | `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` uses Spring's `ExceptionHandlerMethodResolver` for assignability; authentication and access-denied exceptions resolve to the declared generic `Exception` handler rather than being described as unhandled. | (a) Compare annotation values by exact type. Resolver-based assertions pin the actual MVC semantics while the security-chain tests separately prove filter failures are answered before MVC. | A future narrowing of the generic handler changes these assertions even if protected-route security behavior remains unchanged. |
| DL-133 | DTO and mapper components backed by nullable source columns use boxed/reference types and preserve null; only externally required persistent identifiers are rejected as null. | (a) Primitive components. (c) would leave every commit and comment citing DL-139 with a dangling identifier, against this log's revision policy. (d) is ruled out by the record-mapping rule, which maps a required Pydantic field onto a non-null contract: eight of the nine `tweet.py` fields and all five `response.py` fields are declared without `Optional`, so a wire record that admits `null` for them is not the port the record-mapping rule specifies. | A reader following a DL-139 citation from an older commit takes one extra hop to reach `DL-080`. No code cites this identifier: the two records, both mappers and both task components cite `DL-080` directly, so the hop is only ever taken from version history. |
| DL-140 | **Superseded by DL-092.** This identifier and DL-092 recorded the same decision — that `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` declares an explicit handler for each framework-reported client failure, so 400 `Bad request`, 405 `Method not allowed` with an `Allow` header, 415 `Unsupported media type` and 406 `Not acceptable` are answered as such and the catch-all 500 is left for a genuine server failure. `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` cites DL-092, which holds the decision, its alternatives and its risks in full; the conversion-versus-write split of the same hierarchy is DL-188. | (a) Leave both entries standing on one subject. (b) Delete the row. (c) Renumber DL-092 into this identifier. (d) Split the subject so each entry owns part of it. | Two entries on one subject cannot both be checked and a reader has no way to tell which is current — the same defect the review recorded for an identifier carrying three subjects at once. The cited entry is kept and the uncited one becomes the pointer. (b) and (c) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier. (d) would invent a division neither entry was written around, and the one genuine sub-division of this hierarchy already has its own entry in DL-188. | A reader following this identifier from an older commit takes one extra hop to reach DL-092. No artifact in the delivered tree cites this identifier. |
| DL-141 | **Superseded by DL-108 and DL-186.** This entry stated that `scanner.jwt.algorithm` selects, per configured value, the algorithm every token is signed with, the JCA name of the derived key and the one algorithm the parser accepts, across HS256, HS384 and HS512, with a key-length check per algorithm. The delivered `security/JwtService` supports **HS256 only**: it signs with `Jwts.SIG.HS256`, builds its parser with `sig().clear().add(Jwts.SIG.HS256)`, and refuses to start when `scanner.jwt.algorithm` names anything else — DL-108. The secret's minimum length is one value, 32 bytes, and is DL-186's subject. There is no per-algorithm table in the tree. | (a) Leave this entry describing a three-algorithm mechanism the tree does not have. (b) Delete the row. (c) Implement the three-algorithm mechanism so the entry becomes true. | (c) contradicts DL-108, which owns the accepted-algorithm set and argues it down to one member on the grounds that this service both mints and verifies its own tokens and has no second party to negotiate with; widening the set would also widen the parser's acceptance, which is the half that matters for a presented token. The entry is therefore the stale side and is rewritten in place. (b) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier; DL-099 directs a rewrite in place. | An operator who sets `ALGORITHM=HS512` gets a startup failure naming the one supported value rather than HS512 tokens — a louder outcome than this entry described, and the one DL-108 intends. A reader arriving here for the secret-length rule is sent to DL-186 for it. |
| DL-142 | `scanner.jwt.expiration-minutes` is validated to 1 … 60 inclusive while `security/JwtService` is constructed, so a value outside that range fails startup, and the conversion to seconds is performed once with `Math.multiplyExact`. | (a) Accept whatever configuration binding produces — the behaviour delivered before this entry. (b) Clamp an out-of-range value into the range silently. (c) Declare `@Min(1) @Max(60)` on the record component and annotate `ScannerProperties` with `@Validated`. (d) Reject only zero and negative values, leaving the documented maximum unenforced. | DL-017 fixes the lifetime's documented maximum at one hour, and a lifetime is only meaningful above zero, so 1 … 60 is the range the two together define. (a) admits `0` and negative values, which mint a token whose `exp` is at or before its `iat` so every request it is presented on is answered 401, and admits values near `Long.MAX_VALUE`, where the minutes-to-nanoseconds conversion inside `Duration.ofMinutes` overflows and the failure surfaces as an arithmetic error at the first mint rather than at startup. (b) hides a misconfiguration, which is the failure mode DL-016 already rejects for the secret. (c) is a working mechanism but splits the `scanner.jwt` group's validation across two files while the secret and the algorithm are already validated in `JwtService`, and it reports a binding failure rather than a message naming the property, the offending value and the accepted range. (d) leaves the documented maximum a comment. | The upper bound is a compile-time constant, so an operator who wants a longer session cannot configure one: the bound and DL-017 must both change. A deployment that had been running with an out-of-range value — nothing in this repository ships one — stops starting after this change rather than continuing with its previous behaviour. |
| DL-143 | A token whose signature verifies but which carries no `exp` claim is refused: the parsing path answers an empty optional, so neither `extractUsername` nor `extractExpiration` returns a value for it. | (a) Accept it, which is what jjwt does on its own because it validates `exp` only when the claim is present — the behaviour delivered before this entry. (b) Accept it and apply the configured lifetime to its `iat` as an implied expiry. (c) Require `iat` as well, and refuse a token whose `iat` lies in the future. | Every token this service mints carries `exp` (DL-018), so a same-key token without one was not minted here. Accepting it would make that token valid for as long as `SECRET_KEY` lives, which is precisely the exposure the bounded lifetime of DL-017 exists to limit, and it would do so with no revocation path. (b) invents an expiry the token does not carry and makes acceptance depend on configuration that the token's issuer never saw. (c) is defensible and would catch a clock-skewed or forged `iat`, but `iat` is informational in RFC 7519 while `exp` is the claim that bounds validity, and adding an `iat` rule would refuse tokens that are otherwise entirely valid under this service's own contract. | A second issuer sharing `SECRET_KEY` and minting tokens without `exp` would find them refused; no such issuer exists in this repository, where `AuthController` is the only mint path. The refusal is recorded as the `EXPIRATION_ABSENT` reason code of DL-144, so the cause is visible only when `DEBUG` is enabled for `security.JwtService`. |
| DL-144 | **Superseded by DL-111.** This entry stated that a verification failure is recorded at `DEBUG` as one of six fixed reason codes — `MALFORMED`, `SIGNATURE_REJECTED`, `EXPIRED`, `UNSUPPORTED`, `EXPIRATION_ABSENT`, `REJECTED` — each carrying a count of records suppressed for that reason and rate-limited to one record per minute. No reason code, no suppression count and no per-reason interval exists in the delivered tree: `security/JwtService.parseClaims` writes one of five fixed English sentences at `DEBUG`, unsampled, and `security/JwtAuthenticationFilter` writes one fixed sentence when a request authenticates and one when a token names an unknown principal. DL-111 owns that contract. | (a) Leave this entry describing codes and sampling the tree does not have. (b) Delete the row. (c) Implement the codes and the sampling so the entry becomes true. | (c) would add a suppression counter and a clock to a class whose whole logging surface is five `DEBUG` sentences behind an operator's own decision to enable the level, which is the containment DL-111 settles on; the volume argument the codes were introduced for is answered by the level. The entry is the stale side and is rewritten. (b) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier; DL-099 directs a rewrite in place. | A reader who remembers the reason codes will not find them in the code; the five sentences are the whole vocabulary. No artifact in the delivered tree cites this identifier. |
| DL-145 | `scanner.openai.reasoning-effort` is declared with the default **`none`**, is read by `service/LlmService` and reaches the request as `ReasoningEffort.of(value.trim())`; a blank value omits the parameter entirely. The accepted-value comment in `application.yml` names the values the configured model accepts. Earlier revisions of this entry declared the default `minimal` (unread, then rejected by the provider) and then `low`. | (a) Keep `low`, which this entry previously declared. (b) Keep `minimal`, its first declared value. (c) Leave the property unread, which the code originally did. (d) Default to blank so the parameter is always omitted. (e) Default to `medium` or `high`. (f) Validate the value against a fixed accepted set at startup and refuse to start on an unknown one. | Two constraints decide this jointly with DL-200 and DL-202. The provider accepts an explicit `temperature` for the configured GPT-5.x model only while reasoning effort is `none`, and a reasoning effort above `none` spends the completion budget on reasoning tokens before any visible text is produced. The migration requires `temperature` 0.7 and `max_completion_tokens` 150 as transcribed configuration defaults, so `none` is the only effort value under which both mandated source values can be sent and still produce a reply — which is why (a) and (b) are rejected even though both were measured as accepted values on their own: `low` forces a choice between parameter parity and a working generation, and `minimal` is refused outright with HTTP 400 `unsupported_value`. (c) is not available: a declared key nothing reads is the configuration drift this migration exists to close, and DL-193 records the same defect for the Notion version header. (d) omits a parameter the model does support and leaves the property nominally read but never exercised. (e) spends more budget on reasoning, which 150 tokens cannot afford. (f) would hard-code a per-model accepted set into the application, while DL-033 keeps the model in configuration precisely so that set can change without a code change; the provider's own rejection, reported with its `code` and `param` under DL-203, is the check that stays correct as models move. | The accepted-value set and the parameter-compatibility rule are properties of the configured model, not of this service, so the comment in `application.yml` and this entry can go stale the moment `scanner.openai.model` changes: an operator who changes the model must re-check both this value and `temperature`, and the failure if they do not is an HTTP 400 naming `reasoning_effort` or `temperature`, which DL-203 makes legible. Reasoning effort `none` also means the model applies no deliberate reasoning pass to the reply, which is appropriate for one short reply to one post and would not be for a harder task. No test can pin the constraint, since it lives at the provider; the guard is that the value is configuration and the rejection is actionable. |
| DL-146 | The OpenAI client carries an explicit request timeout (`scanner.openai.request-timeout-seconds`, default 30) and an explicit retry limit (`scanner.openai.max-retries`, default 2), and `ResponseService.generateResponse` declares no transaction, so the model call sits between two short repository transactions. No circuit breaker is added: a provider failure surfaces as the 500 literal of `backend/app/api/responses.py:L49`. | (a) Keep the SDK defaults implicitly, as the previously delivered adapter did. (b) Set a timeout but leave retries at the SDK default. (c) Keep `@Transactional` on `generateResponse` and accept that a connection is held for the duration of the model call. (d) Mark the method `@Transactional(propagation = NOT_SUPPORTED)` instead of removing the annotation. (e) Add resilience4j and a circuit breaker around the call. | A timeout and a retry count that are chosen by the library rather than by this service are a deployment property nobody reviewed; naming them makes the worst-case wait per attempt arithmetic rather than a guess. The database consequence is the sharper one: with a transaction open across the call, one slow provider response holds a pooled connection for its whole duration, so a handful of concurrent generations can exhaust the pool and stall unrelated requests. (c) accepts exactly that. (d) is equivalent in effect but leaves an annotation on the method that suggests a transactional contract it does not have. (e) is a new capability with its own dependency, configuration surface and failure semantics, and the scope boundary excludes it. | Removing the transaction means the insert commits before the row is mapped, so a failure after the insert leaves a stored draft rather than rolling it back; the only step after the insert reads columns of the row the insert returned. The mapped `Tweet` is detached, so any future mapper change that touched the lazy `responses` collection would fail outside a transaction — the mapping test of DL-082 covers the current reads. Retries multiply the worst-case wait: three attempts at a 30-second timeout is a 90-second ceiling per request. |
| DL-147 | The mechanism this entry described is withdrawn. It stated that `service/LlmService` closes its client with no coordination beyond the destroyed flag — no active-call counter, no permit bound and no drain interval — and that the client's own shutdown decided the fate of a request already in flight. The delivered class coordinates release with the work using it, through the read/write lock protocol DL-266 states and owns. What this entry still owns is the negative half of that protocol: there is no active-call counter and no permit bound on concurrent calls. An intermediate revision did add an `AtomicInteger` counter with a polling drain; nothing decremented it and nothing invoked the wait, so it was dead state and has been removed. | (a) Leave this entry describing the uncoordinated close it once described. (b) Re-add the counter and the polling drain and wire them up. (c) Keep the counter as an observability field, decremented in a `finally` block. (d) State the delivered protocol here as well as in DL-266. | A row that denies a mechanism the class has is as wrong as one that claims a mechanism it lacks, so the claim is corrected and the protocol itself is left to the single row that owns it. (b) reimplements a read/write lock with a spin, which DL-266 already rejected on its own terms. (c) keeps a field with no reader: the drain is expressed by the write lock, so a counter would be maintained for nothing and would have to be kept correct for nothing. (d) would put one subject in two rows, which is the condition that produced this contradiction. | A reader looking for the shutdown protocol must follow the reference to DL-266; this row states only what is absent. Because no counter exists, the number of generations in flight is not observable from the bean — the request bound and the shutdown bound are what limit it. |
| DL-148 | A provider failure is written to the log exactly once, by the adapter that made the call, and the record carries only the HTTP status, the provider's error `code` and `type`, and the `x-request-id` response header. The throwable is never handed to the logger, in `LlmService` or in `ResponseService`; the cause travels on the exception instead. | (a) Log the throwable with a stack trace at the point of failure and again at the orchestrating layer, as the previously delivered code did. (b) Log the exception's message without the stack trace. (c) Log nothing and rely on the exception reaching a handler. | The OpenAI SDK documents that a service exception's message can carry the provider's response JSON, which for this service means the generated reply and whatever the prompt quoted from a monitored post — a CWE-532 exposure in a log that is not treated as sensitive. Logging it twice doubled both the exposure and the noise. (b) still copies the provider body, because the body is inside the message. (c) removes the diagnostic entirely: the status, the error code and the request identifier are the fields a support request to the provider needs, and none of them is content. | The stack trace is no longer in the log, so an unexpected failure has to be diagnosed from the exception's type name plus the request identifier. `code` and `type` are provider-defined strings; they are enumerated error identifiers rather than content, but they are still values this service does not control. |
| DL-149 | The earlier per-service printable-ASCII/truncation guards are withdrawn. Caller-supplied identifiers are rendered by the keyed-digest rendering DL-119 defines, carried by each writing component. | (a) Keep duplicated private guards. (b) log parsed identifiers only. (c) add a global Logback encoder. | The shared utility gives every remediated logging surface one fixed non-reversible representation and removes duplicated sanitizers. | The truncated digest can collide and no longer preserves a readable prefix of the identifier. |
| DL-150 | The Notion transport carries a bounded connect timeout (`scanner.notion.connect-timeout-seconds`, default 5) and a bounded read timeout (`scanner.notion.read-timeout-seconds`, default 10), the connect bound carried by the retained `HttpClient` bean of DL-264 and the read bound applied per request by the `JdkClientHttpRequestFactory` built over it. A Notion failure reaches the caller as a `RestClientException`; a mirror write is retried within the bounded budget of DL-253 and the relational store has already accepted the data, so the mirror failing never fails the primary write. | (a) Leave the framework defaults, which are null timeouts — the state delivered before this entry. (b) Set only a read timeout. (c) Add a retry with backoff. (d) Add a circuit breaker so a failing mirror stops being called. | Boot's default request-factory settings carry no timeout at all, so a Notion request that never answers holds its caller — a request thread or, once the task files land, a scheduler thread — for as long as the socket stays open. A secondary mirror must not be able to do that. (b) leaves a connect that hangs on an unreachable host unbounded. (c) and (d) are both defensible for a mirror, but each is a new capability: a retry needs an idempotency story for `pages.create`, which is not idempotent, and a breaker needs resilience4j plus its own configuration surface. The scope boundary excludes both, so the failure policy is stated instead: fail the mirror call, keep the relational write. | Five and ten seconds are chosen without production measurements; a slow but healthy Notion response beyond ten seconds now fails where it previously completed. A caller that treats a mirror failure as fatal would propagate it — no caller exists yet, and the policy above is what the task files must follow when they land. |
| DL-151 | The `Notion-Version` header value is `scanner.notion.api-version`, defaulting to `2022-06-28`, and the adapter keeps using the database-query endpoint `POST /v1/databases/{id}/query`. | (a) Keep the version pinned in code with no property, as delivered before this entry. (b) Move to a current API version and the data-source endpoints it introduces. (c) Send no version header and accept the provider's default. | `2022-06-28` is the version whose request and response shapes the adapter is written against: the property-object shapes, the `results` array and the query filter all match it, and the configuration surface this project declares carries a database identifier (`NOTION_DATABASE_ID`) and nothing else. (b) is the upgrade path and is recorded as such — current Notion versions address a *data source* inside a database, so it needs a new configuration value, a new endpoint set and a re-verified response shape; that is a change of external contract rather than a defect fix, and this migration's scope forbids adding the capability here. (c) is not an option: the header is mandatory, and omitting it makes the provider choose a version the code has not been written against. Making the value a property is what lets an operator move a deployment forward, or pin it back, without a rebuild. | The pinned version will eventually be withdrawn by the provider, at which point every Notion call fails until the property is raised and the response mapping is re-verified — the mapping is written against this version's shapes, so raising the property alone is not sufficient. The upgrade plan is: declare the data-source identifier, switch the query path, re-verify the property shapes, then raise the default. |
| DL-152 | **Withdrawn.** This entry described a start-up schema pre-flight: before its first write the adapter was to read the target database once, confirm that it declares all seven properties this mirror writes with the types it writes — `Content` title, `Author` rich_text, `Timestamp` date, `Doubt Rating` number, `Engagement` number, `Tweet Id` rich_text, `Response` rich_text — fail with an `IllegalStateException` naming every absent or mistyped property, and remember the result so the check ran at most once per instance. **No such mechanism was ever delivered.** `service/NotionService` issues no `GET` against the database, declares no property-name inventory, holds no cached check result and has no method that performs one, and `service/NotionServiceTest` asserts none of it; the adapter's only provider calls are the `POST` database query, the `POST` page create and the `PATCH` page update. The delivered behaviour is therefore alternative (a) of this entry: the adapter writes, and a schema mismatch reaches it as a provider validation rejection, reported under DL-153 as the status, the `code` member and the `x-request-id` header. | (a) Write and let the provider reject the request — the delivered behaviour. (b) Create the missing properties by patching the database schema. (c) Document the required schema and check nothing. (d) Re-check on every write. (e) Implement the pre-flight this entry described, which is what a reader of the earlier wording would expect to find. | A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, and this one asserted a delivered, cached, seven-property check that never existed — the defect this log's own revision policy (DL-099) exists to catch, in its sharpest form, because two other rows had come to lean on the phantom for their diagnostics story. It is withdrawn rather than implemented because implementing it now would add a provider request, a property-name contract with the operator's workspace and a start-up failure mode that no requirement asks for: the migration boundary admits no new capability, and mirroring is a secondary concern whose failure must never block the relational system of record. (b) has this service own a schema an operator also edits. (c) is the delivered posture, stated honestly here. (d) spends a request per write. | The diagnosis a schema mismatch produces is coarser than this entry promised: an operator sees the provider's status, `code` and `x-request-id` and not the offending property name, and must reproduce the call outside this service — or read the property map recorded in the entries covering it — to identify the property. DL-084 and DL-153 no longer claim otherwise. The seven property names remain a contract with the operator's Notion workspace, enforced by the provider at write time rather than by this service at startup. |
| DL-153 | A Notion failure is logged once and carries three fields: the HTTP status, the `code` member of the error body, and the `x-request-id` response header. Both members are rendered by the fixed-shape token check DL-119 defines, and a field that is absent or fails that check is recorded as `absent`. The `message` member of the error body is **not** logged. The response body is never logged, the throwable is never handed to the logger, and the failure is named by its class simple name alone, the type-only rendering of DL-119. This is the Notion form of the single policy DL-197 part (1) states and DL-084 enumerates; a later revision of this entry admitted the `message` member cut to 200 characters and that revision is withdrawn. | (a) Log the throwable, which renders the method, the URI and the response body. (b) Log the exception message only. (c) Log the whole error body but only at DEBUG. (d) Log the status alone and read nothing from the body. (e) Log the `message` cut to 200 characters, which a later revision of this entry authorised. (f) Log the `message` unbounded. (g) Keep the two private `Pattern` fields this class declared rather than the shared rendering. | A `RestClientResponseException` renders the request method, the request URI and the response body, and the body of a failed Notion write echoes the property values that were sent — the mirrored post's text, its author identifier and its identifiers — into a log that is not treated as sensitive (CWE-532). (b) is the same exposure by another route, because the body is inside the message. (c) puts the exposure behind a level rather than removing it, and DEBUG is enabled on exactly the systems where someone is investigating. (d) is safe but discards `code`, which is the field that distinguishes a validation mistake from a rate limit from a revoked token. (e) recovered one further diagnosis — Notion names the offending property only in `message` — and is withdrawn because the cost was mispriced three ways: `message` is free text by the provider's contract, so a length cut bounds its size and not its content; the accessor applied no shape check and no line-break folding, unlike its two siblings, so a `message` carrying `\r\n` could forge a log record (CWE-117); and the class's own javadoc promised a record with "no response body", which this member is read from, so the code contradicted its stated contract. It also left this entry, DL-084, DL-148 and DL-197 stating three different permitted field sets, which makes the policy unconformable — the defect Rule 1 is meant to prevent in the document it designates as the single source of truth. The diagnosis is not recovered elsewhere: DL-152 once claimed a start-up pre-flight naming every absent or mistyped property, and that entry is withdrawn because no such check was ever delivered. (f) leaves the record's size to the provider. (g) is the duplication DL-149 withdrew. | A Notion validation rejection now reports the class of fault and not the individual property, so an operator has to reproduce the call outside this service to see which property Notion objected to; the DL-152 startup check that was once claimed to report it was never delivered. `code` and `x-request-id` cannot carry a line break, whitespace or a property value, because a value failing the shared shape check is replaced by `absent` rather than logged, so neither can be used to forge a record (CWE-117). Reading the two members still parses the error body in memory; a body large enough to matter is a provider anomaly and is still never written anywhere. |
| DL-154 | A query's `page_size` is held between 1 and 100: a non-positive limit becomes the transcribed default of 10 and a limit above 100 is capped at 100 and reported. | (a) Forward whatever the caller asked for, as delivered before this entry. (b) Reject a limit above 100 with an exception. (c) Page automatically until the caller's larger limit is satisfied. | The provider rejects `page_size` above 100, so forwarding 500 produces a guaranteed validation failure for a request that could have been served. (b) turns a caller's over-ask into an error on a read path whose contract is "up to this many", where returning up to 100 satisfies the contract. (c) is a new capability — several requests per call, an accumulating result set and a partial-failure story — and the cursor parameter already lets a caller page deliberately. | A caller asking for 500 silently receives at most 100 and must use the cursor to continue; the cap is reported at `WARN` so the truncation is visible. |
| DL-155 | **Superseded by DL-292.** This entry has described three different states of one subject and neither of the first two is the tree. It first recorded a decision to split text written to a title or rich-text property into items of at most 2,000 characters, to write at most 100 items, to truncate beyond 200,000 characters and to write a blank value as an empty array. It was then rewritten as a withdrawal, on the ground that `service/NotionService` wrote one item per property and that the long-value case was unreachable because `tweets.content` and the generated reply were bounded below the provider's per-item limit by the capacity their columns were generated at. **Both statements are now false.** The columns are generated as `text` on MySQL and `varchar(32600)` on PostgreSQL and H2 (DL-068), which is an order of magnitude above the provider's 2,000-character per-item limit, so the long-value case is reachable through this service's own write paths. And the split is delivered: `textItems(String)` carries a value as one item per 2,000 characters and `readText` concatenates every item, which DL-292 owns and states. The item cap of 100, the 200,000-character truncation and the empty-array rendering of a blank value are **not** delivered and are not part of DL-292. | (a) Leave this entry describing a mechanism the tree does not carry, in either of its two earlier forms. (b) Delete the row. (c) Restate the delivered split here as well as in DL-292. | One subject is owned by exactly one row, so the row that states the delivered behaviour keeps it and this one becomes a pointer — the convention DL-099 states. This entry is the clearest instance of the defect that convention exists to catch: its withdrawal rationale rested on a column capacity that a later change to the entity mapping invalidated, so a row can be falsified by a decision recorded elsewhere and not only by a change to its own subject. (b) breaks a citation made in an earlier commit. (c) would put one subject in two rows. | A reader who reaches this identifier from an old commit finds a pointer rather than a mechanism and must follow it to DL-292. Neither of this row's earlier forms survives in the file; both are in version history. |
| DL-156 | A Notion response is validated before it is mapped. An absent body, a query response carrying no `results` array, a created page carrying no identifier and a matched page carrying no identifier are each reported under a fixed malformed-response code and raised as `IllegalStateException`; an empty `results` array is an empty result rather than a failure. A page is mapped only when its `Tweet Id` property carries text, the `Timestamp` offset is normalised to UTC before it is dropped, and an `Engagement` number that is fractional or outside the range of the like-count column is reported as absent. | (a) Treat each of those as success or as empty, which is what was delivered: an absent page identifier returned an empty string, an absent body returned an empty list, a page with no `Tweet Id` borrowed the Notion page UUID, an offset date-time silently lost its offset, and a number was narrowed with `intValue()`. (b) Validate the response but keep the page-UUID fallback. (c) Skip a malformed page rather than failing the whole query. | Each previous behaviour produced a value that looked legitimate: an empty page identifier reads as a page that exists, a Notion UUID reads as a post identifier and correlates with nothing, `2026-02-14T08:45+02:00` read back as `08:45` UTC — a two-hour error — and `3000000000` narrowed to a negative like count. A mirror that answers with plausible wrong data is worse than one that fails, because nothing downstream can detect it. (b) keeps the one fallback that manufactures an identifier. (c) is what is done for a page missing `Tweet Id`, which is a page this mirror did not write and cannot interpret; a body or a `results` array of the wrong shape is a different thing — it means the response is not the response this code was written against, and continuing past it would map arbitrary JSON. | A query whose response shape changes — a provider version bump under DL-094, for instance — now fails loudly instead of returning an empty list. Pages written by anything other than this mirror are skipped rather than partially mapped, and the count of skipped pages is one record per query, so which pages were skipped is not recoverable from the log. UTC normalisation means a timestamp read back may differ from the local time originally written, which is the correct instant rather than the original wall clock. |
| DL-157 | `updateTweetResponse` tests its documented no-op — a `null` or blank tweet identifier — before it reads `scanner.notion.database-id`. | (a) Read the configuration first, as delivered before this entry. (b) Read the configuration first but downgrade its absence to a warning on this path. | The method documents that a blank identifier leaves Notion untouched and raises nothing; with the configuration read first, an unconfigured mirror threw `IllegalStateException` for a call that was going to do nothing anyway, so the no-op was unreachable exactly when it mattered least and most: on a deployment with no Notion configured at all. A locally raised patch release also decouples this service from the Spring Boot release train: the next parent upgrade would silently keep the local value if it were lower, or be overridden by it if it were higher, and neither is what a BOM-managed coordinate is for. (b) Require the claims but throw instead of returning empty, letting `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` classify the failure. (c) Require `exp` alone and accept an absent `iat` or `sub`. (d) Restrict the accepted algorithm through a jjwt parser option rather than a header comparison. (a) leaves a real hole: a correctly signed token carrying only `sub` never expires, so a token that leaks — or one minted before a lifetime reduction — authenticates indefinitely, which is precisely the condition CWE-613 describes. It also lets a token with no `sub` reach `SecurityContextHolder` as a `null` principal. (b) would let an authentication concern reach the error-translation advice, which owns the eight approved wire literals and no 401 path (DL-078); returning empty keeps the single answer for every rejected token. (c) accepts a claim set this service never mints. (d) is not available in jjwt 0.13.0 as an allow-list, and `verifyWith(SecretKey)` already rejects an asymmetric `alg` and an unsigned token — but it does **not** reject `HS384` or `HS512` signed with the same secret, and comparing the header to the one algorithm `generateToken` uses closes that remainder in one line. The `exp`-later-than-`iat` requirement rejects a zero-lifetime token that jjwt would otherwise have reported only as expired. |

| DL-180 | `service/AnalyticsService.getSummary()` declares `@Transactional(readOnly = true, isolation = Isolation.REPEATABLE_READ)`, so the **three** aggregate statements it issues — one per table: `TweetRepository.findAggregates()`, `ResponseRepository.findApprovalCounts()` and `AiToolRepository.count()` — read one snapshot and every metric of the returned `SummaryDto` describes the same instant. Six of the seven metrics come from those three statements and `pending_responses` is arithmetic over two of them. `getTrends()` keeps the default isolation. This row owns the subject; DL-091, DL-125, DL-158 and DL-165 are pointers to it. | (a) Leave `getSummary()` at the default isolation, as it was. (b) Read every metric with its own derived query, which is what the method did before the two closed projections of DL-245 replaced five derived operations with two statements. (c) Compute `pending_responses` with its own `countByIsApprovedFalse()` query instead of by subtraction. (d) Clamp `pending_responses` at zero. (e) `SERIALIZABLE` instead of `REPEATABLE_READ`. (f) State the statement count as six, which this row and DL-091 both did while DL-158 stated three. | The summary carries a derived metric, `pending_responses = total_responses − approved_responses`, whose operands are read by the same projection but whose consistency with the tweet and tool counts still spans statements. Under PostgreSQL's default `READ COMMITTED` each statement takes its own snapshot, so a reply approved between two of them makes the record internally inconsistent — and before the projections landed, `approved_responses` could exceed the earlier `total_responses` and the difference go negative, an impossible value in a field a reviewer reads as a work queue. One snapshot removes the window for the whole record, and because the transaction is read-only it cannot fail on a serialization conflict. (b) is the state the projections replaced and would restore five statements where three suffice. (c) replaces the derivation the metric is defined as (DL-041) and changes its meaning for a row whose `is_approved` is null. (d) hides the inconsistency instead of removing it. (e) is stronger than needed and, on PostgreSQL, exposes a read-only transaction to serialization failures for no gain. (f) is the figure this row is corrected from: it described the pre-projection method and was left behind when DL-245 reduced the statement count, which is why three rows on one subject disagreed about it. All three supported vendors implement `REPEATABLE READ` — it is MySQL's default, and PostgreSQL and H2 2.x both offer it — so no vendor is excluded, and Spring's `HibernateJpaDialect` applies the level to the JDBC connection or raises `InvalidIsolationLevelException` loudly rather than ignoring it. | The summary transaction holds one snapshot for the duration of three statements, which on PostgreSQL keeps a slightly older row version visible to them; the cost is negligible at this scale and the reported instant is the start of the transaction rather than the start of each statement. A future metric added to the summary joins the same snapshot automatically, but a metric that needs a fourth table adds a statement and this row's count has to be re-stated with it. `service/AnalyticsServiceTest` asserts the declared annotation carries `readOnly` and `REPEATABLE_READ`, so removing it fails the build; the statement count itself is prose here and is guarded only by review. The level was verified at runtime against PostgreSQL 16 by reading `Connection.getTransactionIsolation()` inside such a transaction and by calling `getSummary()` against a live schema without an `InvalidIsolationLevelException`. |
| DL-181 | `api/GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` is a `@WebMvcTest` slice that drives every translated exception through the real `DispatcherServlet` with MockMvc against a nested probe handler, and keeps its thirty direct-invocation checks in the same file as supplemental tests. | (a) Keep only the direct-invocation checks, as the class was first written. (b) Add a seventh file in `api/` for the slice. (c) Use `@SpringBootTest` with a live server and `WebTestClient`. (d) Point the slice at a real controller such as `SettingController` and stub its service to throw. | The frozen test inventory lists this class in the `api/` group qualified as `@WebMvcTest` plus MockMvc, and direct construction cannot observe five properties the wire contract depends on: resolution of the exception by the `DispatcherServlet`, registration of the advice in the context, Jackson serialisation and the response `Content-Type`, the unmapped-path branch, and suppression of Spring's `ProblemDetail` and Boot's default error body. The last of these matters most: Boot's default body also carries an `error` member, so only an assertion that the object holds exactly one member distinguishes it from the two envelopes of `backend/app/main.py:L31-37`. A nested probe leaves the file count in `api/` unchanged, which is what (b) would have altered. (c) would require a port, a datasource and the security chain for a translation concern. (d) would couple these tests to a controller's service graph and still could not raise `NoHandlerFoundException` or an unparseable body. This was measured rather than assumed: with `@RestControllerAdvice` removed from the handler, exactly the 25 slice cases fail while all 30 direct cases still pass. | The file now loads a Spring context, so it is slower than a pure unit test and depends on `application-test.yml` binding `ScannerProperties`. The probe must declare `@Controller` because `RequestMappingHandlerMapping.isHandler` in Spring Framework 6.2 accepts that stereotype alone; `@TestComponent` keeps it out of the production component scan, and a guard test fails if it is ever registered twice. `controllers` is restricted to the probe, so a controller added later is not exercised by this file and needs its own slice. `addFilters = false` removes the security chain, so this file asserts nothing about the 401 entry point. |

(b) is the failure Rule 1 exists to prevent. (c) would give one identifier a decision it never made. | A reader who arrives here from an older comment must follow one pointer. Keyset paging assumes the identifier is monotonic, which it is for a generated key; DL-248 records that assumption and the per-pass batch cap that bounds a single pass. |
| DL-183 | `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` declares one `@Bean` of type `ErrorAttributes` — `errorEnvelopeAttributes()`, returning the private nested `ErrorEnvelopeAttributes extends DefaultErrorAttributes` — so the servlet `ERROR` dispatch renders the same single-key envelope the advice methods serve. Two earlier forms of this entry are withdrawn: one named a controller that no longer exists, and one asserted that no `ErrorAttributes` bean is declared at all — which was false of the delivered tree. | (a) Declare no `ErrorAttributes` bean and let Spring Boot's `DefaultErrorAttributes` answer the dispatch, which is what the immediately previous form of this entry asserted was delivered. (a) is what this row previously claimed and it is the shape the bean exists to prevent. (b) is the same mechanism in a different file and buys nothing once the advice is the only consumer; the class that held it is deleted. (c) and (d) both take over more of the error path than the parity clause needs — (c) displaces a framework component and (d) cannot express a body shape at all. (e) reshapes faults the retired stack also left to its own container, which Werkzeug did too, so it is hardening this migration does not admit. Declaring the bean beside the handlers keeps the two literals, the key name and both dispatch paths in one file. | The bean displaces `DefaultErrorAttributes` for the whole context, so any future consumer of the framework's richer attribute map — an error page, a diagnostic endpoint — sees at most one key. A dispatched status other than 404 or 500 renders an empty body rather than a documented shape, which is deliberate but is a body a caller cannot branch on. The mechanism is invisible to a reader of the advice methods alone, and it is asserted only by `ScannerApplicationTests`, so a change to the nested class that keeps the bean type intact would not fail the build elsewhere. |
| DL-184 | The two `scanner.jwt` comments in `src/main/resources/application.yml` state what the code enforces: the secret comment states that the value is the key material itself — its UTF-8 bytes, with no encoding applied or expected (DL-186) — names the 32-byte minimum those bytes must reach and the `openssl rand -base64 32` command that produces a value long enough to clear it, and records that an unset, blank or still-unresolved `SECRET_KEY` refuses to start, and the algorithm comment names `HS256` as the one accepted value in any letter case, states that `HS384`, `HS512`, `RS256` and `none` each refuse to start, and states that a blank value keeps `HS256`. Earlier they read "Minimum length: 32 bytes for HS256, 48 for HS384, 64 for HS512" and "Accepted values: HS256, HS384, HS512", and a later form described the value as an encoding decoded before signing. | (a) Leave the comments as they were. (b) Widen `security/JwtService` to accept `HS384` and `HS512` so the code matches the comment instead. (c) Delete both comments rather than correct them. (d) Move the accepted-value documentation out of the YAML into `docs/` and leave a pointer. (e) Reject an unsupported algorithm at binding time with a Bean Validation pattern on `ScannerProperties.Jwt` instead of describing it in a comment. | An operator configures this service exclusively through `application.yml` and environment variables, so these comments are the only documentation at the point of use — and following them produced a total startup failure: measured, `ALGORITHM=HS384` with a 48-byte secret and `ALGORITHM=HS512` with a 64-byte secret each exit with `IllegalStateException: scanner.jwt.algorithm is set to 'HS384'; this service supports HS256 only.` (a) leaves that trap in place. (b) is the tempting inversion and is wrong: DL-015 fixes HS256 as the single algorithm and algorithm agility is not in scope, so widening the code to rescue a comment would trade a documentation defect for a specification breach. (c) removes the trap but also the two facts an operator most needs, the key-length floor and the accepted value. | DL-185 | `security/JwtService`'s constructor treats a secret that is still an unresolved property placeholder as an unsupplied secret: after the existing `null` and blank tests, `isUnresolvedPlaceholder` trims the value and reports whether it opens with `${` and closes with `}`, and a match raises the same, unchanged `IllegalStateException` naming `scanner.jwt.secret` and the `SECRET_KEY` environment variable. (b) Match a tighter grammar, for example `^\$\{[A-Za-z0-9_.-]+(:.*)?\}$`. (c) Reject the placeholder shape during binding with a Bean Validation constraint on `ScannerProperties.Jwt.secret`. (d) Replace the ignore-unresolvable placeholder resolver with a strict `PropertySourcesPlaceholdersResolver` so binding itself fails. (e) Give `scanner.jwt.secret` a development default so the unset case cannot arise. (f) Raise a new, distinct exception message for the placeholder case. | `application.yml` declares `secret: ${SECRET_KEY}` with no default, and the `@ConfigurationProperties` binder resolves placeholders through a resolver that leaves an unresolvable one in place rather than failing, so an unset `SECRET_KEY` binds the literal thirteen-character text `${SECRET_KEY}` — neither `null` nor blank, and therefore past the guard written for exactly this case. Measured before the change: `env -u SECRET_KEY` exited on `io.jsonwebtoken.security.WeakKeyException: The specified key byte array is 104 bits`, and `grep -c 'scanner.jwt.secret is not configured'` over that run returned `0` while the same grep over a `SECRET_KEY=''` run returned `1`. 104 bits is 13 bytes, which is exactly `len("${SECRET_KEY}")`. First, the diagnostic misdirects: an operator is told the key is too weak when the real fault is a variable they never set. (c) and (d) are the architecturally cleaner locations and are rejected for the same reason: they change the failure from this service's own actionable message to a binding or validation report, and (d) alters placeholder resolution for every property in the application to fix one. (e) is forbidden by DL-016 — the secret has no default. A future required property with no format check would reproduce this class of defect and would need its own guard. |

(a) and (b) reject a perfectly good passphrase, and they rejected the value the project's own documented local-run block supplies: a 33-character hyphenated ASCII passphrase, which is 33 bytes of key material and is not a well-formed encoding in either alphabet, so the documented startup command failed. (d) changes the key derivation, so a token minted before the change cannot be verified after it. (e) makes every restart invalidate every issued token and hides a missing configuration value, which DL-016 rejects. | Any 32-byte string is accepted, so a low-entropy passphrase of sufficient length is accepted as readily as a random key — the length check bounds size and not entropy, and operator discipline carries the rest. | DL-187 | The `mariadb` scheme **translates onto the MySQL JDBC vendor**: `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` maps it, with any `+driver` suffix stripped and in any letter case, to `jdbc:mysql://`, so `SUPPORTED_SCHEMES`, which renders the key set of `VENDOR_BY_SCHEME`, advertises `postgresql, postgres, mysql, mariadb, h2`.
