The agreed scope is the frozen contract for this migration, so an implementation that rejects a scheme the agreed mapping covers is a specification breach, however well-motivated — which is the finding that produced this revision. (a) is therefore withdrawn: it deviated from a mapping the agreed scheme table states in two places, and it did so on a measurement about a *server product* rather than about the *scheme*, which the translator cannot observe. That measurement stands and is worth keeping on the record: against a real MariaDB 11.8.8 server, a raw JDBC connection through Connector/J 9.7.0 succeeds — `getDatabaseProductName()` returns `MySQL` — while booting the packaged jar with `DATABASE_URL=mariadb://…` created the HikariCP pool, opened one connection and then raised `org.hibernate.HibernateException: Unable to determine Dialect without JDBC metadata`. The honest conclusion is that the exposure belongs to the *server*, not the scheme: a `mysql://` URL addressing a MariaDB server reaches exactly the same late failure and always did, so rejecting `mariadb://` closed one spelling of a hazard while leaving the hazard itself, at the cost of a scheme the agreed mapping requires. (b) needs an eighteenth Maven coordinate: `backend/pom.xml` names seventeen — the parent and sixteen dependencies — and the only JDBC drivers among them are PostgreSQL, MySQL and H2. (c) is forbidden by DL-027, which records `spring.jpa.database-platform` as deliberately unset so Hibernate detects the dialect from connection metadata. (d) would leave an operator with no signal at all; a `WARN` at the translation point costs nothing, names the one combination that is unverified, and is a runtime diagnostic rather than rationale, so it belongs in the code while this reasoning stays here. | A `mariadb://` URL now produces a JDBC URL and a connection pool, and if the server behind it is a MariaDB server the refresh still fails at dialect resolution — later than a translation-time rejection would have, which is the cost of honouring the agreed mapping. The `WARN` is the only warning the translator emits, so an operator who suppresses `WARN` for this class loses it. The advertised scheme set is now wider than the verified server-product set, and the class Javadoc says so explicitly. If a MariaDB driver is ever added, this entry, the `Vendor` enum, `UNVERIFIED_SERVER_SCHEME`, the class Javadoc, `config/DatabaseUrlTranslatorTest` and DL-027's dialect-detection claim all have to move together. |
| DL-188 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a division of the `HttpMessageConversionException` hierarchy across three `@ExceptionHandler` methods by the direction of the failure — read, write and the abstract parent — each with its own status and literal, including a strict duplicate-key detection that reported a repeated JSON member as a distinct client fault. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** There is one rule and no hierarchy split: the catch-all answers an `HttpMessageConversionException` that is not an `HttpMessageNotWritableException` with **400 and no body**, and everything else with 500 and the source's 500 envelope. The strict duplicate-key detection is deleted. | (a) Keep the three handlers and the duplicate detector. (b) Keep two handlers, read and write. (c) One rule keyed on 'not a write failure', which is what is delivered. (d) Answer every conversion failure 500. | The wire-contract parity clause freezes the status codes and the error envelopes to what the retired Flask application served: only `@app.errorhandler(404)` and `(500)` produced JSON, so a framework-reported client failure came from Werkzeug's own page. 'The framework's own status with no envelope of ours' is therefore the parity behaviour, and every additional status literal is an invention. The one-line rule was reached by measuring what the container actually raises, and the measurement produced a result worth recording: a JSON body repeating the **last** component of a target record — `{"value":"a","value":"b"}` onto `dto/UpdateSettingRequest`, or a repeated last component of `dto/LoginRequest` or `dto/CreateResponseRequest` — raises a plain `HttpMessageConversionException` and not `HttpMessageNotReadableException`, while a repeated **non-last** component binds to its last value and reaches authentication normally. So the parent type is not an abstract case that never occurs, which is what a reading of the hierarchy would suggest; it is the type a duplicate key actually produces, and treating read-direction failures uniformly as 400 covers it without a third handler or a detector. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | A write-direction conversion failure — a response this service cannot serialise — is answered 500, which is correct, and every read-direction failure is answered 400 with no body, so a client cannot distinguish a malformed body from a duplicate key from an unbindable type. The source distinguished none of them either. |
| DL-189 | `security/SecurityConfig.configuredPasswordHash` reads a `scanner.auth.password-hash` that is still an unresolved property placeholder as an unsupplied hash: after the existing `null` and blank tests, a private `isUnresolvedPlaceholder` trims the value and reports whether it opens with `${` and closes with `}`, and a match raises the existing, unchanged `MISSING_PASSWORD_HASH_MESSAGE` naming the property and the `AUTH_PASSWORD_HASH` environment variable. The pattern and the helper are the same two-character bracket test `security/JwtService` already applies to `scanner.jwt.secret`. This supersedes the forward-looking note in DL-185 that left this property to its format check alone. | (a) Keep the guard testing `null` and blank only, which is the behaviour delivered before this entry, and rely on the bcrypt-shape check to reject the placeholder. (b) Reword `MALFORMED_PASSWORD_HASH_MESSAGE` so it also mentions the possibility that the variable was never set. (c) Extract the placeholder test into a shared utility class used by both `security/JwtService` and this class. (d) Declare a Bean Validation constraint on `ScannerProperties.Auth.passwordHash`. (e) Bind the property with `ignoreUnresolvablePlaceholders = false` so binding itself fails. (f) Delete `MISSING_PASSWORD_HASH_MESSAGE` as dead code and keep one message for every rejection. | DL-185 recorded that this property was left alone because its format check "fails closed with its own clear message", and measurement shows that reasoning was half right: booting with `AUTH_PASSWORD_HASH` unset does exit 1, so the service never starts with a default credential, but the message it exits with is reached 7 times and is `does not carry a bcrypt hash of the expected form … Regenerate it with BCryptPasswordEncoder`, while `MISSING_PASSWORD_HASH_MESSAGE` is reached 0 times. The purpose-built message is therefore unreachable for the one failure a container deployment hits most often, and the message that is reached misdirects: it tells an operator to regenerate a hash when the actual fault is a variable they never set, and it names a remedy that cannot help. That is precisely the misdirection DL-185 and DL-186 spent code to remove from `scanner.jwt.secret`, and DL-186 already named this class of defect as the pattern to apply wherever a mandatory key is validated, so applying it here makes two sibling validators behave identically rather than introducing a new idea. (a) leaves an unreachable branch in the tree and the wrong diagnostic on the wire to the log. (b) would put two unrelated causes in one message and still leave the dedicated message dead. (c) is the cleaner factoring and is rejected only because the tree has no shared utility package for security configuration and creating one to hold a single two-line predicate costs more than the duplication; the pattern text is identical in both classes and each is pinned by its own test. (d) names a property path but not the environment variable that supplies it, which is the part an operator needs. (e) changes placeholder resolution for every key in the tree, including the twenty-one carrying deliberate defaults and the three nested Twitter aliases DL-031 depends on. (f) would discard the more accurate of the two messages. | A genuine bcrypt hash whose text opens with `${` and closes with `}` is now refused before the shape check — unreachable in practice, since the bcrypt modular-crypt form begins with `$2`. A value that merely contains `${…}`, or that opens or closes with only one of the two, is unaffected and reaches the shape check as before. The placeholder pattern is now written down in two classes, so a change to the accepted shape has to be made in both; nothing detects a divergence automatically beyond each class's own tests. `scanner.database-url` is the third mandatory property with no default and is already covered by `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` under DL-186, so after this entry every mandatory key in the tree carries the guard and DL-185's forward-looking caveat is discharged. |
| DL-190 | `service/NotionService` carries no request pacing and no cache, and exactly one of its operations retries. The mirror write of `updateTweetResponse` re-attempts an HTTP 429, any 5xx and a transport failure up to `scanner.notion.mirror-max-retries` times — default 2, so three attempts — waiting `scanner.notion.mirror-retry-backoff-millis` before the first retry (default 500 ms) and doubling that wait once per earlier retry up to the thirty-second ceiling `NotionService.MAXIMUM_RETRY_BACKOFF_MILLIS` declares; the doubling loop is overflow-safe and an interrupt during a wait restores the interrupt and abandons the mirror. That retry is recorded separately as DL-253. Every other operation — the page create, the database query and the page-id lookup — issues exactly one HTTP request. A rejection, including HTTP 429 `rate_limited` against Notion's published request ceiling, is recorded once under DL-084 with the provider status, error `code`, request id and the length of the provider explanation rather than its text (DL-269), then raised to the caller as an `IllegalStateException`. Re-attempting a rejected read or create remains the orchestration layer's responsibility. The relational row is committed before any Notion call, so a rejected mirror never costs data. | (a) Add bounded retry with exponential backoff around each Notion call. (b) Add a request-pacing gate — a permit bucket or a fixed inter-request delay — sized to Notion's published ceiling. (c) Cap the scheduler's per-pass candidate batch so a single pass cannot exceed the ceiling. (d) Queue failed mirrors durably for later retry. (e) Cache the page-id lookup so the second request per candidate is avoided. (f) Add jitter to the retry wait so concurrent replicas do not re-attempt in lockstep. | (a) is now done, but only where a retry closes a real hole rather than adding a mechanism: the mirror write is the one call whose failure the orchestration layer cannot re-select, because the reply row it mirrors already exists and therefore leaves the candidate set on the pass that created it. Bounding that retry by attempt count and by a wait ceiling keeps it a fixed, auditable cost rather than a retry framework, which is what the file-level contract for this class excludes. The remaining calls are left single-attempt deliberately: a rejected create or read leaves the row inside the candidate set, so the next fixed-delay pass re-attempts it at the interval the operator already configures, contributed by the component that owns the candidate set. (b) and (c) are new rate limiting and a batch cap, both out of scope for this migration; DL-182 records why the candidate query was unbounded and DL-248 records the bounded-batch drain that superseded it, neither of which is a provider-pacing gate. (d) is durable queue infrastructure this migration excludes outright. (e) is a caching layer the system has nowhere. (f) was weighed against the ownership gate of DL-250: only the elected owner runs the scheduler, so there is no lockstep fleet for jitter to de-synchronise, and adding a random source to a deterministic wait would make the retry harder to assert. | A backlog larger than Notion's per-second ceiling still produces 429s, and the mirror retry lengthens a single pass rather than shortening the backlog: three attempts at 500 ms and 1 s of waiting add up to 1.5 s of thread time per exhausted candidate, and the calling thread blocks during a wait. The retry budget is therefore deliberately small, and the pass itself is bounded by DL-248. Retries are not durable: a mirror that exhausts its budget is counted as unmirrored and never re-attempted, because the reply row it belongs to is outside the candidate set — the residual limit DL-253 records, and the reason the honest unmirrored counter exists. A rejected create or read is still only as recoverable as the orchestration layer's re-selection. An operator whose backlog exceeds the ceiling has two configuration levers: lengthen the delay, or clear the backlog by other means. Closing the pacing gap properly requires a change to the agreed scope, at which point the mechanism belongs in the scheduler rather than in this adapter, so that the pacing decision sits with the component that knows the batch size. |
| DL-191 | `service/NotionService.getTweets(int, String)` is retained although no production code path reaches it, and its `page_size` is clamped: a non-positive `limit` is replaced by the default and a value above 100 is read as 100, the largest page size the Notion query endpoint accepts. Both facts are stated in the method's Javadoc. | (a) Delete the method as dead code. (b) Retain it and send `limit` through unclamped. (c) Retain it and reject a `limit` above 100 with an exception. (d) Retain it and paginate internally, issuing successive requests until `limit` records are collected. | The method replaces `get_tweets` at `backend/app/services/notion_service.py:L32-53`, and the transformation mapping names it explicitly as one of the two operations this adapter ports, so deleting it would drop a mapped construct and leave a row of the traceability matrix unsatisfied — (a) trades a parity obligation for a tidiness gain. The source method had no caller either, so the absence of one here is itself faithful. Clamping rather than rejecting is what keeps the method usable at all: Notion answers a `page_size` above 100 with HTTP 400 `validation_error`, so under (b) the one caller a future feature adds would fail on a value that reads as perfectly reasonable, and the failure would name a limit no signature documents. (c) converts a satisfiable request into an error for a bound the API owns and the caller cannot see. (d) is pagination this adapter does not own — the endpoint returns a `next_cursor` and the parameter list already exposes `startCursor`, so the caller is the layer holding the loop. | A caller asking for more than 100 records silently receives at most 100 and must read `startCursor` from a subsequent call to obtain the rest; nothing in the return value signals that the request was clamped. The method remains uncovered by any production path, so its behaviour is asserted only by `service/NotionServiceTest`, and a defect reachable solely through a future caller would not be detected by exercising the running service. |
| DL-192 | Two narrowing conversions on the Notion read path are made explicit. `parseOffsetDateTime` normalises a parsed value with `withOffsetSameInstant(ZoneOffset.UTC)` before taking its local date-time, so a page whose timestamp carries a non-UTC offset yields the same instant the mirror recorded. `toTweetDto` narrows the `Engagement` number to `Integer` only after checking it: a non-finite value, a value outside `int` range and a value carrying a fractional part are each recorded at `WARN` and read as `null` rather than narrowed. | (a) Take the local date-time of the parsed value directly and call `intValue()` on the number, which is what both paths did before this entry. (b) Change `TweetDto.createdAt` to `OffsetDateTime` so no normalisation is needed. (c) Store the timestamp as a string and let the caller parse it. (d) Clamp an out-of-range engagement to `Integer.MAX_VALUE` instead of reading it as `null`. (e) Round a fractional engagement to the nearest integer. | Both defects are silent by construction, which is what makes them worth code. Discarding the offset shifts the instant by the offset's magnitude — a page written with `+02:00` read back two hours early — and `task/TweetStreamListener` already normalises to UTC before storing, so the two paths disagreed about the same column. `Double.intValue()` is a narrowing primitive conversion: it truncates a fractional value toward zero and, for a magnitude beyond `int` range, yields `Integer.MAX_VALUE` or `Integer.MIN_VALUE` — so a hand-edited Notion number of `3.9` read as `3` and one of `1e12` read as `2147483647`, each presented as a measured like count. Reading such a value as `null` is the same treatment DL-090 gives every other component the page does not properly carry, so the record stays honest about what is unknown. (b) changes a DTO shape the wire contract fixes. (c) moves parsing to every caller. (d) is precisely the substitution that made the fault invisible. (e) invents precision the mirror never had, and a like count is integral by definition. | The `WARN` names the property and the rejected magnitude but not the page, consistent with DL-090's aggregate reporting, so locating the offending page in a large database still means searching it. A timestamp whose text carries no offset is unaffected — it is read as a local date-time exactly as before — so the normalisation helps only values that do carry one. A page legitimately recording an engagement above `Integer.MAX_VALUE` is now unreadable rather than clamped; no post has such a like count, and the column the mirror copies is itself an `Integer`. |
| DL-193 | `config/RestClientConfig` reads the Notion API version from configuration instead of holding it as the only value: `resolveApiVersion` returns `scanner.notion.api-version` when it carries a value and otherwise falls back to the compiled default, recording the fallback at `WARN`. The same class validates its two timeout values as at least one second, and dereferences the `ScannerProperties.Notion` group through a single null-guarded accessor that every reader in the class shares. | (a) Keep `Notion-Version` compiled in and leave `scanner.notion.api-version` unread, which is what the class did before this entry. (b) Delete the property from `ScannerProperties` and `application.yml` so the declared key matches the hardcoded behaviour. (c) Read the property with no fallback, so an unset value fails startup. (d) Repeat the null check at each of the three read sites rather than sharing one accessor. (e) Leave the timeouts unvalidated. | A declared configuration key that nothing reads is worse than no key at all: an operator who sets `NOTION_API_VERSION` to move the pinned version observes no change and has no way to discover why, and Notion's version header is precisely the value an operator needs to move without a redeploy when the provider advances its API. (b) is the coherent alternative and is excluded by configuration completeness, which requires every referenced environment key to be declared — the key exists because the configuration surface is itself a delivered requirement, so the fix is to read it rather than to withdraw it. (c) would make a key that has always had a working compiled default into a startup requirement, which changes the clean-checkout contract for a value that is safe to default; the `WARN` keeps the fallback visible instead. (d) is the shape that produced the inconsistency being closed — two of the three readers guarded the group and one did not, so a null group was a `NullPointerException` on one path and a clean default on another. The timeout check exists because a value of zero is not a shorter timeout but an unlimited one in the underlying request factory, so an operator setting `0` to mean "fast" would disable the bound entirely; rejecting it at bean creation names the property and the offending value while the context is still refreshing. | The pinned version now depends on configuration, so an operator can set a version Notion does not recognise and every request then fails at the provider with a message this class does not interpret; the value reaches the header only after the character check of DL-289, which rejects it when it carries a control character, and is not otherwise interpreted. The compiled default must still be advanced by hand when the provider's supported window moves, and nothing detects that it has gone stale. The `WARN` fires on every startup where the key is unset, which is the common case, so it is informational noise for a deployment that is content with the default. |
| DL-194 | Both orchestration paths mirror the reply they generated. A mirror write is re-attempted within the bounded retry budget DL-253 owns, which lives inside `service/NotionService` and re-attempts only the provider call; neither path re-attempts a mirror that budget exhausted, and no path re-attempts the persisted response. The two statements this entry once appeared to make at once are therefore one statement about two different subjects, and the alternatives below say so: what `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` is forbidden is a retry policy of its own over the persisted response, not the adapter's bounded re-attempt of a single HTTP call. `task/TweetStreamListener.mirrorGeneratedResponse` calls `NotionService.updateTweetResponse` after a successful generation, which is the step `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler.generateAndMirror` already performed, so a reply reaches the Notion `Response` property by whichever path produced it. The delivered records are two, at two levels. `service/NotionService` writes the single `ERROR` record, and the operation text it names carries the **tweet identifier only** — `Mirroring the generated response for tweet <id> to Notion` — together with the provider status, the Notion `code`, the request id and the length of the provider explanation (DL-084, DL-269). `task/TweetStreamListener.mirrorGeneratedResponse` writes the caller-side record at `DEBUG`, and that one names both the response identifier and the tweet identifier; a generation that yielded no response at all is recorded there at `WARN`. The stored reply is left in place either way, and the scheduler reports the count of replies stored without a mirror in its end-of-pass summary. | (a) Leave the listener path without a mirror step, which is what it did before this entry. (b) Persist a mirror-pending marker on the `responses` or `tweets` row and reconcile it on a later pass. (c) Wrap the mirror in a bounded retry with backoff. (d) Widen the scheduler's candidate predicate so a reply whose mirror is incomplete is selected again. (e) Move the mirror inside the transaction that stores the reply so the two commit together. (f) Add a reconciliation pass or dead-letter store. | Two facts decide this. First, the listener's omission was unambiguous and total: because generation creates a `responses` row, `TweetRepository.findByResponsesIsEmpty()` can never re-select the tweet, so the scheduler's mirror step was permanently unreachable for every ingested post and the Notion `Response` property stayed empty forever. `documentation/Code Structure.md` documents "Update Notion database with response" as a step of the generation flow, not of one particular trigger, so performing it on only one of the two paths was the deviation. Adding the call uses a collaborator the listener already injects and a method already approved for the scheduler, so it introduces no new dependency, no new type and no new configuration. Second, the recovery half cannot be closed within this specification, which is why it is accepted here rather than fixed: (b) requires a column, and the migration fixes the schema at four tables and twenty columns with no additions; (c) and (f) would put a retry policy in the scheduler, and the file-level contract for `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` forbids "a retry policy, rate limiter, cache, batch-size cap or pagination of the candidate list" and states that a Notion failure "must never undo or retry the persisted response" — which is why the bounded re-attempt sits in the adapter under DL-253, where it re-attempts one HTTP call and never the persisted row; (d) requires a repository member, and the file-level contract for `repository/TweetRepository` closes it at "exactly four methods plus one nested projection interface" and forbids adding an existence check; (e) would place an external HTTP call inside a database transaction, which both task contracts forbid explicitly. What is available without a mechanism was done: the failure is attributable to a specific tweet row from the `ERROR` record alone, and to the stored reply as well once `DEBUG` is enabled for `com.codeskeptic.scanner`, which is the input an operator needs to re-drive it; DL-084 names the provider error code alongside it. An earlier form of this row claimed one `ERROR` record naming both identifiers, which no delivered call site writes. | The mirror can still diverge permanently, and after this entry it does so only for a reply whose mirror was actually rejected rather than for every ingested post. Nothing in the database records that a reply is unmirrored, so the record of it exists only in the log — an operator who does not read the `ERROR` line or the pass summary has no way to discover the divergence, and there is no query that lists un-mirrored replies. Tying that `ERROR` line to the reply it belongs to needs the `DEBUG` record beside it, because the adapter names the tweet row and not the reply. Re-driving one by hand means calling Notion outside this application, because `POST /responses` generates a new reply rather than re-mirroring an existing one. Closing this properly requires a change to the agreed scope to admit a mirror-state column, at which point the predicate in the scheduler and the closed method set of `repository/TweetRepository` both have to move with it. |
| DL-195 | Duplicate background response rows are guarded by `service/ResponseService.generateResponseIfAbsent(String)`, the single operation called by both `task/TweetStreamListener` and `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler`. The cross-replica existence check runs twice — once before the paid provider call, with no database connection held across it, and again inside the storing transaction — which DL-252 owns. The raw identifier is parsed before ownership is attempted, and one per-instance `ConcurrentHashMap` key set claims the resulting `Integer`, so decimal aliases such as `7` and `007` share one claim. The subject is read and mapped, and the LLM call completes, with no service transaction open. A short `TransactionTemplate` unit then locks the parent through `TweetRepository.findByIdForUpdate`, checks `ResponseRepository.existsByTweetId` while holding that lock, and inserts only when no reply exists. The claim is released in a `finally`. The parent lock is also used by the route storage helper, but `POST /responses` deliberately skips the existence check and keeps the source's unconditional behaviour. | (a) Claim the raw path string and perform an unlocked `findById` before the existence check, which is what the delivered implementation did. (b) Hold one database transaction and the parent-row lock across the LLM call. (c) Add a unique constraint on `responses.tweet_id`, or a claim column. (d) Add broker-based distributed ownership. (e) Remove the generation trigger from the listener so the scheduler is the only producer. (f) Have `POST /responses` return an existing reply instead of generating a second one. | (a) protects only one spelling inside one JVM: `7` and `007` are different strings for the same row, and two application instances do not share either set. More importantly, an existence check and insert without a shared database lock are not atomic under the schema the migration freezes, so two instances can both observe absence and both insert. Parsing first makes the in-process owner match the repository identity. Locking the already-existing parent row makes the database identity the cross-instance mutex: a second storage transaction waits, then performs its existence check after the first commit and stores nothing. The lock begins only after the provider has answered, preserving DL-086's no-transaction external-call seam and avoiding a pooled connection or row lock during unbounded provider latency. (b) closes the wasted-call window but holds scarce database resources across an external request. (c) is a schema change the frozen schema forbids. (d) is infrastructure this migration excludes. (e) contradicts the listener contract. (f) changes the observable route behaviour endpoint parity freezes. | The per-instance claim cannot prevent two JVMs from both spending an LLM call; the parent lock prevents the second **row**, not the second provider charge. Storage for the same tweet serialises at the parent row and can wait or fail under a database lock timeout, although no provider call is inside that interval. The guarantee remains code rather than a unique constraint, so every background producer must keep delegating to this operation and must take the lock before checking absence. A direct database writer can still create duplicates because the frozen schema has no uniqueness rule. |
| DL-196 | `POST /auth/token` accepts an unlimited number of attempts. No throttle, no attempt counter, no lockout and no CAPTCHA is applied, on that route or anywhere else, and the route stays `permitAll` as DL-019 declares. One wait is applied and it is not a limiter: `api/AuthController.VERIFICATION_WAIT_MILLIS` is 250, the bound the controller waits for a credential verification already in progress rather than starting a second one, so it does not lengthen with the attempt count, does not accumulate across attempts and bounds nothing an attacker controls. An earlier revision of this entry stated that no delay is applied anywhere, which the delivered controller contradicts. | (a) Add a per-address or per-principal rate limiter to the token route. (b) Add failed-attempt counting with a temporary lockout. (c) Add a fixed delay to every rejected attempt. (d) Put the throttle in front of the service, in the ingress or the API gateway, rather than in the application. (e) Reduce the exposure by lengthening the required credential instead of limiting attempts. | New rate limiting is out of scope for this migration explicitly and without qualification, alongside caching, autoscaling changes, new indexes and new constraints, so (a), (b) and (c) are each excluded by an instruction rather than by preference — and (b) additionally needs a store for attempt state, which means either a fifth table (the schema is frozen) or in-memory state that no specification declares. The retired implementation could not be a source of parity here either way, since it had no token route at all (`backend/app/main.py:L26-29` registers four blueprints and none of them is an auth blueprint) and its `create_access_token` had zero call sites, so there is no throttling behaviour to port. (d) is the correct place for this control and is out of scope for a different reason: it is an infrastructure change, and this migration holds `infrastructure/terraform/**` structurally unchanged and confines the operations edits to the backend build and image. (e) does not limit attempts and would change the credential contract. Recording the gap is what the explainability rule requires when a known exposure is left open. | A single shared credential (DL-020) behind an unthrottled endpoint is brute-forceable at whatever rate the deployment will serve, and the only defences in place are the strength of `scanner.auth.password-hash` and the cost of BCrypt, which does bound the attempt rate per CPU but is not a limiter. There is no lockout, so a failed campaign leaves no state and no signal beyond log records; the successful issuance is attributable in the log but the failures are not aggregated or alerted on anywhere. This is the highest-severity accepted exposure in the service and closing it needs either a change to the agreed scope or an ingress-level control configured outside this repository. |
| DL-197 | One logging policy governs the whole tree, in three parts, each stated as what the delivered code does. **(1) Free text a caller or a provider controls passes the printable-ASCII guard DL-119 defines before it reaches a record, and is otherwise not written.** Every character outside printable ASCII is replaced and the value is cut to 64 characters, so a record boundary cannot be forged and a record cannot be flooded. Where a provider publishes a machine-readable field, that field is written under a shape check instead — `service/NotionService` admits a `code` matching `[a-z_]{1,64}` and an `x-request-id` matching `[A-Za-z0-9-]{1,64}` — and its `message` member is guarded. `service/LlmService` guards the `type`, `code` and `param` members of a rejection. No stack trace, no request URI, no response body and no request-body excerpt is written on these paths; a failure is named by its class simple name alone (DL-119). **(2) An identifier is written either as itself or as a correlation token.** A business identifier the service assigned — a tweet or response key — is written through `logSafe`; an external resource value is written as the keyed truncated HMAC-SHA-256 token of DL-119 that is stable within one process and unrelated across processes — DL-119. **(3) One failure has one owning record.** The class nearest the failure records it at `ERROR` or `WARN` and a caller that adds business context records at `DEBUG`, so raising `com.codeskeptic.scanner` to `DEBUG` joins the two. **(4) No record anywhere in the tree carries a `Throwable` argument.** The catch-all `api/GlobalExceptionHandler.handleUnexpectedException` writes, at `ERROR`, the bounded cause chain and the originating frame it renders itself under the type-only and frame renderings DL-119 defines — a class, a method and a line, in preference one under `com.codeskeptic.scanner` — and a correlation token over those two, so two occurrences of one defect share a token; it writes no message, no cause text and no stack. The sanitized failure detail DL-119 defines — up to five links, each message guarded and cut to 256 characters, plus up to ten frames — is written at `DEBUG` only, behind a level an operator raises deliberately and which no deployment carries by default. That is the whole diagnostic mode: there is no second switch and no configuration key for it. | (a) Attach the throwable everywhere, which is the most diagnostic option and is what `service/SentimentAnalysisService` did. (b) Attach it nowhere, which is what DL-084 implied for every seam. (c) Log the provider or library message raw. (d) Log an unguarded full detail at `DEBUG` and the guarded form at `ERROR`. (e) Keep both the owning and the calling record at `ERROR` and accept the duplication. (f) Introduce MDC or a correlation-id filter so records can be joined without logging values. (g) Salt the correlation token per run so it cannot be compared across runs or dictionary-attacked. (h) Keep `log.error(message, throwable)` on the catch-all path alone, which is what the revision before this one delivered. (i) Return the correlation token to the client in the 500 body so a report can be tied to a record. (j) Gate the detail on a new `scanner.*` diagnostic property instead of the logging level. (k) Write the whole stack at `DEBUG` by handing the `Throwable` to the logging framework. | The tree previously did several of these at once, and the inconsistency was itself the finding: a full stack on two 500 paths and at the Google seam, class names only at the OpenAI and Notion seams, a request-body excerpt at the ingestion seam, and a library message on the token-endpoint binding path — so no reader could tell what a record was allowed to contain. Splitting on **who controls the text** is the distinction that makes the policy statable in one sentence and checkable per call site. A single guard function rather than a per-site rule is what makes the check mechanical: a reviewer looks for `logSafe`, `correlation`, `type` or a shape check at every site that writes an outside value. (a) writes provider echoes of our own prompt. (b) leaves failures unnameable. (c) is the log-injection exposure the guard closes. (d) keeps the unguarded value in the file at a lower level, which is the same exposure behind a configuration switch. (e) duplicates every failure. (f) is a new capability the migration excludes. (g) is delivered, by the per-process key of DL-119. On the catch-all path: (h) was the remaining hole and is the finding a security review recorded as CWE-532 and CWE-117 — a JDBC, provider or serialization message reaches the file with the hostname, the query string, a payload fragment or an attacker-supplied newline it happens to carry, and the framework renders the whole cause chain with it, so a single 500 can publish more than every other record in the tree put together. Bounded metadata keeps what an operator actually acts on — what failed, where in our code, and whether it is the same failure as last time — and the type chain plus the originating frame are derived by this application from the class hierarchy and the stack, not copied from text a caller controls. (i) is refused outright: the 500 body is the byte-identical single-key envelope of `backend/app/main.py:L35-37` (DL-210), and adding a member changes the wire contract this migration freezes. (j) adds a configuration key for what a logging level already expresses, and an operator raising the level is the deliberate act the review asks for. (k) hands the framework a `Throwable` whose message can carry a newline, so the log-injection half of the finding would survive at `DEBUG`; rendering the detail through `logSafe` keeps the guard in force at every level. | The guard replaces characters rather than dropping the value, so a non-ASCII identifier reads as a run of `?` and cannot be recovered from the record. The correlation token is a keyed truncated HMAC: not reversible, not confirmable against a guessed value, and not comparable across processes — DL-119. A `DEBUG` demotion means an operator running at `INFO` sees the owning record but not the calling one, so the business identifiers behind a mirror failure need `DEBUG` on `com.codeskeptic.scanner` to recover. The policy is enforced by review and by the per-adapter guard tests, not by a build check, so a new call site can write an unguarded value and nothing fails. On the catch-all path the cost is real: an unexpected 500 no longer leaves a stack in the log at the default level, so a first diagnosis rests on the failure type, the originating frame and the correlation token, and reproducing the fault with `logging.level.com.codeskeptic.scanner=DEBUG` is the step that recovers the detail. The detail itself is bounded at five links and ten frames, so a deep chain or a deep stack is truncated even in diagnostic mode. `originFrame` reports the first frame under `com.codeskeptic.scanner`, which for a failure raised entirely inside a library is the frame that called into it rather than the one that raised — the type chain is what distinguishes those cases. |
| DL-198 | No correlation identifier ties an ingested post to the X post it came from, and no request-scoped correlation identifier ties the log records of one HTTP request together. the keyed-digest rendering of DL-119 supplies a token derived from a value where one is needed to group repeated events, and that is the whole of the mechanism: there is no MDC, no filter, no `X-Request-Id` propagation and no stored external identifier. | (a) Store the X post identifier in a new column and log it. (b) Add a `OncePerRequestFilter` that puts a generated identifier in the MDC and a `logback-spring.xml` pattern that renders it. (c) Accept and propagate an inbound `X-Request-Id` header. (d) Overload the X post identifier onto `tweets.id`. (e) Log the surrogate key alone, which is what the tree does. | The ingestion half is closed by decisions that are already frozen: DL-049 keeps `tweets.id` a generated surrogate and declares no natural-key column, and the migration fixes the schema at four tables and twenty columns, so (a) needs a schema change and (d) would change the column's semantics and risk generator collisions (DL-049). The request half is closed by scope: (b) requires the `logback-spring.xml` that DL-052 deliberately omits, plus MDC plumbing that is the kind of observability stack this migration must not invent — the instruction is a logging baseline, explicitly not an audit or correlation facility. (c) additionally trusts a caller-supplied header, which would have to be validated and bounded before it could be logged at all. (e) is therefore what remains, and it is sufficient for the one join an operator actually performs: every record about a stored post names the surrogate key, which is the key the `tweets` and `responses` tables are related by. | An operator cannot go from a log record to the post on X, or from a post on X to its stored row, without matching on content — the mirror in Notion carries the surrogate key rather than an X identifier too (DL-088). Records belonging to one HTTP request can be grouped only by thread name and timestamp, which is unreliable under concurrency; the ingestion path additionally hands work to a `boundedElastic` thread, so a single record's processing is not even confined to one thread name. Both halves are single, localised changes if the agreed scope ever admits them. |
| DL-199 | `com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.JsonNode` is the payload type at the ingestion boundary: `task/TweetStreamClient` parses each stream line into one and hands it to `task/TweetStreamListener.onStatus(JsonNode)`, which reads the fields it needs and constructs `entity/Tweet`. No record or class models a stream payload, and `JsonNode` reaches no layer below the listener. | (a) Declare a stream-payload record in `dto/` and bind the line to it. (b) Pass the raw line as a `String` and parse it inside the listener. (c) Pass a `Map<String, Object>`. (d) Bind to `TweetDto` directly. | The `dto/` package is closed at the sixteen records the frozen inventory enumerates and none of them models a stream payload, so (a) requires a file the frozen inventory's inventory does not declare — the same closure that makes `JsonNode` the boundary type also makes it the only permitted one. `JsonNode` is not a vendor SDK type in the sense the adapter rule guards against: Jackson is already the serialisation library of the whole web layer, arriving through `spring-boot-starter-web`, so it crosses no dependency boundary that the DTO records do not also cross. It is also what makes the listener directly unit-testable from a hand-built `ObjectMapper().readTree(...)` fixture, which the file's own contract requires. (b) moves parsing and its failure handling into the listener and duplicates the reassembly the client already owns. (c) loses the type information the readers depend on, so every field access becomes an unchecked cast. (d) would put the popularity gate and the doubt-rating call after DTO construction, and `TweetDto` carries components the stream does not supply. | A tree-model payload is read positionally by field name at each call site rather than validated once against a schema, so a change in the X payload shape surfaces as a field reading `null` rather than as a binding failure; the type-correct readers (DL-197's shape discipline, applied as `isTextual` and `isIntegralNumber`) are what keep a wrong-typed field from being coerced instead. The listener's signature names a Jackson type in its public API, so a future move to a different JSON library would touch it. |
| DL-200 | `scanner.openai.temperature` carries the source's literal **0.7** as its default — declared on the record component and in both YAML profiles — and `service/LlmService` adds `.temperature(...)` to a request whenever the value is present. The component stays a nullable `Double`, so a deployment that sets the key to a blank value omits the parameter altogether. | (a) Declare no default and send nothing unless a deployment opts in, which is what an earlier revision of this entry established. (b) Keep the property defaulted to `0.7` but omit the parameter when it equals the default. (c) Send `1.0`, the provider default. (d) Delete the property. (e) Pin the model to one that accepts `temperature` under any reasoning effort. | The migration requires the source's `temperature=0.7` (`backend/app/services/llm_service.py:L25`) to be preserved as a configuration default, and the runtime check that found this entry's earlier form reported the absent parameter as a parity failure against that requirement. The incompatibility the earlier form was avoiding is real but conditional: the configured GPT-5.x model refuses an explicit `temperature` only while its reasoning effort is above `none`, so setting `scanner.openai.reasoning-effort` to `none` (DL-145) removes the incompatibility instead of trading parity away for it. Keeping the component nullable preserves the escape hatch the earlier form valued: an operator running a model that refuses the parameter under every effort sets `OPENAI_TEMPERATURE` to a blank value and the request carries no temperature, with the key still declared as configuration completeness requires. (b) hides the mechanism behind an equality test and still sends the parameter for any explicitly set value. (c) abandons the source's value for no gain. (d) removes a declared key the source read. (e) contradicts DL-033, which holds the model identifier in configuration because OpenAI retires models in dated waves. | Sampling behaviour now depends on two properties that must stay consistent: an operator who raises `scanner.openai.reasoning-effort` above `none` without blanking `scanner.openai.temperature` reintroduces an HTTP 400 on every generation, reported with the provider's own `code` and `param` (DL-084, DL-203). The value is range-checked to 0…2 (DL-201) but its acceptability remains the provider's judgement, and no test can pin it because the constraint lives at the provider. |
| DL-201 | Every `scanner.openai.*` value is range-checked where it is used, and the failure names the property. `maxCompletionTokens` and `n` must be at least 1, `requestTimeoutSeconds` at least 1, `maxRetries` not negative, and `temperature`, when present, within 0..2. `reasoningEffort` reaches the request as `ReasoningEffort.of(value.trim())` when non-blank and is omitted when blank. | (a) Leave the values unchecked, which is what the tree did. (b) Declare Bean Validation constraints on the `Openai` record and let binding report them. (c) Clamp an out-of-range value to the nearest accepted one. (d) Check them once at bean creation rather than at each use. | An unchecked `scanner.openai.request-timeout-seconds` of `0` is the sharpest case: the underlying HTTP client reads zero as *no timeout*, so a value an operator would set meaning "fail fast" instead removes the bound entirely and a stalled provider call holds a scheduler thread indefinitely — the scheduler pool holds two threads (DL-251), so a stalled call occupies one of them for as long as the socket stays open. `n` or `maxCompletionTokens` at 0 produces a provider rejection whose message names neither the property nor the file it came from. (b) is the cleaner location and is excluded by validation parity, which limits Bean Validation to the required-versus-optional parity the Pydantic schemas declare and forbids adding range constraints to the validated surface; a guard in the code that consumes the value is not part of that surface. (c) hides the operator's mistake and picks a value nobody configured. (d) is where the checks effectively run anyway, since the client is built once, and stating them at the point of use keeps each check beside the parameter it protects. | The guards are plain conditional checks rather than declared constraints, so they do not appear in the generated configuration metadata an IDE reads, and an operator sees the failure at startup rather than while editing. A value that is in range but nonsensical for the configured model still reaches the provider. |
| DL-202 | `scanner.openai.max-completion-tokens` defaults to **150**, the source's own value (`backend/app/services/llm_service.py:L22`), and `firstChoiceContent` records the response's `finish_reason` at `WARN` on the one branch that accepts content, whenever that reason is other than `stop`. An earlier revision of this entry raised the default to 1000. | (a) Keep 1000, which this entry previously declared. (b) Omit the parameter and accept the provider default. (c) Set it far higher, for example 4096. (d) Keep 150 and treat a truncated reply as a failure without recording why. (e) Log the `finish_reason` on the three no-usable-content branches instead, which is what this row previously described. | The 1000-token default existed for one reason: for a reasoning model the completion budget is consumed by reasoning tokens before any visible text is produced, so a 150-token ceiling was spent before the reply began and the response returned `finish_reason` `length` with empty content, which `firstChoiceContent` reports as a generation failure (DL-083). Setting `scanner.openai.reasoning-effort` to `none` (DL-145) removes that consumption entirely, so the whole 150-token budget reaches visible output and the source's value — which this migration requires as a transcribed configuration default — is once again both faithful and workable. 150 tokens is ample for one reply to one post, whose own medium bounds a post at a few hundred characters. (a) is a documented deviation from an explicit stated requirement and was reported as such by a runtime check. (b) makes the ceiling invisible and provider-defined. (c) raises the cost per call with no benefit for a single-reply prompt. (d) leaves an operator with a failure and no way to tell a starved budget from a filtered response — which is exactly what the `finish_reason` in the record now distinguishes. (e) is where this row was wrong rather than merely imprecise: the branch that matters is the one that succeeds, because a truncated reply is *accepted* under DL-083 and DL-178, so the reason has to be recorded beside the success or it is never recorded at all; the three failing branches carry a fixed code and a fixed description, which name the state without needing the provider's token. | A reply longer than 150 tokens is truncated, exactly as the source's replies were, and is stored: the truncation surfaces as `finish_reason` `length` in a `WARN` beside a 201, not as a failure and not as an unexplained empty draft, so an operator who reads only failures never sees it. The budget is only sufficient while reasoning effort stays `none`: an operator who raises the effort without also raising this value will see every generation report an unusable reply, which is the coupling DL-145 records. |
| DL-203 | `service/LlmService` catches `OpenAIServiceException` at the adapter boundary, writes the provider's HTTP status together with its `type`, `code` and `param` members — each through the guard of DL-197 — and rethrows unchanged. The response body is never written. The post text is interpolated into the source template between the single quotes that template carries, verbatim and unbounded (DL-035), and **no trust-boundary instruction, no delimiter block and no system message separates the post text from the instruction**, so those single quotes are punctuation of the ported template and not a prompt fence. | (a) Let the SDK exception propagate uncaught, which is what the tree did — the operator saw only an exception class name from the caller. (b) Log the provider response body. (c) Wrap the rejection in a local exception type. (d) Cut the post text to 1,000 characters and fold its line breaks, which an earlier revision of this entry described as delivered. (e) Escape or strip the post text. (f) Add a real trust boundary: a system message that names the post text as untrusted data, a delimiter block the text is escaped out of, or an instruction-hierarchy prompt. | A rejected OpenAI request is the failure an operator will actually meet — a retired model, a revoked key, an unsupported parameter, an exhausted quota — and every one arrives as the same status; the provider publishes the distinguishing detail in `type`, `code` and `param`, which are enumerated tokens rather than content. (b) writes the provider's echo of our prompt, which carries the post text. (c) would discard fields the caller may want. (d) is withdrawn: the tree never truncated or folded the body, the constant that named the bound was never read, and the delivered tests assert an untruncated, unfolded interpolation — DL-035 owns the interpolation contract and records why verbatim is the faithful port. (e) would alter the post text the source interpolated verbatim. (f) is the change that would actually reduce prompt-injection exposure and is **not** delivered: the migration boundary preserves the source prompt template verbatim, and a system message or a delimiter grammar is a new prompt design rather than a port. | The post text reaches the model inside the instruction with no trust boundary and with no length bound, so a post can address the model directly and influence the generated reply, and a long post produces a long request. The only structural protection is that **no generated reply is ever published**: every one is stored with `is_approved` false and waits for a human, and no code path in the tree publishes to X. The completion side is bounded by `scanner.openai.max-completion-tokens` and each call by `scanner.openai.request-timeout-seconds`. |

| DL-206 | `src/main/resources/application.yml` declares `logging.level.com.codeskeptic.scanner: ${SCANNER_LOG_LEVEL:INFO}`. (b) Set the application logger to `DEBUG` by default. (c) Add a `logback-spring.xml` with an appender and pattern. (d) Set levels per class rather than for the package. | Several diagnostics the tree emits are load-bearing and only reachable at `DEBUG` — the per-record decisions DL-197 demotes, the skipped-payload categories, the rejected-token sentences of DL-111 — and with no key naming the application logger an operator had nothing to raise: they had to raise the root level and take every framework logger with it. | DL-207 | `task/TweetStreamClient` treats a stream body that ends without a stop having been requested as a condition to reconnect from, not as completion. `consumeStream` composes the dispatched record sequence with `.then(Mono.defer(...))`, which reads `stopRequested`: an intentional stop completes cleanly with `Mono.empty()`, and every other end raises `IllegalStateException("The X filtered stream connection ended.")` into the `retryWhen` policy `ingestionCycle` declares. The end is therefore recorded by the reconnection `WARN` in `reconnectPolicy`, which names the attempt number, the applied delay and the failure's class simple name (DL-119); there is no separate completion handler. | (a) Let a completed body complete the pipeline, which is what the class did when first written. (b) Resubscribe from `doOnComplete` without routing through the retry. (c) Restart the client from a scheduled health check that observes `isRunning()`. (d) Treat completion as a stop and require an operator to restart the process. | The X filtered stream is a long-lived connection that the provider may close at any time — during a deployment on its side, at a rate-limit boundary, or on an idle timeout — and a closed connection arrives as a normal completion rather than as an error. With (a) that ended ingestion **permanently and silently**: the pipeline completed, the retry never saw a signal, nothing reconnected, and the only trace was a single `INFO` line, so a service that had been ingesting simply stopped and continued to report itself healthy. Converting completion into an error routes it through the backoff the class already declares, so a provider-side close is handled by the same reconnection path as a provider-side failure, with the same jitter and the same rate-limit handling — one mechanism rather than two. (b) would resubscribe with no backoff, turning a provider close into a tight reconnect loop. (c) adds a second scheduled task and a liveness protocol to compensate for a signal the pipeline already receives. (d) is the behaviour being fixed. | A deliberate provider-side termination is now indistinguishable from a fault, so the client reconnects when the provider may have intended it to stop; the backoff bounds the cost, and the reconnection `WARN` is written on every occurrence. That record names the failure's simple type under the policy of DL-197, so an ended connection appears as `IllegalStateException` rather than by its message — the type is shared with other internal faults, and it is the reconnection record rather than the type that identifies the condition. The retry is unbounded (`Long.MAX_VALUE` attempts), so a permanently withdrawn stream produces reconnection attempts at the maximum backoff interval indefinitely rather than exiting — the blank-credential guard and `stop()` remain the ways to end ingestion. |
| DL-208 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a retention argument for four `util/` classes the frozen file inventory does not name — `util/LogSafe`, `util/ConfiguredValues`, `util/QueryParameters` and `util/StreamRuleTerms` — on the ground that each held one definition several callers shared and that a shared definition is worth a file the inventory omits. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** All four classes are deleted. `util/` holds exactly `util/DelimitedStringListConverter.java`, which is the single class the inventory names. Each definition was re-homed into the component that owns the concern: the log-redaction renderings into their calling services, the unset-value test into `security/JwtService`, `security/SecurityConfig` and `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator`, the page-parameter handling into `service/TwitterService` and `service/ResponseService`, and the rule-term grammar into `task/TweetStreamClient` and `service/SettingsService`. | (a) Keep the four classes and argue the inventory is indicative. (b) Merge all four into `util/DelimitedStringListConverter`, the one authorised file. (c) Re-home each definition into its owning component, which is what is delivered. (d) Keep only `util/LogSafe`, the most widely shared of the four. | The frozen file inventory is a boundary and not a guideline: it names one class under `util/`, and four more is four unauthorised files however useful each is. The de-duplication this entry argued for is real, and it is paid for by repeating a small definition in the two or three components that need it, which is the cost the boundary chooses. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | One definition now exists in more than one place, so a change to the redaction rendering or to the unset-value test has to be made in each component that carries it, and a reader comparing two components sees the same few lines twice. | DL-209 | `ScannerApplication` carries exactly two annotations — `@SpringBootApplication` and `@ConfigurationPropertiesScan` — and a `main` that does nothing but `SpringApplication.run(ScannerApplication.class, args)`. (b) Declare `@ComponentScan` and `@EntityScan` explicitly rather than relying on the base-package default. (c) Use `@EnableConfigurationProperties(ScannerProperties.class)` instead of the scan. (d) Keep a second, module-level application object beside the factory, mirroring `main.py:L13`. (e) Move `main` into a separate `Launcher` class. | `@EnableScheduling` belongs on `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig` and on no other class, because that class also implements `SchedulingConfigurer` and its `configureTasks` is what registers the fixed-**delay** trigger DL-047 requires; declaring the annotation in two places would give a reader two candidate pacing sites to reconcile, and the pacing site was already the subject of three rival implementations, so keeping it single is what makes the delay contract locatable. (b) restates what the base package already yields and would need editing on every package addition. (c) names one class and would need editing for each further `@ConfigurationProperties` type, where the scan binds `ScannerProperties` wherever it sits. (d) is the source's own defect: `main.py` built a `Flask` object at `:L13` and a second one inside `create_app()`, so which object the routes and error handlers bound to was ambiguous — one context is the point of the port. (e) splits a two-line entry point across two files. | The class is the scan root, so a package added outside `com.codeskeptic.scanner` is silently not scanned and its beans never appear; the symptom is a missing-bean failure at startup rather than a compile error. Relying on the default scan means the scanned set is not visible from this file and a reader must know the convention. |
| DL-210 | `dto/ErrorResponse` is a single-component record serialising to `{"error": <string>}`, and it is the only error body type in the tree: the eleven error branches the source emitted, carrying ten distinct message literals between them, and all **six** `@ExceptionHandler` methods of `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` serialise through it — those six handle `NotFoundException`, `BadRequestException`, `ResponseGenerationException`, `MethodArgumentNotValidException`, the pair `NoHandlerFoundException`/`NoResourceFoundException`, and `Exception`.
