The servlet `ERROR` dispatch carries the same one key, rendered as a map rather than as this record, because the `ErrorAttributes` bean of DL-183 returns a map. | (a) A richer envelope carrying a code, a timestamp, a path or a field list. (b) Spring Boot's default `ErrorAttributes` body. (c) RFC 7807 `application/problem+json`. (d) A `Map<String, String>` assembled at each handler. (e) One record per status. | Wire-contract parity fixes the wire body at exactly `{"error": "Not found"}` and `{"error": "Internal server error"}`, and the frontend is out of scope, so any added key is a contract change no caller asked for and none can consume. (a) and (c) are the changes a reviewer is most likely to propose and both add keys the source never emitted; (c) additionally changes the content type. (b) emits `timestamp`, `status`, `error` and `path`, which is precisely the body the `ErrorAttributes` bean of `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` exists to replace on the `ERROR` dispatch (DL-183). (d) loses the single declaration point that makes the shape checkable and lets a mistyped key pass unnoticed. (e) multiplies types for one identical shape. | A caller receives no machine-readable discriminator, so it must branch on the HTTP status and, for branches that share a status, on the literal text — which is what makes those literals contract (DL-212). A field-level validation failure collapses to one sentence, so a client cannot mark the offending field; that is the source's behaviour and the wire contract freezes it. The handler count stated above is one a reader can check against the class, and `api/GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` asserts the handled-type set, so a handler added without a table row is visible rather than silent. |
| DL-211 | `service/ResponseService` is one singleton bean holding **six** collaborators in final fields — `ResponseRepository`, `TweetRepository`, `LlmService`, `ResponseMapper`, `TweetMapper` and a `TransactionTemplate` — each injected through the single constructor and null-checked there. Nothing is instantiated per request and no collaborator is reached statically. | (a) Instantiate collaborators inside each method, which is what the source's controllers did (`TwitterService()` at `backend/app/api/tweets.py:L14`). (b) Field injection with `@Autowired`. (c) Split the class so each operation carries fewer collaborators. (d) Annotate the methods `@Transactional` instead of holding a `TransactionTemplate`. | The injection rule replaces import-time and per-call construction with constructor injection, and the constructor is what makes the dependency set visible and the class testable without a context — `service/ResponseServiceTest` builds it from mocks directly. The `TransactionTemplate` is held rather than annotated because the generation path must issue the provider call **outside** any transaction and then open a short transaction to claim and insert (DL-195): a method-level `@Transactional` would hold a database connection across the OpenAI round trip, which is the seam DL-195 exists to keep open. (a) rebuilds a service per request and defeats pooling. (b) hides the dependency set from the constructor and needs reflection to populate in a test. (c) would scatter one aggregate's four call-site-dictated operations across classes to reduce a field count. | Six collaborators is a large constructor and a fair signal that this class does the most work of any service here; it is the count the four call sites at `api/responses.py:L15,L26,L44,L60` plus the background entry point require, and further growth should split the class rather than add a seventh. Explicit transaction handling is more code than an annotation and its boundaries are correct only by inspection — the tests that pin them are the guard. |
| DL-212 | The three types under `com.codeskeptic.scanner.exception` own the wire literals. Each declares its approved messages as `public static final String` constants, keeps its constructor private, and exposes one named static factory per reporting branch — `BadRequestException.tweetIdRequired()`, `updateDataRequired()` and `noValueProvided()`, the `NotFoundException` factories for the tweet, response and setting branches, and `ResponseGenerationException` for the single 500 literal. `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` copies `getMessage()` into `dto/ErrorResponse` and composes no text of its own. | (a) Throw `new NotFoundException("Tweet not found")` with the literal written at each call site. (b) Hold the literals as constants on the handler or on a shared `ErrorMessages` class. (c) Use Spring's `ResponseStatusException` with the reason inline. (d) Resolve the text from a `messages.properties` bundle. (e) Let the handler derive the text from the status. | The eight per-route literals are wire contract under wire-contract parity, so the risk to manage is a typo or a drift in one of eight strings spread across four controllers; a private constructor plus a named factory makes an unapproved literal impossible to construct, which is a compile-time guarantee where (a) and (c) offer only review. (b) puts the text one indirection away from the throw and leaves the constructor open. (d) introduces localisation the source never had and turns a frozen literal into a resource a translator could change. (e) cannot work: `Tweet not found` and `Response not found` share status 404, and `Tweet ID is required`, `Update data is required` and `No value provided` share 400, so the status does not determine the text. | The factory names are the API, so a new branch needs a new factory rather than a new string — deliberate friction. Because `getMessage()` reaches the wire, any instance of these types must carry only approved text; the private constructors enforce that for these three, and `handleUnexpectedException` answers the fixed `Internal server error` for everything else, so no third-party message can reach a caller. |
| DL-213 | The trend series is read by one JPQL query, `TweetRepository.findDailyTrendsBetween(LocalDateTime, LocalDateTime)`, whose two bounds close the window at the instant of the call as well as opening it (DL-247), and which buckets on `cast(t.createdAt as LocalDate)`, aggregates `count(t)`, `avg(t.doubtRating)` and `sum(t.likeCount)`, groups and orders by the same expression, and binds into the closed interface projection `TweetRepository.DailyTrend`. No native SQL, no vendor date function and no entity list reaches `service/AnalyticsService`. | (a) A native query using `date_trunc` (PostgreSQL) or `DATE()` (MySQL). (b) Load the window's `Tweet` rows and bucket them in Java. (c) A class-based DTO projection with a constructor expression. (d) A `Tuple` or `Object[]` result. (e) A database view. | The migration must not resolve the database vendor at compile time and DL-027 deliberately leaves the dialect to Hibernate's metadata detection, so (a) picks a vendor in source and would have to be written twice and selected at runtime; `cast(… as LocalDate)` is JPQL that Hibernate renders per dialect, which is the only bucketing expression satisfying both constraints. (b) transfers every row in the window to compute four numbers and grows without bound as `tweets` grows — the aggregation belongs where the rows are. (c) works but couples the query to a constructor signature, where a closed projection binds by select alias and lets each of the four accessors be documented individually. (d) returns positional untyped values, so every read becomes an unchecked cast. (e) is a schema object, and the migration freezes the schema at four tables. | `avg` and `sum` return provider-typed numbers, so `DailyTrend` declares them nullable and a bucket whose rows all carry `null` yields `null` — `service/AnalyticsService` is what substitutes for that, and the substitution is only correct because the accessors are nullable. Rows whose `created_at` is `null` fail the `>=` predicate and appear in no bucket, and a day with no rows produces no element, so the series is sparse rather than gap-filled; both are the source's behaviour by omission and are asserted by `service/AnalyticsServiceTest`. A `cast` used as a grouping key is not indexable, and this migration forbids adding an index, so scan cost grows with the window. |
| DL-214 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded two dedicated test classes for `task/TweetStreamClient`: `task/TweetStreamClientTest` driving the client through a stubbed `ExchangeFunction` and asserting the app-only token exchange, rule reconciliation, NDJSON reassembly across chunk boundaries and the `429` and `x-rate-limit-reset` handling, and `task/TweetStreamClientLifecycleTest` asserting the `SmartLifecycle` contract — `start`, `stop`, `isRunning`, idempotence, and that no blocking read happens on the caller's thread. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** Neither class exists. The frozen test inventory names nineteen classes and no test for this component, so both were deleted under DL-216. The behaviour the review of this round required to be covered is asserted from `ScannerApplicationTests`, which already resolves the client as a bean: that new intake is refused once a stop is requested and nothing is counted in flight, that a terminal callback from a replaced cycle writes no shared state, and that a usable `ai_tools` name behind a full rule cap of unusable rows is still collected. Each of the three was confirmed to fail when its fix is reverted. | (a) Keep both classes. (b) Keep one, merging the lifecycle cases into the transport cases. (c) Move the coverage into `ScannerApplicationTests`, which is what is delivered. (d) Move it into `task/TweetStreamListenerTest`, whose subject is the listener and not the client. | The frozen inventory declares this component no test class, and DL-216 records why an unauthorised one cannot stand. The coverage was moved rather than dropped because the findings this round is answering asked for it explicitly, and the authorised full-context class is the only place in the inventory where this bean is reachable — which makes the relocation the one option that satisfies both the boundary and the findings. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | The token exchange, the rule reconciliation requests and the NDJSON chunk reassembly are no longer covered by any test: a stubbed `ExchangeFunction` is the only practical way to assert them and the authorised classes provide none. That is a real coverage loss on the one integration this migration hand-rolled, and it is stated rather than implied. The relocated cases reach the client's private methods reflectively, which couples them to method names — an accepted cost of testing a component the inventory gives no home to. |
| DL-215 | The operations delivery is exactly four files, each held to the line-level carve-out defined for it, and nothing wider. `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` is rewritten at `L2`, `L8-11`, `L14`, `L20` and `L22-28`, keeping `EXPOSE 5000`. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` changes at `L16-19`, `L26-29`, `L35-39`, `L47-50` and `L56-59` — the JDK 21 toolchain step and the single `mvn -B clean verify` step with `working-directory: ./backend` — and nothing beside them: the toolchain action within `L16-19` is referenced by tag as `actions/setup-java@v4`, matching the bare-tag form every other action in the file uses, and the full-commit-SHA pin an earlier revision recorded is withdrawn (DL-103), and the `node-version` input is the mandatory Node.js floor rather than the retired `14` (DL-285). Every other line, the single `build-and-test` job and all five frontend commands included, is byte-identical to the source branch; no `permissions` key, no checkout input and no job split is added (DL-104). `.github/workflows/cd.yml` changes at `L36` alone, adding `-f infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` to `docker build -t $BACKEND_IMAGE ./backend` (DL-056). `scripts/deploy.sh` changes at `L18` alone, `npm run build` becoming `mvn clean package` (DL-053). No fifth file is delivered: no `.yamllint.yml` and no `.github/dependabot.yml` (DL-276). The two container-build mechanisms therefore still differ — `cd.yml` builds and pushes with Docker while `deploy.sh` keeps `gcloud builds submit --tag gcr.io/code-skeptic-scanner/backend` — and that asymmetry is inherited rather than chosen. No test asserts these boundaries: `OperationsContractTest` was deleted because the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary does not declare it (DL-216), so the four operations files are held to this carve-out by review against the source branch rather than by an assertion in the build. The two claims that test was said to make are in any case withdrawn: there is no 40-hex pinned toolchain action and no Node.js floor. | (a) Migrate `deploy.sh` to the same `docker build`/`docker push` pair `cd.yml` uses, so one mechanism serves both. (b) Delete the `gcloud builds submit` line as redundant. (c) Point `gcloud builds submit` at the Dockerfile through `--config` or a `cloudbuild.yaml`. (d) Migrate `cd.yml` to `gcloud builds submit` instead. (e) Keep the hardened CD workflow an earlier revision delivered — Artifact Registry, Secret Manager verification, an explicit container port, `--no-cpu-throttling`, a single-instance floor and ceiling, a digest deploy, a frontend artifact handoff and a post-deploy probe — and keep the CI workflow split into three jobs. | The agreed carve-out confines `deploy.sh` to its backend-build line — "Line 18 only … Nothing else changes" — and `cd.yml` to its build line, and `gcloud builds submit` is a separate line implementing a different mechanism, so (a) and (b) exceed the carve-out however sensible convergence would be. (c) is a second and larger edit to a protected file. (d) would rewrite the authorised workflow to match an unauthorised one. (e) is what an earlier revision delivered and what a review recorded as a critical breach: it altered the deployment architecture and, in setting a minimum and maximum instance count, changed the very autoscaling the plan names as leave-alone. Each of its parts is defensible engineering, which is exactly why the boundary has to be the plan and not the merit; every one of them remains available to whoever widens the carve-out. A submit that finds no Dockerfile in its directory is a pre-existing defect of the same family as the undeclared `var.backend_image` this migration leaves broken on purpose. | `scripts/deploy.sh` does not build a working image, because `gcloud builds submit` runs from `backend/`, which holds no Dockerfile — a developer following the script meets a failure at that line while `cd.yml` succeeds. Continuous deployment does not fire at all, because the CD trigger names a workflow that does not exist (DL-102). The deployed revision binds no `DATABASE_URL`, `SECRET_KEY` or `AUTH_PASSWORD_HASH`, so it cannot start, and no probe reports that; deployment relies on a long-lived service-account key (DL-104) and on a mutable image reference (DL-105). Every one of those is a property of the source-branch operations files, retained deliberately, and every one of them is one authorised line away from repair. A reader comparing `cd.yml` with `deploy.sh` will assume one of them is wrong; this row is the reconciliation. |
| DL-216 | The delivered test inventory is **nineteen** classes — exactly the nineteen the frozen test inventory names, with no additions. `ScannerApplicationTests`; `api/{TweetControllerTest, ResponseControllerTest, SettingControllerTest, AnalyticsControllerTest, AuthControllerTest, GlobalExceptionHandlerTest}`; `service/{SentimentAnalysisServiceTest, TwitterServiceTest, NotionServiceTest, LlmServiceTest, ResponseServiceTest, SettingsServiceTest, AnalyticsServiceTest}`; `task/{ResponseGenerationSchedulerTest, TweetStreamListenerTest}`; `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest`; `security/JwtServiceTest`; and `config/DatabaseUrlTranslatorTest`. An earlier revision recorded forty-six — the nineteen plus twenty-seven additions — and all twenty-seven are deleted: the six documentation and contract guards (`BuildDependencyContractTest`, `DecisionLogCitationTest`, `DecisionPointerIntegrityTest`, `DocumentationConsistencyTest`, `OperationsContractTest`, `TraceabilityCoverageTest`), `api/AuthControllerAdviceTest`, nine `config/` classes, three `security/` classes, `service/SettingsServiceSeedingIntegrationTest`, three `task/` classes and four `util/` classes. | (a) Keep the forty-six and argue the frozen inventory is a floor rather than a ceiling. (b) Keep only the six documentation guards, which enforce Rule 1 mechanically. (c) Fold every addition's coverage into the nineteen authorised classes, which is what is delivered. (d) Delete the additions and their coverage with them. | The frozen inventory is a boundary in both directions: it names nineteen classes, and twenty-seven more is twenty-seven unauthorised files however valuable each is. (b) was the most tempting, because those six are the only mechanical enforcement Rule 1 ever had — and it is refused for the same reason as the rest, with the consequence stated below rather than hidden. (d) was refused: where an addition covered behaviour the authorised classes did not, the coverage was moved rather than dropped, which is why `ScannerApplicationTests` now carries the stream client's shutdown-intake, cycle-generation and rule-composition cases and `NotionServiceTest` carries the item-split boundary sweep. | Rule 1 is now enforced by review alone. The six guards asserted that every row populates four columns, that every `DL-nnn` cited from code resolves, that pointer rows are enumerated, that the traceability matrix carries no rationale, and that the dependency and operations surfaces match this log — and none of that is checked by the build any more, so a future change can contradict this file without failing `mvn verify`. That is the largest single loss in this round and it is recorded plainly. `task/TweetStreamClient` has no dedicated test class, which DL-214 covers. |
| DL-217 | `GET /tweets` and `GET /responses` bind `page` and `per_page` as **optional text** — `@RequestParam(name = …, required = false) String` — and convert through a private static `intOrDefault(String, int)` that `api/TweetController` and `api/ResponseController` each declare: absent, blank or unconvertible text yields the default, 1 for `page` and 10 for `per_page`, and any successfully parsed integer passes through unchanged. Neither service caps `per_page`, and no `MAXIMUM_PER_PAGE` constant exists anywhere in the tree. Degenerate parsed values are normalised inside the services before a `Pageable` is built. The shared `util/QueryParameters` class that held this conversion at an earlier revision is not in the delivered tree; DL-208 owns its withdrawal, and the conversion is now declared once per controller. | (a) Bind primitive `int` parameters and let Spring MVC convert. (b) Cap `per_page` at a maximum such as 100. (c) Reject an unconvertible value with a new 400 branch. (d) Pass the parsed value straight to `PageRequest` without normalising. (e) Declare the conversion privately in each controller, which is what is delivered. (z) Keep the conversion in one shared `util/` class, which is the form DL-208 withdrew. | The source is `request.args.get(name, default, type=int)` (`backend/app/api/tweets.py:L12-13`), which falls back to the default on conversion failure and otherwise forwards the parsed integer with no range policy — so endpoint parity fixes both the defaults and the absence of a range policy. (a) is the defect this contract replaces: a primitive `int` binding answers HTTP 400 for `page=abc`, where the source answers 200 with page 1, and it was found in two independently written controllers. (b) invents a cap the source never had, which is validation improvement rather than parity; a cap was proposed, implemented and withdrawn for that reason. (c) adds a status the route never emitted. (d) is what the code did and it produced HTTP 500 where the source answers 200 — `PageRequest.of` rejects a negative index, and `page = Integer.MIN_VALUE` overflowed the `page - 1` conversion to the maximum index; both were live faults in `service/ResponseService` and `service/TwitterService`. (e) is delivered: the two declarations are private, static and six lines each, and the drift that an earlier revision attributed to duplication came from two controllers converting by different rules, not from the conversion living in two places. (z) is excluded by the module inventory of the migration boundary, which declares `util` as holding one class; the conversion is six lines and is declared twice, so the duplication is visible at both call sites and neither controller depends on the other. | An uncapped `per_page` lets one request ask for an arbitrarily large page, so database and heap work is bounded only by the table size; that is the source's exposure and adding a bound would be a contract change (DL-038 records the wire-shape half of the pagination envelope). Normalising rather than rejecting means a caller sending `page=0` receives the first page and no signal that its value was adjusted, which is the source's behaviour. The conversion is a private method and not a framework binding, so a new route must call it explicitly. The conversion now exists twice, so a change to the fallback contract has to be made in both controllers; the controller tests assert the 1 and 10 defaults and the unconvertible-text fallback on both routes, so a change made to one alone fails the build. |
| DL-218 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded two runtime facets of the container image: a `HEALTHCHECK` polling `http://127.0.0.1:${PORT:-5000}/tweets` every 30 seconds after a 60-second start period, treating any answer as liveness, and a heap ceiling of 75 per cent of container-visible memory set through `-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0` in a `sh -c` entrypoint. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** Neither is in the image. `docker image inspect` reports `Healthcheck=none` and `Cmd=[java -jar app.jar]` — the exec form, with no shell wrapper and no JVM flag. | (a) Keep both. (b) Keep the heap ceiling and drop the probe. (c) Keep the probe and drop the heap ceiling. (d) Neither, which is what is delivered. | AAP section 0.4.1.8 states the Dockerfile carve-out line by line — the base images, the removal of the `requirements.txt` copy and the `pip install`, the two source copies, `EXPOSE 5000` unchanged, and `java -jar` in place of `CMD ["python","app.py"]` — and nothing else. Section 0.2.3 excludes new hardening capabilities by name. Every addition beyond that translation is therefore outside the boundary however sound it is in isolation. The health probe and the heap ceiling are both operational tuning, and section 0.2.3 excludes autoscaling and related operational capabilities by name. Cloud Run performs its own liveness check against the container port, so the probe was duplicating a platform facility. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | The JVM takes its own default heap sizing, which on a small container is a fraction of the available memory, and no in-image probe reports liveness. Cloud Run's own port check covers the second; the first is a real efficiency loss on a memory-constrained revision and is the argument for (b) if the carve-out is ever widened. |
| DL-219 | `service/NotionService.toTweetDto` returns `null`, and `readTweets` counts the page, reports the aggregate once at `WARN` and leaves it out of the returned list, when a mirrored Notion page carries no value for one of the five mirrored properties `dto/TweetDto` declares required — `Content` for `content`, `Engagement` for `likeCount`, `Timestamp` for `createdAt`, `Doubt Rating` for `doubtRating` and `Author` for `userId`. This extends to the required-value case the skip that DL-090 already established for a page carrying no identifier at all; DL-090's `Tweet Id`-to-page-identifier fallback is applied first, so a page is skipped for a missing identifier only when both are absent. No default is substituted and no exception is raised, and the aggregate `WARN` names both causes in one message. | (a) Substitute a neutral value for the absent property — `0` for the engagement, `0.0` for the doubt rating, the empty string for the content or the author, the epoch for the timestamp. (b) Let the record's own rejection escape, so one incomplete page fails the whole read from the adapter boundary. (c) Keep the records permissive and serialise `null`, the contract the withdrawn form of DL-139 authorised. (d) Report each skipped page individually at `WARN` rather than once in aggregate. (e) Add a second counter and a second message so an incomplete page is reported separately from an unidentifiable one. | DL-080 makes those five components non-null on the wire record, so an incomplete page has no wire form and the only open question is which of skip, substitute and raise applies to it. DL-090 already answered that question for this read path and rules out both of the other two: it forbids substitution because a page that never carried a measurement must not read back as though it carried zero — the fault DL-075 records for a null aggregate — and it forbids raising because a mirror that is a secondary store must not let one malformed page take down a read of the pages that are well formed. This entry therefore applies DL-090's existing policy to the case the record contract newly creates, and applies it through the skip counter DL-089 and DL-090 already put in `readTweets` rather than adding a second mechanism. (a) is what DL-090 forbids. (b) inverts the mirror's secondary status, which integration parity fixes. (c) is ruled out by the record-mapping rule, as DL-080 records. (d) turns one malformed export into an unbounded number of log lines, against the aggregate-once discipline DL-089 set for this same loop. (e) reports a distinction no caller can act on differently, since the remedy for both is to repair the export. | A mirrored page missing any of the five properties disappears from `getTweets` with nothing but the aggregate `WARN` to show for it, so a caller cannot distinguish a partial mirror from a small one without reading the log; the count in the message is what keeps that detectable. `NotionService.getTweets` has no production caller (DL-191), so the reachable effect today is confined to `service/NotionServiceTest`; the rule is in place so a future caller inherits it rather than meeting the record's rejection at runtime. The write path is unaffected, because `storeTweet` mirrors from a `TweetDto` that has already been constructed and therefore cannot present an incomplete page. |
| DL-220 | Every record the X filtered stream delivers is handed to `task/TweetStreamListener` on `Schedulers.boundedElastic()`: `task/TweetStreamClient.consumeStream` composes `publishOn(Schedulers.boundedElastic())` between the record source and the dispatch step, so the listener's JDBC write, its Google Natural Language call, its OpenAI call and its two Notion mirror writes all execute on a blocking-capable worker rather than on the reactor-netty thread that emits the response body. | (a) Leave dispatch on the emitting thread, which is what the class did: `subscribeOn` in `start()` and in `runOneConnection` moves only the subscription and the rule composition, so once the body arrives every record was handled on a Netty event loop. (b) `concatMap(record -> Mono.fromCallable(() -> dispatchRecord(record)).subscribeOn(Schedulers.boundedElastic()))`. (c) `flatMap` with a concurrency of one. (d) A dedicated single-thread scheduler owned by this class. (e) Keep dispatch where it is and make each collaborator non-blocking. | A Netty event loop thread carries Reactor's non-blocking marker, and any code that blocks on it is either refused outright or starves the loop. Under (a) both consequences were measured: the Notion adapter's synchronous `RestClient` call was refused with `block()/blockFirst()/blockLast() are blocking, which is not supported in thread reactor-http-epoll-*`, so **no ingested post reached the Notion database at all** and the reply mirror never issued its `PATCH`; and two records delivered in one response body were processed 3.5 s apart against a 3 s model answer, because the loop that also carries the stream connection was held for the whole provider call. `boundedElastic` is the scheduler Reactor documents for blocking work and the one already named for this class. (b) and (c) are equally correct on the thread question; `publishOn` was chosen because it keeps one worker for the connection's lifetime instead of taking one per record, keeps arrival order without an ordering operator, and leaves the operator's prefetch queue between the socket and the listener so the connection thread can keep reading while a record is being handled. (d) adds a lifecycle to own for a pool the framework already bounds. (e) would rewrite four adapters, two of which wrap synchronous vendor clients, to fix a defect that lives in one operator. | The prefetch queue holds up to Reactor's small-buffer size of records while the listener is slow, so a stall now shows as a bounded queue and then as TCP back-pressure rather than as an immediately stalled loop; records are held in memory for that interval and are lost if the process dies, exactly as they were before. Dispatch remains strictly sequential, so a slow provider still delays the next record — bounded now by the queue rather than by the loop. One `boundedElastic` worker is occupied for the lifetime of each connection. |
| DL-221 | The Notion transport names its request factory: `config/RestClientConfig` builds `ClientHttpRequestFactoryBuilder.jdk()` with the configured connect and read timeouts, instead of `ClientHttpRequestFactoryBuilder.detect()`. | (a) `detect()`, which is what the class did. (b) `httpComponents()`. (c) `simple()`. (d) `reactor()` named explicitly. (e) Leave the factory detected and rely on callers never being on a non-blocking thread. | `detect()` resolves against the classpath, and because `spring-boot-starter-webflux` is a declared dependency for the X streaming path it resolved to `ReactorClientHttpRequestFactory` — a factory that implements a synchronous call by blocking on a reactive one. That made the adapter's behaviour depend on the caller's thread: identical calls succeeded from the scheduler's thread and were refused from a reactive thread. The JDK factory is backed by `java.net.http.HttpClient`, is part of the platform on Java 21, adds no dependency, and blocks on its own without Reactor's marker, so a Notion call behaves the same from any thread. (b) is unavailable: only Apache HttpClient 4.5.14 is on the classpath, transitively through `google-cloud-language`, and that builder requires HttpClient 5. (c) is `HttpURLConnection`, whose `PATCH` support needs a workaround, and `PATCH /v1/pages/{id}` is one of the three calls this transport carries. (d) makes the thread coupling explicit but keeps it. (e) is what DL-220 fixes for the ingestion path, but it leaves the adapter thread-sensitive for every future caller; the two changes are independent and both are kept. | The transport implementation is now pinned by this application rather than tracking what the framework detects, so a future move to a different client is a code change here. The JDK client negotiates HTTP/2 where the server offers it and falls back to HTTP/1.1 otherwise, and it maintains its own connection pool with its own defaults; neither is configured by this application. The two configured timeouts are the only bounds applied. |
| DL-222 | `task/TweetStreamClient` bounds the bytes it accumulates for one newline-delimited record at `MAX_RECORD_BYTES` = 1 MiB, held as a code constant. A record that reaches the bound is discarded: the accumulator is emptied, one `WARN` naming the byte count and the bound is recorded, and the bytes up to and including that record's next line feed are dropped, after which accumulation resumes and the following record is delivered normally. | (a) No bound, which is what the class did. (b) Fail the connection so the reconnect path handles it. (c) Emit the truncated prefix as a record and let the JSON parse fail. (d) Make the bound a configuration property. (e) Bound the codec buffer instead, through `maxInMemorySize`. | The body is consumed as raw `DataBuffer`s, so no codec limit applies, and the accumulator grew for as long as bytes arrived without a line feed: 4 MB was buffered with no record delivered, no log record and no failure, and nothing capped the growth, so a provider or intermediary that never emits a line feed grows the heap without limit. 1 MiB is three orders of magnitude above a post of the filtered stream carrying the requested fields, and it keeps the 300 000-character record the suite already exercises working unchanged. Discarding and skipping to the next line feed matches the policy this class already applies to an unreadable record — log and skip, never end the connection — so one oversized record costs one record rather than a reconnection cycle. (b) turns a single malformed record into a reconnection with backoff, which is the behaviour the malformed-record path deliberately avoids. (c) hands the parser a prefix that cannot be valid JSON and reports it as an unreadable record, which misnames the condition. (d) adds a key to `application.yml`, which is closed to new keys, for a bound no deployment has a reason to tune. (e) does not apply to a raw-buffer body and would also change how every other X response is read. | A genuine record larger than 1 MiB would be dropped rather than delivered; the filtered stream does not produce one, and the `WARN` names the byte count so the condition is visible if it ever occurs. The bound is not operator-tunable without a code change. The discard is per record, so a body that never emits a line feed is dropped 1 MiB at a time with one `WARN` per megabyte rather than accumulating. |
| DL-223 | `task/TweetStreamListener.onStatus` requires every member a stored column declares required to carry its wire type before any row is written, and applies one rule to all of them: `data.text`, `data.public_metrics.like_count`, `data.created_at` and `data.author_id` are read first, a missing or wrong-typed member is named at `WARN`, the record is skipped and `true` is returned to continue streaming. No neutral value is substituted for any of them, and the guard sits before the sentiment call so no provider call is spent on a record that cannot be stored. `readCreatedAt` therefore returns `null` for an absent, blank, non-textual or unparseable value rather than a substitute. | (a) Store the row and leave the column empty, which is what the listener did for `author_id` and `created_at`. (b) Substitute `LocalDateTime.now(ZoneOffset.UTC)` for an absent or unusable `created_at` and store the row, reporting the substitution at `WARN`. (c) Give `service/mapper/TweetMapper` a documented substitute for an empty column, for example the `ABSENT_TEXT` constant it declares. (d) Coerce a numeric `author_id` to text so fewer records lack one. (e) Add `nullable = false` to the columns so the database refuses the row. | `dto/TweetDto` declares `content`, `like_count`, `created_at`, `doubt_rating` and `user_id` required (DL-080), so under (a) the listener knowingly stored a row no downstream step could use: the Notion mirror failed inside the mapper before any request was issued, response generation failed for the same reason, and because the scheduler selects candidates by the absence of a response the row was retried on every pass indefinitely. Enforcing the requirement at the listener — the only writer of new `tweets` rows — keeps the wire contract, the mapper and the API exactly as they are. One rule for all four members is preferred over (b) because a substituted `created_at` is indistinguishable in the table from a delivered one and silently shifts the day bucket `service/AnalyticsService.getTrends` groups on, so it trades an unrenderable row for a wrong number; X sends `created_at` whenever it is requested, which this client always does, so the record being declined is one the provider should not have delivered. (c) and (d) would put a fabricated author or timestamp on the wire. (e) is a schema change, which this migration forbids. | A record the provider delivers incomplete is dropped rather than stored in a degraded form, so an operator reading only `INFO` and above sees the `WARN` naming the members but has no row to inspect afterwards; the count of skipped records is not aggregated anywhere. Rows written with an empty `user_id` or `created_at` by earlier builds remain unrenderable, and no migration mechanism is available under the no-schema-change mandate of the frozen schema — an accepted limitation. |
| DL-224 | `task/TweetStreamListener.mirrorToNotion` reports at two levels: a failure raised while `service/mapper/TweetMapper` prepares the wire form is recorded here at `WARN`, and a failure raised by `service/NotionService.storeTweet` stays at `DEBUG`. The preparation step is guarded separately from the adapter call. | (a) One `try` around both steps at `DEBUG`, which is what the method did. (b) One `try` around both steps at `WARN` or `ERROR`. (c) Rethrow a preparation failure. (d) Move the preparation outside the guard so a failure ends `onStatus`. | DL-197 places a provider failure's record with the adapter that owns it, which is why the adapter call is logged at `DEBUG` here: `service/NotionService` already reports it at `ERROR` with the provider's status, code and request id. A preparation failure has no such owner — it is raised before any adapter is reached — so under (a) the only record of a mirror that never happened was a `DEBUG` line, invisible under the shipped `SCANNER_LOG_LEVEL` of `INFO`. Splitting the guard reports each failure exactly once, at the level that matches whether it is reported anywhere else. (b) would duplicate every adapter failure at `WARN` beside the adapter's own `ERROR`. (c) and (d) would let a mirror failure stop the response-generation trigger, contradicting the secondary-mirror rule that a failed mirror leaves the stored row in place and does not stop the trigger. | The mirror path now emits records at two levels, so an operator reading only `WARN` and above sees preparation failures but not adapter failures, and must read the adapter's own `ERROR` for the latter — which is where the provider detail lives. With DL-223 in place a preparation failure is not reachable from ingestion at all, so the `WARN` guards against a future contract drift rather than naming an expected condition. |
| DL-225 | `service/TwitterService.getPaginatedTweets` and `service/ResponseService.getPaginatedResponses` answer every `page` value, including one naming a page whose first row lies at an offset beyond `Integer.MAX_VALUE` — the largest offset `org.springframework.data.jpa.support.PageableUtils` lets a paged query express. Each carries its own private `withinQueryableOffset(Pageable)` predicate — `service/TwitterService` and `service/ResponseService` alike, the shared `util/QueryParameters` class that once held it being withdrawn under DL-208 — which reads the offset from the `Pageable` itself so no product of two `int` values can overflow, and beyond the ceiling both serve `new PageImpl<>(List.of(), requested, repository.count())`: Spring Data's own counters then build the envelope, restating the requested `page` and `per_page` and carrying the true `total` and `total_pages`, exactly as every other page beyond the last row already reports. No page size is reduced, no `per_page` maximum is introduced and no pagination argument is answered with an error status. | (a) Leave the arithmetic to Spring Data, which raises `InvalidDataAccessApiUsageException` ("Page offset exceeds Integer.MAX_VALUE") for such a request — the delivered behaviour. (b) Reject the request with 400 and a new error literal. (c) Clamp the page number to the last populated page and serve that page's rows. (d) Introduce a `per_page` maximum so the product cannot overflow. (e) Handle `InvalidDataAccessApiUsageException` in `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` and translate it there. | The route already has a defined answer for a page that holds no row, and it is used for every other out-of-range value: `page=0`, `page=-5`, `page=101` and `page=1000000` all answer 200 with an empty list and a populated block. (a) made one arithmetic band — `(page - 1) x per_page > 2147483647`, reached from a single query parameter on both vendors — the only page value answered 500, and the inconsistency is the defect: nothing about that page differs from `page=1000000` except the size of the number. It is also a cheap amplifier, since one such request writes a stack trace roughly twelve times the size of a successful response's log record for about the same latency. (b) invents a status and a literal the source route never had, which endpoint parity and wire-contract parity close. (c) would report rows for a page that was not asked for, so a client walking pages would silently receive the last page forever. (d) is forbidden: this migration excludes adding a `per_page` maximum, and the unbounded size is parity-preserved (DL-123, DL-200). (e) would place pagination arithmetic in the error path and answer 200 from an advice that exists to answer failures, and it would still let the query be built and rejected. | The clamped path issues `count()` where the repository page would have issued a count and a select, so a request in this band costs one statement rather than none — the trade for a correct `total` and `total_pages`. The empty list is indistinguishable by the client from a page that is genuinely empty, which is the existing behaviour for every other beyond-last page. `total_pages` is computed here rather than read from a `Page`, so the two must be kept in step if Spring Data's rounding ever changes; both are integer divisions of the same two values today, and the tests assert them against `Math.ceil` of the same quotient. |
| DL-226 | `service/ResponseService` declares the generate-if-absent operation as **two distinctly named methods, not two overloads of one name**: `generateResponseIfAbsent(String)` for a caller that holds only the raw identifier, and `generateResponseIfAbsentFor(Tweet)` for a caller that already holds the row. Both order the pre-provider existence read of DL-252 ahead of the model call. Both reach one private body, which takes the in-process claim, resolves the subject — from the supplied row when there is one and from `TweetRepository.findById` when there is not — calls the generator and stores the row under the same transaction-scoped `existsByTweetId` guard. `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` passes the row `TweetRepository.findByResponsesIsEmpty()` selected; `task/TweetStreamListener` keeps passing the identifier. | (a) Keep the identifier-only signature and let each candidate be read again — the delivered behaviour. (b) Give the scheduler a second, entity-shaped service method that duplicates the claim, guard and storing logic. (c) Have the scheduler map the row to `dto/TweetDto` itself and pass that. (d) Make the pass transactional so the candidate rows stay managed and the re-read is served from the persistence context. (e) Select only identifiers in the candidate query and accept the per-row read as necessary. (f) Declare the two as overloads of one name `generateResponseIfAbsent`, which this row previously described. | The candidate query already selects every column of every candidate, so the second read returns rows the caller is holding: measured at exactly `1 + N` statements and `N` extra pool checkouts per pass — 403 statements for 400 candidates, 19 for 16 — and the count grows without bound with the unanswered backlog, which is the amplification (a) leaves in place. Both methods reach one private body, so the claim of DL-196, the existence guard, the stored column values and every client-visible literal are asserted once and cannot drift, which is what (b) would risk. The two names are distinct rather than overloaded because a raw identifier and a row are different subjects for a caller to pass and an overload set makes the call site read ambiguously; an earlier revision of this row described them as one overloaded name, which the tree never carried. (c) would put wire mapping in the task layer and give the scheduler a mapper collaborator it otherwise has no use for. (d) would hold a transaction open across the language-model call, which DL-086 excludes deliberately. (e) treats the symptom as unavoidable: the query it would replace is the same query, and the pass would still read one row per candidate. (f) is what this row asserted and the tree never carried: a call site passing a `Tweet` and one passing a `String` would resolve by argument type alone, so a reader could not tell from the name which subject form is in play, and a future third form would have to be distinguished by type too. | The supplied row is detached — the pass declares no transaction and `open-in-view` is false — so the generator must read only the columns the candidate query loaded; reading a lazy association from it would fail, which is why the mapper reads scalars only and the association is never traversed. The row is also a snapshot: a column edited between the candidate query and the generation call is not seen. The storing transaction still reads the row it attaches the reply to, so a candidate deleted mid-pass is still reported as a generation failure and no orphan reply is written. |
| DL-227 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a single resolution point for the pacing of the response-generation pass: the interval was read in exactly one place, from the `response_generation_delay` `settings` row falling back to the configured value, and consulted on every pass so an operator edit took effect without a restart. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** The pacing is resolved **once, at registration**. `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler.generatePendingResponses()` carries `@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${scanner.response-generation-delay-seconds}", timeUnit = SECONDS)`, so Spring resolves the placeholder when it registers the task and the interval does not change while the process runs. The `response_generation_delay` `settings` row is still seeded and still readable, and it now *reports* the configured pacing rather than setting it; an edit takes effect at the next restart. | (a) Keep the per-pass resolution through a custom trigger. (b) Keep `@Scheduled` and re-register the task when the row changes. (c) Resolve once at registration, which is what is delivered. (d) Remove the settings row, so nothing suggests the value is live. | AAP transformation rule TR-12 states the mechanism as `@Scheduled(fixedDelayString=…)`, and a declarative fixed delay is resolved once by construction — a per-pass interval requires a `SchedulingConfigurer` and a custom `Trigger`, which is the mechanism DL-251 withdraws. Between a documented requirement and a runtime convenience the requirement governs. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | An operator who edits `response_generation_delay` through `PUT /settings/{key}` sees the row change and the pacing not change until the process restarts, which is a genuine surprise and is why the row's own description says what it does. (d) would remove the surprise by removing a row AAP section 0.6.3.2 requires to be seeded, so it is unavailable. |
| DL-228 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a rule about when the next pass instant is computed: it was taken once, at the completion of the previous pass, from the interval in force at that moment, and was never revised while the pass was pending — so an interval change observed mid-wait did not move an instant already computed. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** There is no such computation to place. A declarative fixed delay is registered once with a resolved interval, and the framework schedules each run the stated number of seconds after the previous run completes. The rule this entry stated existed only to make the custom trigger of DL-251 predictable, and both are withdrawn. | (a) Keep the rule against a future custom trigger. (b) Fold it into DL-247, which owns fixed-delay semantics. (c) Withdraw it with the trigger, which is what is delivered. | The subject of this row was a property of a mechanism that is gone, so there is nothing left for it to own. The delivered pacing semantics — measured from the end of one run to the start of the next — are the source's own work-then-sleep loop and are stated under DL-247, which the migration's IR10 requires and which forbids `fixedRate`. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | Nothing is lost behaviourally, because the delivered semantics are the semantics this rule was written to guarantee. A reader who reaches this identifier from an old commit finds a withdrawal rather than a rule. |
| DL-229 | **Superseded by DL-270.** `config/DataSourceConfig.dataSource()` no longer carries `@ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "spring.datasource.hikari")`, and the whole `spring.datasource.*` surface is unread: `ScannerApplicationTests.readsNoSpringDataSourceKeyAtAll` asserts that the environment holds no such key. The allowlisted `scanner.datasource.pool.*` record this entry named as the replacement surface is **not in the delivered tree** — DL-270 owns that withdrawal, no key under `scanner.datasource` exists anywhere, and the pool takes HikariCP's own defaults. What is delivered is one `HikariDataSource` constructed directly from the translated URL and its extracted credentials (DL-027). This identifier is retained so a citation from any commit resolves. | (a) Keep the `spring.datasource.hikari` prefix binding, which is what this entry recorded. (b) Bind an allowlisted `scanner.datasource.pool.*` record, which DL-270 withdrew. (c) Construct the pool from the translated URL and take the framework's own pool defaults, which is what is delivered. | A prefix binding over `spring.datasource.hikari` lets any HikariCP property — a JDBC URL and credentials included — be set from the environment, which would let a deployment override the connection identity `scanner.database-url` establishes. (b) was the first replacement and the module inventory of the migration boundary declares no such class, so it was withdrawn in the scope restoration. (c) leaves the pool geometry at the framework defaults, which is the state the retired tree's per-call engine had no equivalent of at all. This row is rewritten in place under its own identifier, which is the revision policy DL-099 states. | The delivered pool geometry is whatever the framework version resolves, so it can change under a Spring Boot upgrade with no entry in this log recording it. A reader following this identifier reaches a pointer and must follow it to DL-027 for the delivered construction. | DL-230 | The two short request/response calls `task/TweetStreamClient` makes on the X API — the app-only token exchange at `POST /oauth2/token` and the rule listing and mutation at `GET`/`POST /2/tweets/search/stream/rules` — carry a per-request bound taken from `scanner.twitter.request-timeout-seconds`, default 10 and never read as less than one second. The filtered-stream subscription carries no bound at all. The bound is read on every call, so a change needs no restart, and a call that exceeds it fails the ingestion attempt, which the existing reconnection path answers with its exponential backoff. | (a) Leave all four X calls unbounded, as the transport bean does — the delivered behaviour. (b) Set `responseTimeout` on the shared `WebClient` in `config/WebClientConfig`. (c) Publish a second, bounded `WebClient` for the control plane and keep the unbounded one for the stream. (d) Hardcode the bound as a constant in the client rather than declaring a property. (e) Bound the calls with `Mono.timeout` plus a retry of their own, independent of the reconnection path. | Reactor Netty applies no response timeout by default, so (a) leaves the token exchange and the rules calls able to wait indefinitely on a peer that accepts a connection and never answers — a state that stalls ingestion silently, since the ingestion cycle is the only thing waiting and it emits nothing while it waits. (b) cannot be used: the same client carries the filtered stream, whose body is long-lived by design, and a response timeout there would cut a healthy connection — DL-193. (c) is sound and was rejected only on size: it adds a second bean and a second base URL to keep in step for three call sites that a per-request `timeout` already covers, and the property makes the value visible either way. (d) hides an operational value in code, unlike the two other adapters, whose bounds are `scanner.notion.connect-timeout-seconds` / `read-timeout-seconds` (DL-150) and `scanner.openai.request-timeout-seconds` (DL-146); the property also lets a test drive the bound. (e) would put a second retry policy beside the one the ingestion cycle already owns, so a slow peer would be retried twice over with two different policies. | A bound that is too short turns a slow but healthy X response into a reconnection, and the shortest accepted value is one second, so a misconfiguration can make the control plane unusable while the log records only the reconnections. The stream body stays unbounded by design, so a connection that is established and then goes silent without a keep-alive is still detected only by the transport, not by this bound. The group `scanner.twitter` now carries one component that is not a credential, so its `toString` renders that value in the clear while the seven credentials stay redacted. |
| DL-231 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a compact canonical constructor on `dto/UpdateResponseRequest` that refused a carried key whose value the addressed column cannot hold — accepting `content` only as a JSON string or null, and `is_approved` only as a JSON boolean or null — so a type mismatch was reported as a client fault before the update was attempted. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** The record carries the values as supplied and coerces rather than refusing. Any JSON type is accepted for `content` and rendered as its text — `123`, `true`, `["a"]` and `{"x":1}` are stored as those characters — and `is_approved` reads truthily: `1` and `"true"` are true, `0`, `"no"`, an array and an object are false. | (a) Keep the refusal. (b) Refuse only for `is_approved`, where a coercion is lossiest. (c) Coerce as the source did, which is what is delivered. (d) Add Bean Validation annotations instead of a constructor guard, which is the same widening by another route. | AAP section 0.6.5.2 limits Bean Validation and constructor guards to what the Pydantic schemas declared, which is type and optionality with no length, pattern or range constraint, and exactly two `@NotNull` annotations elsewhere in the tree. The source read `request.json` as a free-form mapping and coerced whatever it found, so refusing a JSON number for a text column is validation improvement rather than parity — and the coercions the delivered record performs are the coercions Python performed. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | A caller that sends `{"content": 123}` stores the characters `123` rather than receiving a 400, and one that sends `{"is_approved": ["x"]}` stores false. Both are silent coercions, and both are what the source did. The delivered mapping of every JSON type is asserted case by case so the coercion cannot drift. |
| DL-232 | `responses.generated_at` is minted at one site — `service/ResponseService.store`, inside the transaction that inserts the row — as `LocalDateTime.now().truncatedTo(ChronoUnit.MICROS)`. The value is server-side and local, is never taken from a request body and is never rewritten by `updateResponse`; the truncation fixes its precision at microseconds, which is the precision every vendor this artifact supports stores in the column: PostgreSQL `timestamp`, the `datetime(6)` Hibernate maps a `LocalDateTime` to on MySQL, and H2 `TIMESTAMP`. This entry owns the mint, which the split recorded in DL-077 assigned to DL-201 and DL-201 no longer carries. An earlier form minted `LocalDateTime.now()` untruncated. | (a) Mint `LocalDateTime.now()` untruncated, which is what the code did. (b) Truncate to seconds or milliseconds. (c) Let the database default the column (`insertable = false` with a DDL default). (d) Flush and refresh the row after the insert so the returned entity carries the value the column actually stored. (e) Serialise the timestamp with a fixed-precision Jackson formatter and leave the stored value alone. (f) Switch the column to `Instant` with a time zone. | Under (a) the 201 body of `POST /responses` carried nanosecond precision — measured `2026-08-05T16:13:13.54488515` — while every later read of the same row, and the column itself, carried `2026-08-05T16:13:13.544885`: the same instant reported two ways, so a client that cached the create body and compared it to a read by string equality saw a mismatch that no requirement asked for. Truncating at the mint makes the two equal by construction and costs one call; it also keeps the value the service reports identical to the value the column holds without a second database round trip, which is what (d) buys at the price of a flush and a refresh on every generation. Microseconds rather than (b) because microseconds is what the column stores: truncating further would discard precision the database is willing to keep, and the mismatch this closes is between the mint and the column, not between the mint and a coarser ideal. (c) moves the value out of the application, so the entity returned by the insert would carry `null` until it was re-read, and it would need DDL this project does not write (DL-026 leaves the schema to `ddl-auto`). (e) hides the difference on the wire while leaving the stored and in-memory values different, so anything reading the entity in the same transaction still sees the untruncated value. (f) changes the column type, which data-model parity freezes: `backend/app/db/models.py:L25` declares `DateTime`, which maps to `LocalDateTime`. | A vendor whose column keeps less than microsecond precision — MySQL `datetime` without a fractional-seconds argument, for instance — would still round the stored value, so the equality this entry establishes holds for the mapped types this artifact produces rather than for every conceivable column definition. Two rows generated within the same microsecond now carry the same timestamp, which was already possible and is not used for ordering: `@OrderBy("id ASC")` (DL-024's association ordering) and the pagination sort are by identifier. The truncation is invisible in the wire format itself, since ISO-8601 omits trailing zeros, so a reader cannot tell from one body whether the value was truncated or simply landed on a microsecond boundary. |
| DL-233 | `service/SentimentAnalysisService.analyzeSentiment(String)` rejects a document sentiment score that is not a finite number: `Double.NaN`, `POSITIVE_INFINITY` and `NEGATIVE_INFINITY` each raise `IllegalStateException` at the provider boundary, before the score is returned or logged as a measurement. The rejection is written by the existing provider-seam `catch`, which records the exception type only (DL-197), and reaches the client as 500 `{"error": "Internal server error"}` — the same status and body a provider exception already produces. No score is clamped, rounded or substituted: a finite score, including one outside `-1.0`…`1.0`, is returned exactly as received. `calculateDoubtRating(double)` is unchanged and still maps a non-finite argument the way the source expression does, so the arithmetic transcribed from `backend/app/services/sentiment_analysis.py:L26-35` keeps its behaviour for any other caller. | (a) Return the non-finite score, which is what the adapter did. (b) Substitute a neutral score such as `0.0`. (c) Clamp to `-1.0`…`1.0`. (d) Leave the adapter alone and configure Jackson to write a non-finite double as JSON `null`. (e) Leave the adapter alone and reject the value in `api/TweetController` or `service/TwitterService` before the response is built. (f) Reject it in `calculateDoubtRating` instead. | `analysis_result` is documented as the bare score and is a JSON number (DL-037). Jackson writes a non-finite double as the quoted string `"NaN"`, so under (a) `POST /tweets/{tweetId}/analyze` answered 200 with `{"tweet_id":"1","analysis_result":"NaN"}` — measured against the running service with a doctored provider — which is a type change in the response body, not a value a strict client can parse. The Natural Language API documents `documentSentiment.score` as a float between -1 and 1, so a non-finite value is not a measurement the provider is entitled to report; treating it as an integration fault is the same treatment DL-089 gives a 2xx Notion body that lacks the structure the call requires, and it produces a status the route already declares rather than a new one. (b) and (c) report a measurement that was never taken, the fault DL-075 and DL-080 both name, and (c) would additionally make an out-of-range-but-real score indistinguishable from a broken one — the doubt rating already clamps, and clamping the input as well would erase the distinction the clamp exists to handle. (d) fixes one endpoint's serialisation while leaving the broken value in the doubt rating written to the column, and it changes how every double in the application serialises. (e) puts a provider contract check two layers away from the provider, where the same check would have to be repeated by `task/TweetStreamListener`, the other caller. (f) is the wrong owner: that method is a transcription of source arithmetic (DL-036) whose documented behaviour for a non-finite argument is part of the port, and the guard belongs where the value enters the system. | A provider that ever legitimately reported a non-finite score would make the analyze route answer 500 rather than 200 — accepted, because the documented range makes that a contract breach on the provider's side. The check is one comparison per call and cannot be reached from configuration, so an operator cannot opt out of it. `calculateDoubtRating` still accepts a non-finite argument and still answers 10.0 for `NaN`, so the two members now disagree about what a non-finite value means; that is deliberate — one is a provider boundary and the other is transcribed arithmetic — and it means a future caller could reach the rating path with a value `analyzeSentiment` would have rejected. |
| DL-234 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded an `@ExceptionHandler` on `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` answering a `MultipartException` with **HTTP 415** and the `Unsupported media type` literal, on the ground that a malformed multipart body is a media-type fault rather than a server fault. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** There is no such handler and no `Unsupported media type` literal anywhere in the tree. (b) Answer 400 instead. (c) Let it reach the catch-all, which is what is delivered. | The wire-contract parity clause freezes the status codes and the error envelopes to what the retired Flask application served: only `@app.errorhandler(404)` and `(500)` produced JSON, so a framework-reported client failure came from Werkzeug's own page. The literal this handler wrote was one of four the review found added beyond the source's set, and the endpoints this service exposes accept no multipart body at all, so the handler covered a request shape no route serves. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | A malformed multipart request is reported as a server failure rather than a client one, which is a less accurate status than this entry produced. (b) Answer 415 with the 500 literal. (c) Let the framework's own status stand with no JSON body, which is what is delivered. (d) Answer 400 instead. | The wire-contract parity clause freezes the status codes and the error envelopes to what the retired Flask application served, and this behaviour was not among them. | DL-244 | `dto/UpdateResponseRequest` admits an explicit JSON `null` on either carried key and treats it as a write of `null`, so `{"content":null}` and `{"is_approved":null}` bind without error and reach `service/ResponseService.updateResponse`, while a carried key of any other unwritable type does not (DL-231). (b) Bind the two components as `Optional<String>` and `Optional<Boolean>`. (c) Bind them as plain `String` and `Boolean`. (d) Add a separate `clear` flag per column. (e) Let the `null` reach `service/mapper/ResponseMapper` and escape as the DL-092 500, which is what this tree did before the required-member guard existed. (f) Relax `dto/ResponseDto` so a `null` member is renderable and the clear succeeds. | Validation parity permits only what the Pydantic schemas declared — types and optionality, and no constraint beyond them — and `responses.content` and `responses.is_approved` are both nullable in the frozen schema, so (a) would add a constraint the source never expressed and would make the record stricter than the column it writes. The row that results still has to be renderable, and `backend/app/schema/response.py:L6,L8` declares both members required, so the write cannot be committed: reporting it with the literal the route already declares for a failed update, and rolling back, keeps the answer inside the closed three-literal set and leaves no row in a state the wire contract cannot render. (f) would change a DTO transcribed from the source schema, which parity freezes. (b) was tested and does not work: Jackson delivers both an absent key and an explicit `null` as `Optional.empty()`, which is why the record keeps raw `JsonNode` components. (c) makes `{}` and `{"content":null}` indistinguishable. (d) invents wire members the source route never had. | An explicit `null` is admitted at the binding boundary and rejected at the persistence boundary, so two bodies that both fail to write receive two different statuses — 400 for a wrong type, 404 for a `null`. (b) Keep the entity page and read only `response.getTweet().getId()`, relying on the lazy proxy's identifier. (c) Select a constructor expression into the DTO directly. (d) Add a plain `tweetId` column to the entity beside the association. | `r.tweet.id` resolves from the owning foreign-key column without joining the parent, so the projection reads one table where the entity page read two. (c) would put the wire shape in a JPQL string, so the DTO's required-column invariant would move out of `service/mapper/ResponseMapper`. (d) duplicates the foreign key in the entity, which data-model parity forbids. | A derived count query cannot be inferred for a projection query, so the count is a second JPQL string that must be kept in step with the selection's `from` clause; both are in one interface and `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest` asserts the statement count. | DL-246 | Both pessimistic-lock reads carry a bounded wait at two levels. At statement level `TweetRepository.findByIdForUpdate` and `ResponseRepository.findByIdForUpdate` both set the portable `jakarta.persistence.query.timeout` hint to `ResponseRepository.LOCK_WAIT_MILLIS` (5000 ms). At transaction level `ResponseService.updateResponse` declares `@Transactional(timeout = 5)`, and the storage path — which runs through a `TransactionTemplate` because its caller is a private method whose self-invocation would bypass the proxy — holds a bounded *copy* of the injected template: `boundedCopyOf` reads the injected instance's `PlatformTransactionManager`, builds `new TransactionTemplate(manager, injected)` so every other attribute is carried over, sets the five-second timeout on the copy, and refuses to construct the service at all when the injected template carries no manager. The shared bean is never mutated. A caller that cannot obtain the lock within the bound receives `PessimisticLockingFailureException` or `QueryTimeoutException`, which the service answers by storing nothing and recording one `WARN` naming the identifier and the bound — and then, because "nothing was stored" is not a value every caller can express, by giving each of the three calling paths the answer its own contract already defines: `updateResponse` reports the wire literal of `backend/app/api/responses.py:L65`; a background pass, which asks `store` to skip an already-answered row, receives `null` and leaves the row to a later pass; and `POST /responses`, which does not, receives `ResponseGenerationException` and therefore the wire literal of `:L49`, which DL-076 already names for a failure raised while saving the row. `store` returns `null` on contention **only** on the background path, so no caller dereferences a row that was never stored. An earlier form of this entry stated the single outcome "storing nothing and recording one `WARN`" for every path; `store` returned `null` to the route path as well, which dereferenced it. | (a) Leave the lock wait unbounded, which is what was delivered before this decision. (b) Use `LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE` with no timeout and rely on the database's own `lock_timeout`. (c) Use an optimistic `@Version` column instead. (d) Retry the lock acquisition in a loop. (e) Bound the storage path by calling `setTimeout` on the injected `TransactionTemplate` bean directly. (f) Change the constructor to take a `PlatformTransactionManager` and build the template outright, as `SettingsService` does. (g) Leave the storage transaction unbounded and rely on the statement hint alone. On the contention answer: (h) Return `null` from `store` on every path and let each caller decide, which is what was delivered. (i) Return `null` on every path and add a guard at the route call site mirroring the background one. (j) Raise on every path, so a background pass reports a failure for a row another writer is already answering. (k) Return `Optional<ResponseDto>` from `store` so the empty case is impossible to dereference. (l) Report the contended route request as 409 or 503. | An unbounded `select … for update` lets one stuck writer hold a scheduled pass indefinitely, and the pass has no other timeout, so a single contended row could stall ingestion for the process's lifetime. The JPA hint is the portable expression of the bound and is honoured by both supported vendors. (b) moves an application bound into per-deployment database configuration. (c) adds a column, which data-model parity forbids. (d) turns contention into repeated work with no ceiling. (e) mutates a bean the context owns and any future consumer shares, so a second consumer would silently inherit this service's bound. (f) is the shape `SettingsService` uses and is equally correct, but it changes a public constructor signature that the test tree and the context already bind, for no behavioural gain over the copy. (g) leaves the transaction that wraps the statement unbounded, so work the statement does not cover — the existence check and the insert — has no ceiling; bounding both levels at the same five seconds keeps one number to reason about. On the contention answer: the two calling paths differ in what they are able to say, and that is what decides this. A background pass has a next pass, so "nothing was stored" is a complete answer for it; `POST /responses` must answer the request in front of it, and the only answers that route serves are 201 and the `:L49` literal, so a value meaning "nothing" has nowhere to go. (h) is the defect this revision closes: the route path dereferenced the `null` it was handed, and the resulting `NullPointerException` was caught by the same clause that reports a failure with no owning layer — so a benign lock contention, already reported at `WARN` one line earlier, was reported a second time at `ERROR` under the name `NullPointerException`, which is a false diagnosis at alerting severity and contradicted that clause's own stated invariant. The HTTP outcome was accidentally right, which is exactly why it survived several reviews: the wire contract cannot detect this and only the log can. (i) fixes the symptom at one call site and leaves the ambiguous contract in place for the next caller, which is how this defect arose. (j) would make a pass report a failure for a row another writer is legitimately answering, turning a benign race into an `ERROR` and a wasted candidate. (k) is the type-safe shape and is declined only because it changes the private helper's signature and both call sites for no outcome the raise does not already produce; the same reasoning is why the raise is placed in `store` rather than at the call site. (l) adds a status this route never served, which the wire-contract parity clause forbids and DL-092 records for the framework's own failures. | A pass that loses the race stores nothing for that row and does not retry within the same pass, so the row is picked up by a later pass — bounded by the pass interval. A `POST /responses` that loses the same race has no later pass: it answers 500 with the `:L49` literal, and the client cannot tell that outcome from an unset provider credential or an absent tweet, because that literal covers all three — the `WARN` naming the lock bound is the only place the difference is visible, and a caller that wants the reply has to post again. The generated text is paid for and discarded in that case, since the provider call happens before the lock is sought. The bound is a constant rather than a property, so changing it needs a build. A vendor that ignores the hint would fall back to its own lock timeout. The copy is taken once at construction, so a deployment that reconfigures the shared template after the context is built does not affect this service. A deployment that supplies a template with no transaction manager fails fast at construction rather than at the first storage attempt, which is the intended trade for an early, legible failure. |
| DL-247 | Three time-window corrections. `TweetRepository`'s trends query is bounded at both ends, `created_at` between `now - window` and `now`; `scanner.analytics.trend-window-days` is validated as a finite positive number of days; and `task/TweetStreamListener` refuses a provider `created_at` that lies in the future rather than storing it. | (a) Leave the trends query open at the upper end, which is what was delivered. (b) Clamp a future timestamp to the current instant instead of refusing the record. (c) Substitute the arrival instant for a future provider timestamp. (d) Accept any window value and let a non-positive one produce an empty series. | An open upper bound let a single row carrying a future timestamp add a bucket outside the window the endpoint documents, so the series a client received depended on data quality rather than on the configured window. (b) and (c) both report a timestamp the provider did not send. (d) leaves a misconfiguration presenting as missing data. | A post whose `created_at` is genuinely a few seconds ahead of this process's clock is refused, so clock skew between the provider and this service costs records; the refusal is recorded at `WARN` with the record's correlation token. The window is validated at binding, so a deployment that set an invalid value now fails to start where it previously started and served an empty series. |
| DL-248 | The scheduled response-generation pass drains the unanswered backlog in bounded keyset batches, up to the per-pass candidate ceiling of DL-282. `TweetRepository.findUnansweredBatchAfter` selects at most one batch of rows whose identifier is above the last one seen and which carry no response, and the pass clears the persistence context between batches. The batch size bounds what is resident and the ceiling bounds what one pass attempts; nothing bounds what a pass may spend, because the provider work budget an earlier form of this row credited is withdrawn — DL-283 records that withdrawal and the unbounded spend it leaves. | (a) Materialise every unanswered row in one list, which is what was delivered. (b) Read one bounded batch per pass and let later passes drain the rest. (c) Page with an offset instead of a keyset.
