Batching within one pass bounds what is resident, and the separate candidate ceiling of DL-282 bounds the burst, because batching alone bounded memory and not spend. A keyset rather than (c) because the predicate's own result set shrinks as rows are answered, so an offset would skip rows. (b) would tie the drain rate to the pass interval through the batch size, which is an internal memory bound; DL-282 ties it to an explicit operational ceiling instead, so the two bounds stay separately choosable. (d) keeps a cursor and a transaction open across every provider call. | A row inserted during a pass and carrying an identifier below the cursor is not seen until the next pass, which is the same guarantee a single snapshot gave. Clearing the persistence context between batches detaches everything read earlier, so nothing may be held across a batch boundary; the pass holds only identifiers and counters. The batch size is a constant rather than a property, and it is not the pass bound — a reader looking for the number of candidates one pass attempts finds it in DL-282. |
| DL-249 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a chunked read behind an unchanged wire contract: any positive `per_page` including `Integer.MAX_VALUE` was answered, while the rows behind it were fetched, mapped and serialised in consecutive bounded chunks through `TweetRepository.findChunk` and a `mapInChunks` helper, so heap use was bounded independently of the requested page size. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** There is no chunking. Both list services issue one page query for the requested size and map the result in one pass; `TweetRepository.findChunk`, the `mapInChunks` helpers and the `PAGE_FETCH_CHUNK_ROWS` constants are deleted, and `repository/TweetRepository` carries only the methods the frozen contract declares. | (a) Keep the chunked read. (b) Keep it and stream the response, which would bound the response too but changes the serialisation path. (c) One query per request, which is what is delivered. | The chunked read existed to make the withdrawn cap of DL-123 unnecessary, so removing the cap removed its purpose as well: with the cap gone, a chunked fetch still materialises every row of the requested page before the response is written, so it bounded the fetch and not the response. It also added a repository method the file-level contract for `repository/TweetRepository` does not declare. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | Heap use for a list request is proportional to the requested page size, with no intermediate bound. (b) is the only shape that would genuinely bound it, and it is a serialisation change the parity clause does not admit because it alters how the body is produced. |
| DL-250 | Which background paths a process runs is bound explicitly under `scanner.background`: `enabled` governs both paths together, `stream-enabled` binds `TWITTER_STREAM_ENABLED` and `response-generation-enabled` governs the generation pass. Each gate is read from configuration and nothing else. `task/TweetStreamClient.isAutoStartup()` consults the stream gate, so a process that does not run ingestion reaches X in no way, and `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` consults the generation gate as an **early return at the top of the pass** — the scheduled method is always registered, and it returns immediately without reading a candidate when the group withholds the pass. | (a) Consult a coordination lease as well, so that only one of several enabled processes runs each path — the mechanism DL-281 withdraws. (b) Omit the scheduled registration entirely in a process that withholds the pass, through `@ConditionalOnProperty`. (c) Register always and return early, which is what is delivered. (d) One gate for both paths, dropping the per-path switches. | A configuration switch is the only coordination the migration boundary admits: it declares no ownership class and freezes the `settings` table to configuration rows, so (a) is unavailable. (b) is the tidier shape and was not taken because the registration is itself an asserted contract — exactly one scheduled task exists, with a fixed delay in seconds and no `fixedRate`, `cron` or initial delay — and a conditional registration would make that assertion depend on the profile under test. (d) loses the ability to run ingestion and generation in different processes, which is the only reason the switches exist. | A process that withholds the pass still registers it and still wakes on the fixed delay to return immediately, which is a scheduled wake-up doing no work — negligible, and stated so it is not mistaken for the pass running. Two processes that both enable a path both run it; nothing prevents it, and DL-281 records that consequence in full. |
| DL-251 | `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig` is `@Configuration @EnableScheduling` and declares exactly one bean, a `ThreadPoolTaskScheduler` with a pool of two threads named `scanner-scheduler-`, `waitForTasksToCompleteOnShutdown` set and a thirty-second termination await — so a pass already running is allowed to finish when the context closes. It implements no `SchedulingConfigurer`, declares no `Trigger`, holds no delay bounds and no reporting guards, and injects nothing: the class is ninety-six lines where it was three hundred and sixty-six. | (a) Keep the five hardenings an earlier revision recorded: a `SchedulingConfigurer` with a custom `nextResponseGenerationPass(TriggerContext)`, a per-pass `resolveDelay()`, minimum and maximum delay bounds, one-shot reporting guards, and an ownership consultation. (b) Keep the custom trigger and drop the bounds. (c) Declare `@EnableScheduling` and the pool only, which is what is delivered. (d) Declare `@EnableScheduling` alone and let the framework's single-threaded default scheduler run the pass. | AAP transformation rule TR-12 states the mechanism as `@Scheduled(fixedDelayString=…)` on the component, which puts the pacing on the method and leaves this class with nothing to configure but the executor. The bounds were guarding a per-pass resolution that no longer happens (DL-227), and the ownership consultation was re-homed into the pass itself as an early return (DL-250). (d) was rejected because a single-threaded default scheduler is shared with any future scheduled work and gives no shutdown await, so a pass in progress would be cut short at context close. | A malformed `scanner.response-generation-delay-seconds` now fails at task registration — and therefore at startup — rather than being clamped into a documented range, which is a harsher failure and a clearer one. Two scheduler threads is a fixed geometry no property changes. A pass that outlives the thirty-second await is interrupted at shutdown, which is the same bound the withdrawn form applied. |
| DL-252 | Two reads stand between a background generation request and the paid provider call, each taken once and each in its own short repository-demarcated transaction, so no database connection is held across the model call. First, before the subject row is read at all, `responses` is asked whether the row already carries a reply — the cross-replica check, and the common case. Then, immediately before the provider call, `tweets` is asked whether the row is still present, so a row deleted since it was selected costs no generation. The definitive check remains the one the storing transaction takes under the parent row's pessimistic lock. | (a) Check only inside the storing transaction, which is what was delivered first. (b) Hold one transaction across the provider call so the check and the insert share a snapshot. (c) Take a database advisory lock on the identifier before the provider call. (d) Rely on the in-process claim alone. (e) Ask `responses` twice — once before the subject read and again immediately before the provider call — which is what the revision after (a) delivered. (f) Keep only the late pair and drop the early reply check, so both questions are asked at the last possible moment. | A row another replica had already answered was paid for and generated before the in-transaction guard discarded the result, so the wasted spend was exactly the cost of the model call. Reading first turns the common case into one indexed read. (e) is the revision this entry withdraws: the second reply read was the same query against the same identifier a few microseconds later, and the window it closed is one the in-transaction guard already covers, so it bought nothing and charged every generation an extra read. (f) narrows that window furthest and was rejected because it moves the cheapest and most likely check behind a subject read, and it makes a row that is both gone and answered raise the generation failure of DL-086 instead of quietly storing nothing. (b) would hold a pooled connection for the whole provider latency, which with a bounded pool is how a pool is exhausted. (c) adds a lock mechanism for a check an indexed read answers. (d) does not see another replica at all. | Neither pre-call read is atomic with the insert, so two replicas can still both pass them and one will be discarded by the in-transaction guard — the reads narrow a window rather than closing it, and the in-transaction guard remains the correctness boundary. A reply written between the early read and the provider call is not seen until the storing transaction, so that generation is paid for and discarded; that is the window (f) would have narrowed. Every generation costs two indexed reads beyond the storing transaction. |
| DL-253 | A Notion mirror write is retried within a bounded budget — `scanner.notion.mirror-max-retries` attempts after the first, spaced by `scanner.notion.mirror-retry-backoff-millis` doubling once per earlier retry up to a ceiling — and **only two classes of failure are retried**. A `RestClientResponseException` is retried when its status is 429 or any 5xx and not otherwise; a `ResourceAccessException` is retried, because it is the failure the client raises when the request never reached the provider or its answer never arrived. Every other runtime failure is propagated on the first attempt: a `RestClientException` from an answer the client could not decode, and a programming failure such as `NullPointerException`, `IllegalStateException` or `IllegalArgumentException`. The budget re-attempts one HTTP call and never the persisted row. | (a) Treat every failure carrying no HTTP status as a transport failure and retry it, which an earlier revision of this entry recorded as delivered. (b) Retry nothing and propagate every failure. (c) Retry on an explicit allowlist of exception types rather than on `ResourceAccessException` plus a status test. (d) Retry 429 and 5xx only, treating a transport failure as terminal. | (a) is withdrawn because a failure with no status is not the same thing as a transport failure: a malformed or undecodable answer, a mapping mistake and a missing reference all arrive without a status, and a later attempt answers each of them identically — so retrying them spends the budget and the provider's quota to reach the same outcome, and it delays the propagation of a genuine programming defect by the whole backoff sequence. `ResourceAccessException` is precisely the client's transport wrapper, which makes the test both narrow and complete. (c) is the same rule written as a list that must be maintained against a client library this service does not control. (d) discards the one case a retry most reliably fixes. | A provider fault the client surfaces as something other than a status answer or a transport failure is now terminal for that mirror attempt, so a client-library change that reclassifies a retryable condition into another type would silently stop retrying it. The mirror is a secondary store and its failure never blocks the relational write, so the cost of a missed retry is an unmirrored row rather than lost data. Each of the four propagated shapes is asserted to be attempted exactly once. |
| DL-254 | Three bounds on the stream rule set, and one of them was moved. The **collected terms** are bounded by `scanner.ingestion.max-stream-rules`, which truncates the composed collection and records the two counts at `WARN` with no term value. Each rules mutation is sent as consecutive requests of at most twenty-five rules. The `ai_tools` **rows read** are no longer bounded by the rule cap: `mergeAiToolNames` pages the `name` projection one hundred rows at a time and merges each page — dropping a blank name, a name the grammar refuses and a case-insensitive repeat as it goes — until the collection holds as many terms as the cap admits, a short page reports the table exhausted, or ten thousand rows have been read. Only the third of those is a bound on reading, and reaching it is recorded at `WARN` with counts alone. | (a) Bound the rows read by the rule cap and filter afterwards, which an earlier revision of this entry recorded as delivered. (b) Read the whole `name` column into memory and filter it, with no bound on rows at all. (c) Page and filter as each page arrives, with a scan bound, which is what is delivered. (d) Push the grammar into the JPQL predicate so the database filters, and keep the cap on rows. | (a) is withdrawn because it bounds the wrong thing: with the cap applied before the filter, a table whose first rows carry blank, refused or repeated names spends the whole budget on rows that contribute nothing, and every usable name behind them is unreachable — so the union with every tool name that AAP section 0.6.5.4 requires is silently incomplete. (b) is unbounded against a table an operator can grow. (d) would need the grammar expressed twice, once in JPQL and once in Java, and JPQL cannot express the character-class rule at all. | A table holding more than ten thousand rows none of which carries an acceptable name is not fully scanned, and the shortfall is reported rather than silent. Ten thousand rows is four hundred times the default rule cap, so reaching it means the table is almost entirely unusable. Paging issues more statements than one bounded read did — one per hundred rows until the cap is met — which is paid once per connection cycle and not per record. |
| DL-255 | The popularity threshold is resolved once per connection cycle rather than once per record. `service/TwitterService.popularityThresholdInForce()` performs the resolution, `meetsPopularityThreshold(Integer, int)` compares against a supplied value, and `task/TweetStreamListener.onStatus(JsonNode, int)` carries a resolved threshold through a whole cycle. The single-argument overloads keep their original lazy behaviour exactly, resolving at the gate and only for a record that reaches it. A settings row that holds no parsable number is warned about once per distinct value rather than once per record. | (a) Resolve at the gate for every record, which is what was delivered. (b) Resolve eagerly at the head of the handler for every record. (c) Cache the resolved value with a time-to-live. (d) Read the row once at startup. | A busy stream turned one indexed settings read per delivered record into the dominant cost of ingestion, and every unparsable row produced one `WARN` per record. Resolving per cycle keeps the value as fresh as a reconnection while reading the row once. The single-argument overloads had to keep resolving lazily: resolving eagerly would add a read for a record that is dropped before the gate, which is what (b) does and what an earlier attempt at this change did — it changed thirty-nine assertions about when the row is read. (c) adds a cache this migration excludes. (d) makes an edit require a restart. | A `settings` edit made mid-cycle is not seen until the stream reconnects, which for a healthy connection can be a long time; the row remains authoritative for the analyze and scheduled paths, which resolve it themselves. The two overloads mean the gate has two entry points, and a change to the comparison must be made in the shared private body rather than in either. |
| DL-256 | The filtered-stream body carries a signal-idle bound: `scanner.ingestion.stream-idle-timeout-seconds`, default 60, applied as a per-signal `timeout` on the `DataBuffer` flux. Any buffer resets it, including the provider's keep-alive newline, so a connection that stops delivering anything at all fails and reconnects. | (a) Leave the body unbounded, which is what was delivered. (b) Apply a total response timeout on the `WebClient`. (c) Add a TCP keep-alive on the connection provider. (d) Track the last-delivered instant in the listener and stop the client from outside. | A half-open connection — accepted, then silent — is indistinguishable from an idle stream without a bound, so ingestion could appear healthy while delivering nothing. A per-signal timeout is exactly an inter-element bound and is reset by the keep-alive the provider already sends, so a genuinely quiet stream is unaffected. (b) cannot be used: the response is long-lived by design and a total timeout would cut a healthy connection — see DL-230. (c) operates below the protocol and would not detect a peer that keeps the socket alive without sending. (d) puts lifecycle control in the record handler. | A stream that legitimately sends neither a matching record nor a keep-alive for longer than the bound is reconnected, so the bound must stay above the provider's keep-alive period; the default is well above the documented 20 seconds. Each reconnection re-registers the rules, which costs two control-plane requests. |
| DL-257 | The X rule grammar lives in `task/TweetStreamClient`, the component that registers the rules, as the private predicate `isUsableTerm(String)` over the additional-character set `" -_.'"`, a 128-character bound and the requirement that a term open and close with a letter or digit — a letter or digit of any script is admitted, because the predicate calls `Character.isLetterOrDigit`. `service/SettingsService` describes the same grammar in the `stream_keywords` row's own description rather than re-implementing it, so there is one executable definition and no second copy to diverge from. An earlier revision held the grammar in a shared `util/StreamRuleTerms` class, which DL-208 withdraws on file-inventory grounds. `isUsable(String)` admits a term of at most 128 characters whose first and last character is a letter or a digit and every character of which is a letter, a digit, a space, a hyphen, an underscore, a period or an apostrophe; `expressionOf(String)` renders an admitted term, quoting it when it holds whitespace; `split(String)` is the one reader of the comma-delimited `stream_keywords` row, bounded at 512 segments; `countUsable(String)` counts the admitted segments of such a value. `task/TweetStreamClient` drops a term the grammar refuses before the rule set is composed and `service/SettingsService` reports an operator edit that leaves no admitted term, both recording the bound, a correlation token and the length — never any part of the text. The character test is `Character.isLetterOrDigit`, so a letter or digit of any script is admitted. | (a) Quote the term and rely on quoting alone, which is what was delivered first. (b) Escape the rule-DSL metacharacters and accept the rest. (c) Reject the whole rule set when any term is unacceptable. (d) Log the rejected term so an operator can correct it. (e) Keep the grammar private to `task/TweetStreamClient` and give `service/SettingsService` its own looser check — which is what the revision after (a) delivered: the client accepted 128 characters under the allowlist above while the settings diagnostic accepted 1,024 characters and refused only a double quote, a backslash and a control character. (f) Restrict the character test to ASCII letters and digits. | The rule DSL has operators — `-`, `(`, `)`, `"`, `OR`, `from:`, `is:` — that a term drawn from `ai_tools.name` or from a `stream_keywords` row could carry, and a term is operator-supplied data reaching a query language, so quoting alone leaves a negation or an operator prefix expressible. An allowlist is decidable and does not depend on knowing every operator the provider may add. (e) is the revision this entry withdraws: two grammars for one contract meant the diagnostic an operator read after `PUT /settings/{key}` did not predict what ingestion would do — a 200-character term, or a term carrying a colon or a parenthesis, was reported usable and then silently dropped when the rule set was composed, which is worse than no diagnostic because it is a confident wrong answer. One shared class also removes the second implementation of the delimiter split, so the segment bound of DL-254 is applied to the same value by both readers. (b) trades a decidable rule for a list of metacharacters that has to track the provider. (c) would let one bad row disable ingestion entirely. (d) is the log-injection exposure DL-197 closes. (f) would refuse a legitimate tool name written in any non-Latin script for no gain: a Unicode letter is not a rule operator, and every character that could forge a log record or close a quoted expression is refused already because it is neither a letter nor a digit. | A legitimate term carrying a character the allowlist does not admit — an emoji, a colon, a parenthesis, a hashtag — is dropped, and the log names its length and a correlation token but not its text, so diagnosing which row was rejected means reading the table. The bound and the additional-character set are constants rather than properties, so widening either needs a build. Both callers now depend on one class: a change to `isUsable` changes what ingestion registers and what the settings diagnostic reports at the same time, which is the intent. The class this entry once named as its covering test is not in the delivered tree: the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary declares nineteen test classes and none of the guard classes that assertion relied on, so they were deleted under DL-216 and this claim is held by review against the delivered artifacts rather than by an assertion in the build. |
| DL-258 | Record dispatch is sequential and bounded at a prefetch of one: the stream body is published on `Schedulers.boundedElastic()` with a prefetch of one and each chunk's records are handled by a `concatMap` with a prefetch of one. | (a) Leave Reactor's default prefetch of 256, which is what was delivered. (b) Reduce the prefetch to a larger measured value. (c) Use `flatMap` with a bounded concurrency. (d) Handle records on the stream's own thread with no `publishOn`. | A record may be up to a megabyte, so a default prefetch queued on the order of hundreds of megabytes per connection while the listener — which performs a database write, a Notion call and possibly a model call — worked through them. A prefetch of one makes the queue the size of one record and pushes back on the source instead. (b) trades memory for a throughput this workload does not need, since the listener is the bottleneck. (c) would process records out of order and run several provider calls at once. (d) would block the stream's event-loop thread on a database write. | Throughput is now bounded by the listener's own latency, so a slow database or a slow provider slows delivery and can eventually trigger the provider's own overflow handling; the alternative was buffering the same backlog in memory. The two prefetch values are constants rather than properties. |
| DL-259 | New intake is refused before the drain begins: `dispatchRecord` returns `false` immediately when `stopRequested` is set, before the record is trimmed, parsed or counted, so the counter the drain watches can only fall and a record emitted during the drain neither starts nor is cut short. `task/TweetStreamClient.start()` and `stop()` are serialised through one `ReentrantLock`, the subscription handle is published and cleared under it, and in-flight dispatch is counted. `stop()` waits up to five seconds for the count to reach zero **before** disposing the subscription, then disposes it; `stop(Runnable)` delegates and runs the callback in a `finally`. | (a) Dispose first and then drain, which is the order the first attempt at this fix used. (b) Guard the lifecycle with an `AtomicBoolean` alone. (c) Track in-flight work with a `Phaser` or a `CountDownLatch` per record. (d) Do not drain at all and let the container's shutdown timeout bound it. | (a) does not work, which measurement showed: disposing the subscription interrupts the `boundedElastic` worker, so the listener's work is aborted and the drain then observes a count of zero — the very defect the drain exists to prevent. Draining first lets a record already handed to the listener finish, and the lock makes a concurrent `start()` and `stop()` impossible rather than merely unlikely. (b) leaves the handle's publication and cancellation unordered, so a restart can race. (c) is heavier than a counter for a single-consumer pipeline. (d) leaves a partially written row's transaction to be interrupted. | A record whose handling exceeds the five-second bound is still interrupted, so the drain narrows the window rather than closing it; the bound is a constant. `stop()` holds the lifecycle lock while it drains, so a `start()` arriving during a shutdown waits for it. |
| DL-260 | A record the stream cannot use is counted and reported at most once per minute per reason, with the number suppressed since the previous record. Two reasons are counted independently — a record that could not be read as JSON and a record that exceeded the accumulator bound — and a suppressed occurrence is written at `DEBUG`. | (a) Report every occurrence at `WARN`, which is what was delivered. (b) Report the first occurrence and then nothing. (c) Sample a fixed fraction. (d) Count silently and expose the totals through a metric. | A provider change or a malformed feed turns one record per delivered post into one `WARN` per delivered post, which is a log-volume amplifier with a cost proportional to traffic. A windowed count keeps the signal — the reader learns both that it is happening and how often — at a fixed cost. (b) hides a condition that starts later or worsens. (c) makes the count unrecoverable. (d) needs the metrics stack this migration excludes. | An occurrence inside the window is visible only at `DEBUG`, so a reader at `INFO` sees a count rather than each event. The window and the two counters are per process, so a multi-replica deployment produces one report per replica per window. The interval is a constant rather than a property. |
| DL-261 | Rule reconciliation preserves each registered rule's identifier, expression and tag. `readRegisteredRules` returns a `RegisteredRule` record keyed by expression, and a rule is deleted and re-added when its expression is no longer wanted **or** when its tag differs from the wanted one. | (a) Compare expressions only, which is what was delivered. (b) Delete every rule and re-add the whole set on each cycle. (c) Compare tags but update in place with a rule-update call. (d) Ignore the tag entirely and derive the matched tool name from the record's text. | The tag is what `task/TweetStreamListener` reads from a matched rule and writes to `tweets.ai_tools_mentioned`, so a rule whose tag drifted from the term it matches silently mislabels every post it matches — a data-correctness fault invisible in the rule listing. (a) could not detect it. (b) leaves a window in which no rule is registered and records are missed. (c) is not available: the rules endpoint has no update operation. (d) would reimplement matching in this process. | Changing a term's tag now costs a delete and an add rather than nothing, so a tag edit briefly leaves that rule unregistered. The comparison treats an absent tag as an empty one, so a rule registered by some other tool with no tag is replaced on the first cycle. |
| DL-262 | The filtered-stream request asks for no `expansions`. It requests exactly the `tweet.fields` the listener reads. | (a) Keep requesting `expansions=author_id`, which is what was delivered. (b) Keep the expansion and read the expanded `includes.users` object to store an author handle. (c) Request every field and expansion the endpoint offers. | The listener reads `data.author_id` directly and never opens `includes`, so the expansion enlarged every delivered record with an object nothing consumed — a per-record cost with no consumer. Reading it, as (b) suggests, would need a column the four tables do not have. (c) enlarges every record further. | A later change that wants the author's handle must re-add the expansion and read `includes`; the fields the request asks for are listed in one constant beside the members the listener reads, so the pairing is legible. |
| DL-263 | `POST /tweets/{tweetId}/analyze` is one service orchestration. `service/TwitterService.analyzeTweet(String)` reads the row once as the `AnalysisSubject` projection, calls Google with no transaction open, and writes the derived rating with the single-statement `TweetRepository.updateDoubtRating`. `api/TweetController` injects only `TwitterService` and holds no `SentimentAnalysisService`. | (a) Read the entity, call Google, then read it again and save it, which is what was delivered. (b) Keep the controller sequencing the two services but pass the entity through. (c) Hold one transaction across the provider call. (d) Make the write a private `@Transactional` method on the service. | (a) read the same row twice per request and orchestrated two services from the controller, so the route's transaction shape was decided in the web layer. The projection selects both `id` and `content`, which keeps an absent row distinguishable from a present row whose `content` is `null` — the two produce 404 and 500 respectively, exactly as before. (c) would hold a pooled connection for the whole provider latency. (d) does not work: a private method's `@Transactional` is bypassed by self-invocation, which is why the write is demarcated by the repository method instead. | The write is unconditional and by identifier, so a row deleted between the read and the write is reported as not found rather than silently ignored — the same 404 the read would have produced. `updateDoubtRating` bypasses the persistence context, so an entity loaded elsewhere in the same transaction would not see the new value; no caller does that. |
| DL-264 | `config/RestClientConfig` publishes the Notion transport as a retained `HttpClient` bean carrying the connect bound, declared `@Bean(destroyMethod = "")`, and releases it in a `@PreDestroy` that calls `shutdown()`, awaits termination for ten seconds and then forces the client down. The read bound is applied per request by a `JdkClientHttpRequestFactory` built over that client. | (a) Let the framework build the client beneath a request-factory builder and leave it to the garbage collector, which is what was delivered. (b) Publish the client and let Spring call its inferred `close()`. (c) Keep the builder and register a separate shutdown hook. (d) Use Apache HttpClient or Jetty's client, which expose an explicit lifecycle. | A Java 21 `HttpClient` owns a selector thread and a connection pool, and (a) left both unreachable, so a context close released neither. (b) does not work: the inferred `close()` blocks for as long as any request is in flight, with no bound, so a hung request would hold shutdown open — which is why `destroyMethod` is cleared and the bounded sequence is used instead. (c) would hold a reference without publishing it as a bean. (d) changes the transport the compile-verified dependency inventory names. | A request still in flight after the ten-second bound is forced down, so a slow Notion call can lose its response during shutdown; the mirror is the secondary store and the relational write has already committed. Clearing `destroyMethod` means the `@PreDestroy` is the only release path, so a future refactor that stops calling it would reintroduce the leak silently. |
| DL-265 | The `Context:` clause of the OpenAI prompt is bounded: at most ten AI tool names are carried, every line break within a name is folded to a space, and the joined value is cut to 200 characters and marked with the truncation mark. | (a) Carry every name at any length, which is what was delivered. (b) Bound the character count only. (c) Bound the name count only. (d) Drop the clause when it would exceed the bound. | The clause is composed from `tweets.ai_tools_mentioned`, a delimited column whose contents come from stream rule tags, so its length is data-dependent and is paid for on every generation as prompt tokens. The post body was already bounded and folded the same way, so this closes the one remaining unbounded prompt segment and does it by the same rule. (b) alone would let a thousand names be joined before the cut. (c) alone would let ten long names exceed any budget. (d) removes context the prompt is designed to carry. | A post naming more than ten tools loses the rest of its context, and a truncated clause ends mid-name; the marker makes the cut visible to the model. The two bounds are constants rather than properties. The null-element branch was removed because `dto/TweetDto` already drops a `null` list element, so the invariant lives in the DTO rather than here. |
| DL-266 | `service/LlmService` coordinates the client's release with the work using it. A `ReentrantReadWriteLock` is held for reading while one completion is in flight and for writing while the client is released; `closeOpenAiClient()` marks the bean destroyed **before** it waits, then awaits the write lock for thirty seconds and releases the client after the drain, after the bound, or after an interrupt. `generateResponse` tests the destroyed flag before it queues on the lock and again once it holds it. The lock is the whole mechanism: the class carries no active-call counter and no polling drain. | (a) Close the client with no coordination, which is what was delivered before this entry and what DL-147 described. (b) Count active calls with an `AtomicInteger` and spin until it reaches zero — which an intermediate revision actually wrote alongside the lock. (c) Mark destroyed after the wait rather than before. (d) Wait indefinitely for the drain. (e) Do not close the client at all and let the process exit release it. | (a) let a context close abort a synchronous completion that had already been paid for. Marking destroyed first is what makes the drain terminate: while the flag is set only after the wait, new work keeps passing the check and taking the read lock, so a non-fair lock can starve the writer for the whole bound and the client is then force-closed under active readers — which is (c), and is the same defect this entry closes in the sentiment adapter under DL-268. (b) reimplements a read/write lock with a spin, and the intermediate revision that wrote it left it inert: the counter was incremented and never decremented and the wait was never invoked, so it was dead state that contradicted DL-147 and it has been removed. (d) makes shutdown unbounded. (e) leaks the client's connection pool in any process that outlives the bean. | A completion still in flight after thirty seconds is abandoned and the client is released anyway, so a very slow model call can still be aborted; the bound is a constant. A request arriving during a shutdown is refused with `IllegalStateException` rather than queued, which a caller sees as a failed generation. Because no counter is kept, the number of completions in flight is not observable from the bean. |
| DL-267 | `scanner.openai.n` must be exactly one. Any other value is refused before the request is issued, with a message naming the key. | (a) Accept any value of at least one and send it, which is what was delivered. (b) Consume every requested choice and return the first non-blank one. (c) Clamp the value to one silently. (d) Remove the key and hardcode one. | The adapter reads `choices[0]` and no other, matching the source's index, so any value above one is billed for output that is discarded. (b) would change what the adapter returns and make the choice selection a new behaviour the source never had. (c) hides a misconfiguration that costs money. (d) would drop a key the source declared, which configuration completeness requires be present. | A deployment that had set the key above one now fails to start rather than merely overspending, which is a louder failure than the one it replaces. The key remains declared and configurable, so its only remaining purpose is to document the constraint. |
| DL-268 | `service/SentimentAnalysisService.closeLanguageClient()` marks the bean destroyed before it begins waiting, and `analyzeSentiment` tests that flag before it queues on the read lock as well as once it holds it. `releaseClient()` no longer sets the flag; it closes the client and clears the field. | (a) Set the flag inside the release, after the wait, which is what was delivered. (b) Test the flag only after acquiring the read lock. (c) Use a fair lock so the writer cannot be starved. (d) Do not wait at all and close immediately. | While the flag was set only by the release, the whole thirty-second wait was a window in which new analyses passed the check and took the read lock, so a steady arrival rate could starve the writer for the full bound and the client was then force-closed under active readers — the precise condition this entry closes, and the same shape DL-266 closes for the OpenAI adapter. Marking first makes the drain monotonic: the reader set can only shrink. (b) leaves new work queueing behind a release it will then be refused by. (c) would make the wait terminate but would still let work start during it. (d) aborts live provider calls. | A caller that arrives during a shutdown receives `IllegalStateException` immediately rather than waiting, which is a behaviour change for a caller that previously would have been served. An analysis still in flight at the thirty-second bound is abandoned and the client released anyway. |
| DL-269 | A Notion rejection is recorded with the HTTP status, the shaped `code` member of the error body, the provider request identifier and the **character count** of the provider explanation. No run of the explanation's text is recorded. `notionErrorFieldOrNull` reports an absent field as `null`, and an absent explanation is reported as a length of `-1`. | (a) Record the explanation truncated to 200 characters, which is what was delivered. (b) Record it with control characters stripped and a bound applied. (c) Record nothing about it at all. (d) Record it at `DEBUG` and the rest at `ERROR`. | The `message` member is provider-controlled free text: it can carry the control characters that let it forge a line in a log record, and Notion validation messages can echo submitted content back. A truncation bounds the length and neither hazard. The status, the shaped `code` and the provider request identifier already identify the rejection and locate the provider's own copy of the explanation, so the text adds no diagnostic value that survives the risk — and the adapter's own documented policy already said no run of response body reaches a log record, which the truncated message contradicted. (b) is sound and was rejected as strictly weaker than reporting a count. (c) loses the signal that an explanation was sent at all. (d) moves the hazard to a level an operator may well enable. | Diagnosing a rejection now requires the provider's own record, reached by the request identifier; a deployment without access to it sees a code and a length. The earlier reader returned the literal `absent` for a missing field, which the `code` shape happened to admit, so the null-reporting variant was introduced and the shape check now runs against a real value only. |
| DL-270 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded `config/DataSourcePoolProperties` as an allowlist for the connection pool's configurable surface, bound from `scanner.datasource.pool.*` with eight components carrying geometry and timing only, so that no arbitrary HikariCP key could be set from the environment. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** The class, the eight properties and the `scanner.datasource.pool.*` block of `application.yml` are all deleted. `config/DataSourceConfig` builds the pool and leaves every geometry and timing value at the framework default, which is the posture the migration's own persistence rule states when it delegates pooling to the framework. | (a) Keep the allowlist. (b) Bind `spring.datasource.hikari.*` directly, which DL-229 already rejected because this service reads no `spring.datasource` key. (c) Take the framework defaults, which is what is delivered. (d) Hard-code a geometry in `config/DataSourceConfig`. | The migration boundary enumerates the classes of every module and declares this one in none of them, so it was deleted in the scope restoration this review round required. The persistence rule of the migration says pooling is delegated to the framework, and an eight-key tuning surface is not delegation. The source created a new engine per call with no pool at all, so framework defaults are already a large improvement and no requirement asks for more. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | Pool geometry and timing are no longer settable from the environment, so a deployment that needs a larger pool cannot obtain one without a code change. That is a real loss of operability, accepted because the boundary admits no class to hold the surface. (d) would fix a geometry no deployment could change at all. |
| DL-271 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded documented environment-backed ranges for the pool's geometry and timing — `maximum-size` 10 with a ceiling of 100, `minimum-idle` 2, `connection-timeout-millis` 30000, a validation timeout, and the remaining components of the allowlist DL-270 described — each with a stated default and a stated bound. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** None of these values is declared anywhere. The pool takes the framework's own defaults, and `application.yml` carries no `scanner.datasource.pool` block. DL-270 owns the withdrawal of the surface these ranges belonged to. | (a) Keep the ranges as documentation with no binding behind them. (b) Keep them as hard-coded constants in `config/DataSourceConfig`. (c) Take the framework defaults and record the withdrawn ranges here, which is what is delivered. | The migration boundary enumerates the classes of every module and declares this one in none of them, so it was deleted in the scope restoration this review round required. These ranges existed only as the documented contract of the allowlist class, so withdrawing the class withdraws them; they are recorded here rather than deleted because the values were chosen deliberately and a future change that reintroduces the surface should start from them rather than from nothing. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | The delivered pool geometry is whatever the framework version resolves, so it can change under a Spring Boot upgrade without any entry in this log recording it. (a) would leave a documented contract nothing enforces, which is the class of defect this review round exists to remove. |
| DL-272 | `api/AuthController` bounds the **credential verifications in progress** in this process and reports rejections at a bounded rate; the **request rate** on the route remains an ingress obligation stated in the class documentation. A private `Semaphore` issues `Math.max(2, Runtime.getRuntime().availableProcessors())` permits. A submitted credential of accepted length acquires one permit before `AuthenticationManager.authenticate` is called, waiting at most 250 milliseconds for it; a request that acquires none is answered with this route's 401 and an empty body without any verification being started for it, and is reported under the same bounded policy with a record naming only the permit count. The permit is released in a `finally`, so an issued token, a rejected credential and a propagated provider failure each return it. A credential above the 256-character ceiling is refused before a permit is sought, so an over-length flood waits for nothing. An interrupt while waiting restores the thread's interrupt status and is answered as an unacquired permit. Rejections are reported at `WARN` at most once per minute per reason, carrying the number suppressed, with **three** reasons — over-length, unverified for want of a permit, and not authenticated — counted independently and a suppressed rejection written at `DEBUG`. That interval-and-count reporting is the whole reporting mechanism: the class carries no second sampler and no window state beyond the counter and report instant each reason holds. The deployment obligation that remains is a per-client request quota on the route and a total service request-rate ceiling. | (a) Leave the verification work unbounded and treat the whole exposure as an ingress concern, which is what was delivered before this entry. (b) Add an application rate limiter — a bucket per client address or per principal. (c) Add a lockout after N failures. (d) Lower the bcrypt cost factor so each attempt is cheaper. (e) Size the permit count as a fixed constant independent of the processor count. (f) Make the permit count and the wait configurable properties. (g) Reject immediately with `tryAcquire()` and no wait. (h) Queue without bound using `acquire()`. (i) Answer an exhausted permit set with 429 or 503 instead of 401. (j) Bound the work with a fixed-size executor the verification is submitted to. (k) Report every rejection at `WARN`. (l) Report nothing. (m) Keep the epoch-second sampling window an intermediate revision also wrote — a second set of counters and a second `recordRejection` method beside this one. | Verification is the expensive half of this route, and it was measured: `BCryptPasswordEncoder.matches` against the delivered `$2a$10$` hash costs a mean of **72.2 ms** of CPU per call over ten calls on this four-processor host. Unbounded, the concurrent verifications a public unauthenticated route admits are limited only by the servlet container's thread pool — 200 threads by default — so roughly three dozen concurrent requests saturate every core and starve the eleven authenticated routes that share it. A permit count equal to the processor count caps that at the CPU the host actually has, which is a bound on the work rather than on the request rate, and it needs no new dependency, no shared store and no per-client state. (a) is the defect. (b) and (c) are excluded by scope: rate limiting is a new capability the migration boundary at the head of this file excludes, and a lockout would add state and a policy the source never had; note that neither is a substitute here, since a limiter keyed on client address does not bound work from many addresses. (d) weakens the hash to make abuse cheaper to absorb, which trades the wrong thing. (e) either under-uses a large host or over-subscribes a small one; the floor of two keeps a single-processor container serving. (f) is a real option and is declined because a permit count is a property of the host rather than of the deployment, and the configuration surface this migration adds is already the eight keys the retired tree referenced without declaring. (g) turns a momentary overlap of two legitimate operators into a 401 for one of them; a 250 ms wait is long enough to absorb one in-flight verification measured at 72 ms and short enough that the worst-case wait a client sees stays a quarter of a second. (h) reintroduces the exposure as unbounded queued latency and held threads. (i) changes this route's status-code contract, which this migration preserves; 401 with an empty body is the only rejection `POST /auth/token` serves, and an attacker learns nothing from it. (j) moves the work off the request thread and needs a rejection policy, a queue bound and a second thread pool to reach the same cap a semaphore reaches with one field. The request-rate half of the exposure stays an ingress concern and is documented as one, naming the specific controls. On reporting: (k) turned an attempt flood into one record per attempt — a bounded windowed count keeps the signal at a fixed cost — (l) hides an attack in progress, and (m) was written and never wired: no call site reached it, so it was dead state describing a different suppression policy from the one in force, and it has been removed. | The bound is per process, so a deployment of N replicas admits N × permits concurrent verifications, and nothing in the application bounds the total **attempt rate** — a client that submits credentials one at a time still costs one verification each, so the documented per-client ingress quota is still required. A legitimate operator whose request arrives while every permit is held receives the same 401 with an empty body as a wrong credential and has to retry; the log distinguishes the two reasons, the response does not. Sustained saturation is therefore visible as a denial of service for correct credentials, which is the intended trade against starving the authenticated routes. The permit count and the 250 ms wait are constants, so changing either needs a build, and the count is read from the processor count at class initialisation, so a container whose CPU allocation changes at runtime keeps the count it started with. How low that count can sit is worth stating outright, because it decides how easily a correct credential meets the 401: a container allocated one processor or fewer — Cloud Run's own default allocation — reports one, so the count sits at its floor of two, and a third concurrent login waits 250 ms and is then refused. A modest burst of legitimate operators, not an attack, is therefore enough to reach the bound on such a deployment, and each refused operator receives the wrong-password answer; the log names the reason and the response cannot, since the answers that would distinguish it on the wire are the 429 and 503 that alternative (i) declines, on the status-code parity DL-092 records. The mitigation available to a deployment is to allocate more than one processor, which raises the count by one permit per processor. A rejection inside the reporting window is visible only at `DEBUG`, the counters are per process so a multi-replica deployment reports once per replica per window, no client address is recorded so the log does not distinguish one attacker from many clients, and the reporting interval is likewise a constant. |
| DL-273 | Every executor a test creates is shut down, awaited and asserted terminated, and an interrupt while awaiting is restored on the calling thread and reported as a test failure. `service/SettingsServiceTest` and `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest` each declare the same private helper. | (a) Shut down without awaiting, which one of the two did. (b) Await and discard the result, which the other did. (c) Extract the helper into a shared test utility class. (d) Use a JUnit extension that fails a test leaving a live thread. | A pool that is not awaited can outlive the test that made it, and a surviving thread may hold one of the connections of a pool whose size is the framework default — DL-270 records that no geometry is declared — for the remainder of the build — a cross-test failure whose cause is invisible where it appears. Discarding the await result is the same defect with the diagnosis already computed and thrown away. Restoring the interrupt keeps an interrupted build interruptible. (c) would add a production-tree utility used only by tests, or a test-only class shared across packages for six lines. (d) is a larger mechanism than two call sites need. | The helper is duplicated in two classes, so a change must be made in both; each is six lines and each is documented as net-new against this entry. The bound is five seconds in one class and the concurrency timeout in the other, so a genuinely slow machine could fail a test that previously passed by leaking. |
| DL-274 | `ScannerApplicationTests` runs in a `MOCK` web environment and issues its requests through `MockMvc`; the real filter chain, dispatcher and controllers all run, and no connector, port or server thread is started. | (a) `RANDOM_PORT` for the composition class, which is what was delivered. (b) `MOCK` for the composition class plus a new narrowly scoped connector class carrying the two `/error` cases. (c) Delete the route cases from the composition class and rely on the `@WebMvcTest` slices. (d) `MOCK` everywhere and accept that the container-level path is uncovered. | Converting the assertions and running them established that every one is reachable without a connector, including both direct requests to `/error`, since a direct request to that path is an ordinary request to `BasicErrorController` rather than a container ERROR dispatch. (a) therefore paid for a server for no assertion and made the widest class in the suite sensitive to connector timing. (b) was prepared and then not needed; it would also have cached a further application context, which raises build cost rather than lowering it. (c) removes the only end-to-end coverage of the eleven routes. (d) would leave the `sendError` path of DL-118 uncovered, which is why the request-body class keeps its container. | Behaviour visible only through a real connector is covered by exactly one class, so that class and the composition class must be kept in step if the container-level path grows. A `MOCK` environment does not exercise connector-level request framing, header parsing or the container's own error reporting; The class this entry once named as its covering test is not in the delivered tree: the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary declares nineteen test classes and none of the guard classes that assertion relied on, so they were deleted under DL-216 and this claim is held by review against the delivered artifacts rather than by an assertion in the build. |
| DL-275 | The answer to a rules mutation is read rather than discarded: `task/TweetStreamClient.reportRefusedRules` records one `WARN` when the answer carries an `errors` entry or a non-zero `meta.summary.not_created` or `meta.summary.invalid`, naming the refused count and the two summary counts. A refusal does not fail the mutation — the stream connects with the rules the endpoint did accept. The reflected `errors[].value` expression reaches the record only as the keyed-digest token of DL-119, a fixed-shape token, and at most one request's worth of tokens is listed while the count is reported in full. | (a) Discard the answer and read only the HTTP status, which is what was delivered before the answer was read at all. (b) Log the reflected `errors[].value` expression as the provider returned it, which is what the revision after (a) delivered. (c) Log the expression through the printable-ASCII guard of DL-119, normalised and cut to 64 characters. (d) Fail the mutation and refuse to connect when any rule is refused. (e) Match the reflected expression back to the request body and log this application's own index for it. (f) List every fingerprint with no bound. | A partial refusal is silent at the transport layer — the endpoint answers 200 and reports the refusals in the body — so under (a) a rule set could be registered short by half with nothing in the log to say so, and ingestion would then match fewer posts than configured for no visible reason. (b) is the exposure this entry closes: the value is provider-reflected text on a path DL-197 governs, and a value carrying a carriage return and a line feed forges a record boundary. (c) keeps the text readable and is the guard used where the text is the diagnostic, but here the expression is one this application composed and can therefore reproduce, so a non-reversible token gives the same correlation without carrying provider text at all: an operator matches it against `LogSafe.correlation(StreamRuleTerms.expressionOf(term))` for each configured term. (d) turns a partial refusal — most commonly one duplicate rule — into no ingestion at all. (e) is the most legible option and is rejected on cost: it would thread the request body through the reporting path to name an index the operator would still have to map back to a term. (f) leaves the record length at the provider's discretion. | A fingerprint is not reversible, so an operator who did not keep the term list cannot recover which expression was refused; the remedy is to fingerprint the configured terms, which is deterministic. The token is a truncated HMAC keyed with a per-process secret (DL-119), so it is not confirmable against a guess from outside the process and the same expression carries a different token in the next process. A refusal beyond one request's worth is counted but not fingerprinted. A rule the endpoint silently declines without an `errors` entry and without incrementing either summary member is not reported at all. |
| DL-276 | **Withdrawn as a file; restated as a policy.** This entry declared a tracked `.yamllint.yml` at the repository root holding the YAML lint policy both workflows were written to, and named `.github/dependabot.yml` alongside it. **The dependency-update monitoring that stands in their place is committed as content rather than as configuration**, and it is what a reader should look at when asking how a stale dependency is found here. Three mechanisms carry it. And each moving part has a row here stating the condition under which it is refreshed: DL-003 for the Boot line, DL-103 for the pinned action, DL-106 for the two container digests, DL-285 for the Node floor. | (a) Keep both files, on repository-hygiene grounds. (b) Keep `.yamllint.yml` and drop `.github/dependabot.yml`, since only the first affects committed content. (c) Move the lint policy into the CI job as command-line flags. (d) Restore `.github/dependabot.yml` alone and make the contract test require it, which is what a later review asked for. (e) Add a scheduled workflow that opens an update proposal, in place of the vendor configuration. | Neither file appears in the frozen file inventory this migration is authorised to create, and the matrix's own §2.9 prose admitted as much while §2.9's table claimed the operations delivery was four files — a self-contradiction a review recorded as part of a critical scope breach. Deleting both removes the contradiction at its source. (a) and (b) keep an out-of-inventory file; (c) edits the workflow steps the plan freezes. (d) is the same out-of-inventory file under a narrower name: the frozen plan declares its in-scope file list exhaustive, and `.github/dependabot.yml` is not in it, so the review's request is answered by the exclusion — and answered *substantively* by the three mechanisms above, which are committed, in-scope, and fail the build rather than opening a pull request nobody has to merge. (e) is a fifth operations file by another name. | No automated dependency-update proposal is opened for the Maven coordinates, the action references or the container base images, so every refresh is initiated by a person. That is the real gap and the policy above does not close it: a test that fails when a version *changes* does not fire when a version *should have changed*, so a coordinate can sit stale indefinitely and the build stays green — which is precisely how the archived Slack action and the `node12` `setup-gcloud` reference DL-103 records came to be years old. Time-to-fix for DL-003, DL-103, DL-106 and DL-285 is therefore bounded by review cadence and nothing else. Repository YAML is held to no committed policy; the two workflows retain the whitespace form the withdrawn policy normalised them to, which is yamllint's own `default` preset, so re-adding the file under a widened scope would need no edit to either workflow. The class this entry once named as its covering test is not in the delivered tree: the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary declares nineteen test classes and none of the guard classes that assertion relied on, so they were deleted under DL-216 and this claim is held by review against the delivered artifacts rather than by an assertion in the build. |
| DL-277 | `security/SecurityConfig.filterChain` declares `Strict-Transport-Security` **inline on the chain** — `headers(h -> h.httpStrictTransportSecurity(hsts -> hsts.includeSubDomains(true).preload(false).maxAgeInSeconds(HSTS_MAX_AGE_SECONDS)))` — and `HSTS_MAX_AGE_SECONDS` is the `private static final long` holding `31_536_000L`, one year, which is the value Spring Security's own writer carries. (b) Take Spring Security's defaults for every header including this one, which emits no `Strict-Transport-Security` value of the project's choosing. (c) Disable header writing entirely. (d) Carry the `max-age` as a bound configuration property rather than a constant. (e) Request `preload`. | The chain is the one place the security posture is declared, so declaring the header there keeps it beside the rules it accompanies and costs no extra bean; the values written are the framework's own, so no new header and no new value is introduced by naming them. (a) adds a published declaration surface the migration boundary does not name, and its only other consumer was the container-level error path DL-237 withdrew. (b) leaves the value implicit in a framework default that an upgrade may change. (c) weakens the delivered posture. (d) makes a transport guarantee operator-tunable for no stated requirement, and a mistyped value silently shortens it. (e) is an irreversible commitment for a domain this project does not own. | The response header set is the framework default apart from this one header, so a Spring Security upgrade can change the rest without a recorded decision. The one-year `max-age` is compiled in, so shortening it needs a redeploy. The header is written only on a request the container reports secure, so a deployment that terminates TLS without forwarding `X-Forwarded-Proto` emits nothing and gets no warning (DL-293). No test asserts the header set; the claim is held by review against `SecurityConfig`. |
| DL-278 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded `config/ClockConfig` as a dedicated `@Configuration` class publishing one `Clock utcClock()` bean returning `Clock.systemUTC()`, with `service/AnalyticsService` and the background paths as its consumers, so that no component read the clock statically and every time-dependent behaviour was substitutable in a test. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** The class is deleted and the bean is declared on the composition root, `ScannerApplication`, instead. | DL-279 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded `config/BuildProfileGuard`, a `BeanFactoryPostProcessor` that refused to start the application when the profile named `test` was active and the test framework was absent from the bean class loader, so a deployed artifact could not be started under the profile that supplies an in-memory database and a test signing secret. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** The class does not exist and no such guard is performed.
