| DL-280 | The delay `task/TweetStreamClient` waits after an HTTP 429 is finite for every `x-rate-limit-reset` header value a provider or an intermediary can send. `rateLimitDelay` parses the header as UTC epoch seconds inside a `try` that catches `NumberFormatException`, `ArithmeticException` and `DateTimeException`, so a value out of the range `Instant` represents leaves the computed jittered backoff in force rather than propagating; and a reset instant further away than the `MAX_RATE_LIMIT_DELAY` reconnect horizon of fifteen minutes is applied as the horizon. The header therefore raises the delay from the backoff floor towards the reset instant and can never raise it above the horizon, so the largest wait any answer produces is fifteen minutes and ingestion always reattempts. A `WARN` records which of the three outcomes applied — absent header, unreadable header, or horizon substituted — and names the header and the horizon in seconds, never the received value. | (a) Trust the header outright and wait however long it reports, which is what was delivered. (b) Catch a wider `RuntimeException` around the parse. (c) Ignore the header entirely and always use the computed backoff. (d) Clamp with the existing five-minute `MAX_RECONNECT_BACKOFF` ceiling instead of a separate horizon. (e) Bind the horizon to a configuration key. (f) Treat an unreadable header as a fatal fault and stop the stream. | `Instant.ofEpochSecond(long)` raises `DateTimeException` for any value outside the instant range, and the delivered catch listed only `ArithmeticException` and `NumberFormatException`, so a header of `31556889864403200` — a value inside `long` and outside `Instant` — escaped the reconnect loop's own handling. (a) also let a header of `99999999999999999` suspend ingestion for longer than the service will run, which is indefinite data loss produced by a value the service does not control. (b) would swallow a defect in the surrounding code along with the parse. (c) discards a signal the provider sends to prevent a further rejection, which is what DL-045 records the floor as being for. (d) would conflate two bounds with different jobs: the backoff ceiling paces reconnection after a fault, while this horizon bounds a wait the provider asked for, and the horizon is deliberately the longer of the two so a genuine reset inside it is still honoured. (e) adds a key to the configuration surface this migration caps, for a bound no operator has a reason to move. (f) contradicts the indefinite-retry decision of DL-045. | The horizon is a judgement, not a documented X requirement: a genuine reset more than fifteen minutes away is reattempted early, which draws a further 429 and a further bounded wait rather than one long one. Requests spent that way are answered by the provider without delivering records. `Duration.between` is computed against `Instant.now()` rather than the one `Clock` bean `ScannerApplication.utcClock()` declares at `ScannerApplication:67-69`, so the reset comparison is not substitutable in a test; No test covers the horizon: the class that reached the private method reflectively is deleted under DL-216, and the authorised inventory declares this component no test class (DL-214). |
| DL-281 | **Withdrawn: `task/BackgroundOwnership` does not exist.** This entry recorded a lease-based ownership mechanism: a `SmartLifecycle` bean claiming a time-limited lease at startup, renewing it from a fixed-delay task and releasing it at shutdown; the lease held in one row of the existing `settings` table keyed `background_owner`, carrying a process `UUID` and a lapse instant separated by `@`; a compare-and-set claim through `SettingRepository.replaceValueIfUnchanged` and `replaceAbsentValue`; and `isOwner()` consulted by `task/TweetStreamClient.isAutoStartup()` and by every generation pass, with every failure — including an insert race lost to `DataIntegrityViolationException` — leaving `isOwner()` false so that ownership was never inferred from a failure to disprove it. **None of it is delivered.** The class, the two repository methods that existed only for it, the two lease properties, the reserved settings key and every ownership gate were deleted, because the migration boundary declares the class in no module and the `settings` table is declared to hold configuration rows and not coordination state. What remains is the plain enablement switch DL-250 owns: `scanner.background.enabled` and `scanner.background.stream-enabled` are read from configuration, `task/TweetStreamClient.isAutoStartup()` consults the stream switch and `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` returns early when the group withholds the pass. | (a) Deliver the lease this entry described. (b) Keep the lease but move it out of the `settings` table into a table of its own. (c) Coordinate through an external lock — a database advisory lock, or a broker. (d) The delivered posture: a configuration switch per background path, with the operator responsible for enabling each in exactly one process. | (a) is not admitted by the migration boundary, which enumerates the classes of every module and names no ownership class, and it wrote coordination state into a table the data-model parity clause freezes to three configuration columns. (b) adds a fifth table, which that same clause forbids absolutely. (c) introduces broker or vendor-lock infrastructure the boundary excludes by name. (d) is what the source did — it had no coordination of any kind, and its ingestion never ran at all — and it is the only option the boundary admits. | Two processes that both enable a background path both run it: the stream registers rules twice and ingests twice, and the generation pass can select the same candidate twice. Nothing in the application prevents it; correctness rests on the deployment enabling each path in one process, which is a configuration responsibility the operator carries and which is stated here rather than implied. That is a real regression against the mechanism this entry described, accepted because the boundary admits no class to hold it. |
| DL-282 | One scheduled response-generation pass attempts at most `scanner.background.max-candidates-per-pass` candidates, default 200, bound within 1 and `Integer.MAX_VALUE` by the compact constructor of `config/ScannerProperties.Background`. The keyset drain of DL-248 continues to read batches of at most 100 rows, and the pass now stops at the ceiling as well as at a short batch: it counts attempts across batches, breaks out of the batch and the drain when the count reaches the ceiling, and records one `WARN` naming the ceiling and stating that the remaining backlog is left to the following pass. The ceiling counts attempts rather than successes, so a candidate that fails consumes one. | (a) Drain the whole backlog in one pass, which is what was delivered. (b) Bound the pass by elapsed time instead of by candidate count. (c) Bound it by the batch count rather than the candidate count. (d) Make the ceiling a constant rather than a property. (e) Count successes only, so a failing pass keeps trying. | (a) bounded what was resident in memory but not what one pass would spend: a backlog of any size produced that many serial OpenAI calls and that many Notion mirrors within a single pass, with no bound the deployment could state. A candidate count is the unit the spend is proportional to, which (b) and (c) both only approximate — elapsed time varies with provider latency and a batch count multiplies to a candidate count anyway. (d) would leave a deployment unable to state its own per-pass bound, and unlike the batch size of DL-248, which is an internal memory bound, this one is an operational limit an operator has a reason to choose. (e) would let a systematically failing provider consume unbounded attempts within one pass, and an attempt-counting ceiling is the only thing that bounds that: no failure circuit stands behind it, as DL-283 records. | A backlog larger than the ceiling drains over several passes, so at the default delay of 60 seconds a backlog of 10,000 rows takes about 50 passes; the drain rate is now a function of the ceiling and the pass interval together, which is a behaviour change from the source's drain-everything loop and is recorded as such. The ceiling is applied per pass and not per hour, so shortening the pass interval raises the aggregate rate and no bound in the tree is independent of the interval — DL-283 records the withdrawal of the one that was. |
| DL-283 | **No provider work budget is delivered.** This entry previously recorded `task/ProviderWorkBudget`, a `@Component` that granted or refused one unit of external work through a fixed-window allowance of `scanner.background.provider-calls-per-window` units per `scanner.background.provider-window-seconds` span and a consecutive-failure circuit governed by `scanner.background.provider-failure-threshold` and `scanner.background.provider-circuit-open-seconds`. That component, its four configuration keys in both YAML profiles, the four components they bound on `config/ScannerProperties.Background`, the gate `task/TweetStreamListener` took ahead of each sentiment call, the gate `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` took per candidate, the outcome-recording calls each made, and `task/ProviderWorkBudgetTest` are all deleted. As delivered, a record that passes the popularity gate reaches the sentiment call directly and a candidate the backlog query returns reaches its provider call directly. The only bound one pass carries is the per-pass candidate ceiling DL-282 owns. No allowance, window, token bucket, attempt counter, failure circuit, lockout or delay is applied to any external call anywhere in the tree. | (a) Keep the component and record the divergence in this row, which is what the previous revision of this entry did. (b) Keep only the consecutive-failure circuit and drop the fixed-window allowance. (c) Replace the fixed window with a token bucket, or the hand-written circuit with Resilience4j or Spring Retry. (d) Keep the allowance and raise its default until no deployment reaches it. (e) Re-express the same bound on the ingestion path as a per-record ceiling, mirroring the shape DL-282 carries. (f) Configure the bound outside the application, in the ingress or in each provider account's own quota. | New rate limiting lies outside the migration boundary this file states at its head, without qualification and alongside caching, autoscaling changes, new indexes and new constraints. A fixed-window call allowance and a consecutive-failure circuit are rate limiting: each refuses work the ported behaviour performs, on a schedule the retired tree never had. (a) is the defect this revision closes — an entry of this log records a decision and cannot authorise a capability the boundary excludes. (b) and (d) keep a limiter and change only its shape or its threshold, so each is the same excluded capability under another name. (c) adds a coordinate the fixed dependency set does not carry as well. (e) reads as a batch bound and is not one: ingestion has no pass and no query to batch, so a per-record ceiling there is a rate. (f) is the correct place for this control and lies outside this repository, which holds `infrastructure/terraform/**` structurally unchanged and confines its operations edits to the backend build and image. The per-pass candidate ceiling of DL-282 survives on a different ground: it bounds how many rows one query hands to one pass, which is the batching decision DL-248 already makes, and it is measured against no clock. | Every external call is unbounded in both volume and failure repetition, which is the retired behaviour: an ingestion burst issues one sentiment call per delivered record, and a provider failing systematically is called once per delivered record and once per candidate for as long as the failure lasts, so a credential fault or a provider outage produces sustained billable traffic with no ceiling the application imposes. Nothing in the tree detects a failing provider or backs off from it: the reconnection backoff of DL-045 and the reset horizon of DL-280 cover the X stream connection alone, and Google, OpenAI and Notion have no equivalent. The controls that remain are the per-pass candidate ceiling of DL-282, the scheduler pool of DL-251 and the Notion retry budget of DL-253, and none of them bounds spend. An earlier form of this column counted the ownership lease among them; that lease is withdrawn (DL-281), so nothing coordinates the background paths either and a deployment that enables one in several processes multiplies the unbounded volume by the number of those processes. A deployment that needs a ceiling has to set one at the provider account or in the ingress, which is stated here because the application no longer offers one. |
| DL-284 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded a reserved `settings` key: the row holding the background-ownership lease of DL-281 was withheld from the configuration surface, so `getAllSettings()` did not render it and `updateSetting` reported it absent, with the reserved name declared once as `BackgroundOwnership.OWNER_SETTING_KEY`. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** No key is reserved. `service/SettingsService` renders every row of the table and updates any row that exists; there is no reserved-name check, no `background_owner` row and no `BackgroundOwnership` class — DL-281 owns that withdrawal. Every row of `settings` is part of the configuration surface, which is what the data-model parity clause describes the table as holding. | (a) Keep the reservation against a future lease. (b) Reserve a key prefix rather than one name. (c) Reserve nothing, which is what is delivered. | The lease this key protected does not exist, so the reservation protects nothing. Withholding a row from `GET /settings` would in any case have been a wire-contract change the parity clause does not admit: the source rendered whatever the table held. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | If a coordination row is ever added to `settings` it will be rendered by `GET /settings` and writable through `PUT /settings/{key}`, which is why DL-281 records that coordination belongs outside this table. (a) would leave a check with nothing to check, which is the class of claim this review round removed. |
| DL-285 | **The `node-version` input of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` is `'14'`, exactly as the retired workflow declared it, and the raise this entry previously recorded is withdrawn.** An earlier revision set the input to `22.23.2` on the ground of a toolchain floor of 22.12.0 on the 22.x line. That value, and the two comment lines that carried its pointer, are removed; the step is now byte-identical to the source branch — `uses: actions/setup-node@v2` with `node-version: '14'` and no comment. DL-074 is the sole owner of the value. Every frontend step the input serves — `npm ci`, `npm run lint`, `npm run type-check`, `npm test` and `npm run build` — remains byte-identical, including the pre-existing breakage that no lock file is committed and that the `lint` and `type-check` scripts do not exist in `frontend/package.json`. A full diff of the delivered workflow against the source branch now shows changes in the backend job alone. | (a) Keep `22.23.2` and record the divergence here, which is what the previous revision of this entry did. (b) Keep the raise and also move `actions/setup-node` to a maintained major pinned by commit SHA. (c) Move the version into a `.nvmrc` or a `package.json` `engines` range. (d) Delete the Node setup step, since two of the four frontend commands it serves cannot succeed anyway. (e) Raise the value to `20.20.2`, the lowest release the floor admits. | The frozen plan states twice, once in this workflow's own delivery and once in the operations carve-out DL-215 records, that every frontend step of this workflow is untouched, and it confines the operations edits to the backend build and image. `node-version` is a line of a frontend step, so changing it is an edit with no authorising requirement, which is what (a) leaves standing and what this revision closes; the earlier argument that a version is not a step does not survive contact with the requirement, which freezes the steps rather than a subset of their lines. (b) and (e) are the same edit at other values. (c) adds a file no inventory row names and still leaves the workflow input to resolve. (d) removes a step the plan holds untouched. Nothing in the backend job reads the Node toolchain — the backend is built by `mvn -B clean verify` on JDK 21 — so the value has no bearing on what this migration delivers, and the floor that governs the toolchain this migration is built with governs no line of a job the plan declares untouchable. | The workflow provisions Node 14, which reached end of life in April 2023 and receives no security fix, in a job whose four frontend commands could not succeed regardless: `npm ci` has no committed lock file and two of the scripts do not exist. A reader may reasonably take the `14` pin for an oversight, and DL-074 is the record that it is not. Raising it is a one-line change to a file this migration holds frozen, so it belongs to whoever next has authority over `frontend/**` rather than to this migration. The class this entry once named as its covering test is not in the delivered tree: the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary declares nineteen test classes and none of the guard classes that assertion relied on, so they were deleted under DL-216 and this claim is held by review against the delivered artifacts rather than by an assertion in the build. |
| DL-286 | `dto/CreateResponseRequest` binds `tweet_id` as a raw Jackson `JsonNode` and reproduces the truth value Python assigns to a decoded JSON value. `namesTweet()` reports false for an absent member, a JSON `null`, an empty string, a zero of any numeric spelling including `0`, `0.0` and `-0.0`, `false`, an empty array and an empty object, and true for every other value; a non-finite number reads true, as it does in Python. `usableTweetId()` returns the identifier text for an accepted value — a string yields its own characters, a number or a boolean its JSON spelling, and an array or object its compact JSON document — and `null` for a rejected one. `api/ResponseController` answers a rejected value with HTTP 400 and `{"error": "Tweet ID is required"}`, and passes an accepted one to `service/ResponseService`. | (a) Bind `tweet_id` as a `String` and treat a blank value as absent, which is what a typed DTO would do. (b) Bind it as a `Long` so binding rejects a non-numeric value. (c) Keep `JsonNode` but accept every carried value, so only an absent member or a JSON `null` is rejected. (d) Answer a value that names no `tweets` row with the guard's own 400 literal. (e) Declare `@NotBlank` beside `@NotNull`. | The source guard is `if not tweet_id` at `backend/app/api/responses.py:L40`, applied to a value read out of a free-form body, so its behaviour is Python's truth test over whatever the body decoded to — not a null check and not a numeric parse. Each of the eight false values is a body a client can send and the source answered 400 for, and every other value is one the source passed on. (a) loses seven of the eight: `0`, `false`, `[]` and `{}` would all reach generation. (b) changes the status code twice over — a non-numeric value becomes a binding failure and a numeric one that names no row still reaches generation. (c) is the widest deviation and sends `0` to the provider. (d) moves a documented 500 onto a 400: the source answers `Failed to generate response` for an identifier naming no row, which DL-232 owns. (e) constrains a `JsonNode` component, which Bean Validation does not evaluate as a text check, and adds a constraint the required-versus-optional parity of DL-050 excludes. | The guard is a transcription of another language's truth table and is correct only as long as that table is: a reader expecting a Java null check will be surprised that `{"tweet_id": 0}` answers 400 while `{"tweet_id": {"a": 1}}` does not. Binding a raw `JsonNode` puts the request's own JSON shape inside the DTO, so a client can send an array or an object and see it stringified into an identifier that names no row; the answer is then the source's 500, which reads as a server fault for a client mistake. `api/ResponseControllerTest` pins all eight false values and the accepted spellings, and that suite is the only thing standing between a future simplification to `String` and four silently changed status codes. |
| DL-287 | **Withdrawn.** This entry recorded `util/ConfiguredValues` as the single definition of an unset configuration value: a final class with a private constructor and two static methods, `isUnresolvedPlaceholder(String)` matching a trimmed value against `^\$\{.*}$` with `DOTALL` and `isUnset(String)` treating `null`, blank and an unresolved placeholder alike, extracted from three components that had written the same shape. **That mechanism is not in the delivered tree.** The class is deleted and the test is delivered nowhere. The two predicates are inlined into each of the three components that needs them — `security/JwtService`, `security/SecurityConfig` and `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` — each carrying the same regular expression and the same blank-or-placeholder reading, so the behaviour is unchanged at every call site. | (a) Keep the class. (b) Move the two predicates onto `config/ScannerProperties`, which is an authorised file, and have the three components call it. (c) Inline into each caller, which is what is delivered. | The migration boundary enumerates the classes of every module and declares this one in none of them, so it was deleted in the scope restoration this review round required. The extraction this entry performed was sound engineering and is undone for scope reasons alone, which is why the behaviour it defined is preserved verbatim in each caller rather than simplified away. A row that asserts a mechanism the tree does not carry is worse than no row, so this entry states what it used to do and what the tree carries instead, under its own identifier, rather than being deleted — the revision policy DL-099 states, which keeps every citation made in an earlier commit resolvable. | The unresolved-placeholder pattern now appears three times, so a change to what counts as unset must be made three times and a divergence between the three would not be caught by any single test. (b) was rejected because a configuration-properties record is a binding target and not a utility holder, and `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` runs before that record is available. |
| DL-288 | `service/SentimentAnalysisService` creates its `LanguageServiceClient` lazily on first use, under double-checked locking over a `volatile` field, from Application Default Credentials, and reuses it for the life of the bean. A `@PreDestroy` method marks the bean destroyed, waits up to thirty seconds on the lifecycle write lock for a sentiment call already in flight, then closes the client; a call arriving after destruction is refused with `IllegalStateException`. The client is built from settings that replace the generated `AnalyzeSentiment` defaults — ten minutes for the initial RPC, the maximum RPC and the total — with a ten-second per-attempt deadline and a thirty-second total deadline, leaving the retryable status codes and the retry delays as the client declares them and every other operation untouched. | (a) Construct the client eagerly in the constructor, which is what the source did at `backend/app/services/sentiment_analysis.py:L8`. (b) Publish the client as a `@Bean` and let the container own its lifecycle. (c) Create a client per call and close it in a `finally`. (d) Leave the generated call settings untouched. (e) Bound the two deadlines through configuration keys. (f) Close the client at shutdown without waiting for an in-flight call. | Application Default Credentials are resolved when the client is created, so eager construction makes credential availability a startup requirement for an application whose other ten routes do not need it, and `mvn clean verify` runs on a host with no credentials at all. Lazy creation moves that failure to the one route that needs the provider and keeps the context refreshable, while the single instance preserves the source's one-client-per-service shape and the channel pooling the generated client performs. (b) has the same lifecycle but publishes a bean that cannot be created on a credential-less host, so the context fails to refresh for the same reason as (a). (c) pays channel setup on every request and discards the pooling. (d) is the sharpest defect: the generated default lets one sentiment call hold its calling thread for ten minutes, and that thread is a request thread on `POST /tweets/{tweetId}/analyze` or one of the two scheduler threads on the ingestion path. Ten seconds per attempt and thirty in total bound the worst case at a value a caller can wait for, and narrowing `AnalyzeSentiment` alone leaves every other operation as its vendor set it. (e) adds two keys to the configuration surface this migration caps, for values no operator has a reason to move. (f) closes a channel underneath a call in progress and turns an orderly shutdown into a provider error. | The first sentiment call of a process pays the client creation, so its latency is not representative, and a credential misconfiguration surfaces on a request rather than at startup: an operator reading a green startup log has no evidence that credentials resolve. Double-checked locking is correct only while the field stays `volatile` and the destroyed flag is read inside the monitor, and only review enforces that. The thirty-second total deadline is shorter than the retry budget the generated settings imply, so a call that would have succeeded on a later attempt now fails — the intended bound, which makes a slow provider indistinguishable from an unavailable one. The shutdown wait is bounded, so a call that outlasts thirty seconds sees its client closed and reports a transport failure. |
| DL-289 | `config/RestClientConfig.headerSafe(String, String)` is the single gate every configured value passes before it becomes a default header on the Notion client. It strips the value, then discards it — returning an empty string — if any remaining character is below `U+0020` or is `U+007F`, recording one `WARN` that names the property and no part of the value. An unsafe `scanner.notion.api-key` yields an empty bearer token; an unsafe `scanner.notion.api-version` falls back to the compiled default of DL-151, so no unsafe text reaches a header on either path. | (a) Send each configured value as bound, which is what the class did before this gate existed. (b) Strip only, relying on `String.strip()` to remove a carriage return or line feed. (c) Remove the offending characters and send what is left. (d) Refuse to create the bean and fail startup on an unsafe value. (e) Log the rejected value so an operator can see what was wrong with it. (f) Validate the two properties with a Bean Validation pattern on the bound record instead. | A default header is written into the header region of every outbound message, so an embedded carriage return and line feed in a configured value is a header-injection primitive: an operator mistake or a compromised configuration source appends an arbitrary header to every Notion request. (c) sends a value nobody configured and hides the mistake. (d) is defensible for the key and wrong for the version, which has a working compiled default; failing startup for a value with a safe fallback changes the clean-checkout contract. (e) writes provider-unrelated but attacker-influenced bytes into the log, which is the injection the guard of DL-149 exists to prevent, and the property name is enough to act on. (f) places the check on the bound record, where the required-versus-optional parity of DL-050 admits no pattern constraint, and reports at binding time rather than at the point the value becomes a header. | An unsafe API key yields an empty bearer token rather than a startup failure, so the first Notion request answers 401 and the cause is one `WARN` earlier in the log; an operator who missed it sees an authentication failure and not a configuration one. The check is a character-class test and not a header grammar, so a value legal in every character and still meaningless to Notion is sent unchanged, which DL-193 records. Only these two properties are gated, so any future default header has to route through this method and nothing mechanically enforces that. The class this entry once named as its covering test is not in the delivered tree: the frozen test inventory of the migration boundary declares nineteen test classes and none of the guard classes that assertion relied on, so they were deleted under DL-216 and this claim is held by review against the delivered artifacts rather than by an assertion in the build. |
| DL-290 | `task/TweetStreamClient` stamps every subscribed ingestion cycle with a generation number and a cycle's terminal callback writes shared state only while its own generation is still current. `cycleGeneration` is an `AtomicLong`; `start()` advances it **after** the `running` compare-and-set and the blank-credential guard have both passed, so a start those guards decline leaves a live cycle's generation current; `stop()` advances it too; and `ingestionCycle(long generation)` clears `running` in its `doFinally` only when `cycleGeneration.get() == generation`. | (a) Clear `running` unconditionally in `doFinally`, which is what an earlier revision delivered. (b) Compare the terminating `Disposable` against the published `subscription` handle instead of a counter. (c) Clear the flag from `stop()` alone and never from the callback. (d) Hold the flag per cycle rather than per client, so there is nothing shared to clobber. | The terminal signal of a reactive sequence can arrive after the sequence has been disposed and after a replacement has been subscribed, so an unconditional write is a race: `stop()` then `start()` then the old cycle's `doFinally` leaves `running` false while a new cycle is live, and `isRunning()` then reports a running client as stopped for the rest of the process. A monotonic counter is the smallest thing that distinguishes the cycles, and it is compared under no lock because a single `get()` against an immutable captured value needs none. (b) is equivalent in effect but compares a handle the cycle does not carry into its own callback, so it would have to be captured in a mutable field and read back — the same race one level down. (c) loses the flag's other job, which is to report a cycle that terminated on its own. (d) would change `isRunning()` from a client property into a cycle property, and `SmartLifecycle` asks the client. | The counter grows without bound over the process lifetime, which a 64-bit value makes theoretical. A cycle whose generation is stale reports nothing when it terminates, so a cycle that failed after being replaced is visible only in its own error record and not in `isRunning()` — which is correct, because the client's state is the current cycle's state. The interleaving is forced in a test rather than left to timing: a stale generation is captured, a fresh one taken, the stale cycle subscribed and disposed, and `running` asserted still true; reverting the guard makes that test fail. |
| DL-291 | The `ai_tools` names are read a page at a time and filtered as each page is merged, so the rule cap bounds the terms collected and not the rows read. `mergeAiToolNames(Map, int wanted)` reads `AiToolRepository.findNames(PageRequest.of(page, 100))` in `id` order and passes each page through the same `mergeTerms` that merges the configured base terms — dropping a `null` or blank name, a name the rule grammar of DL-257 refuses, and a repeat without regard to letter case — then stops on the first of three conditions: the accumulator holds `wanted` terms, a page shorter than 100 rows reports the table exhausted, or 10,000 rows have been read. The third is recorded at `WARN` with the row and term counts and no name value. | (a) Read the first `cap` rows and filter afterwards, which is what an earlier revision delivered. (b) Read the whole `name` column and filter it, with no bound on rows. (c) Page and filter per page with a scan bound, which is what is delivered. (d) Express the grammar in the JPQL predicate so the database filters and keep the cap on rows. (e) Page without a scan bound, stopping only at exhaustion. | (a) bounds the wrong quantity: a table whose leading rows carry whitespace-only, grammar-refused or repeated names spends the entire budget on rows that contribute no term, and every usable name behind them is unreachable — so the union with every `ai_tools` name that AAP section 0.6.5.4 requires is silently incomplete, and an operator who adds a tool sees it never reach the stream. (b) and (e) are unbounded against a table an operator can grow without limit. (d) needs the grammar written twice, and JPQL cannot express the character-class and boundary rules at all — the existing query already goes as far as SQL can, excluding only `null` and empty. | A table holding more than 10,000 rows none of which carries an acceptable name is not fully scanned, and the shortfall is reported rather than silent; 10,000 is 400 times the default rule cap, so reaching it means the table is almost entirely unusable. Paging issues one statement per hundred rows until the cap is met rather than one statement in total, which is paid once per connection cycle and never per record. The case is covered by seeding a full rule cap of unusable rows ahead of the usable ones, and reverting the fix makes that test fail. |
| DL-292 | Text written to a Notion title or rich-text property is carried as **one item per 2,000 characters**, which is the provider's per-item ceiling. `textItems(String)` splits by character index — so concatenating the items in order reproduces the value exactly — and a value at or below the ceiling yields a single item, which is the shape the source's own rendering produced. `readText` is the inverse and concatenates every item (DL-090). No item cap, no truncation bound and no empty-array rendering of a blank value is applied: those three were part of the mechanism DL-155 originally described and they are not delivered. | (a) Write one item carrying the whole value and let the provider reject it, which is what an earlier revision delivered and which DL-155 recorded as the withdrawal. (b) Truncate to 2,000 characters and write one item. (c) Reject a longer value before the request. (d) Split and also cap the item count and truncate beyond a total bound, which is DL-155's original form. (e) Split on a word boundary rather than by index. | The long-value case is reachable: the columns these values come from are generated as `text` on MySQL and `varchar(32600)` on PostgreSQL and H2 (DL-068), an order of magnitude above the ceiling, so `tweets.content` and a generated reply can both exceed it — which is why DL-155's withdrawal rationale, that the columns bounded the values below the limit, no longer holds. (a) makes a valid primary relational row permanently unmirrored through a provider validation rejection, and mirroring failures are silent by design (DL-194), so the loss would not be reported. (b) and (c) discard content the provider would accept once it is split. (d) adds two bounds nothing in the tree can reach. (e) would make the split content-dependent, so a value with no separator inside 2,000 characters could not be split at all, and the round trip would no longer be exact. | The reassembly assumes the provider returns items in the order they were written, which it does; a provider that reordered them would return a scrambled value rather than a truncated one. A value already mirrored as a single over-long item by the withdrawn behaviour was never written at all, so there is no stored data to migrate. The split and the concatenation are asserted together at eight lengths from 1 to 12,345 characters, against text whose every 2,000-character window differs so a reordered or dropped window is detected, and reverting either side makes those tests fail. |
| DL-293 | `server.forward-headers-strategy` is `${FORWARD_HEADERS_STRATEGY:framework}`, so `HttpServletRequest.isSecure()` reports the scheme carried by `X-Forwarded-Proto`. The `Strict-Transport-Security` header `security/SecurityConfig` declares inline on the filter chain (DL-277) is written on a request the container reports as secure, so behind a TLS terminator such as Cloud Run this value is what causes that header to be written at all. | (a) Leave the property unset, which is Spring Boot's `none` default. (b) Set `native` and let the container's own valve read the headers. (c) Remove the inline header declaration so the property has no consumer. | Cloud Run terminates TLS and forwards the original scheme in `X-Forwarded-Proto`, so under (a) every forwarded request reports itself insecure and the header is never written. `framework` has Spring's own `ForwardedHeaderFilter` apply the headers, which is the strategy that works identically on the embedded container and under `@WebMvcTest`. (b) ties the behaviour to Tomcat. (c) was the reading an earlier revision of DL-277 recorded and it does not match the delivered chain. | The forwarded headers are trusted unconditionally, so a deployment that exposes the container directly to untrusted clients would let a client assert `X-Forwarded-Proto: https`. The delivered topology puts Cloud Run in front, which overwrites the header. No test asserts the property value. |
| DL-294 | `server.shutdown` is `${SERVER_SHUTDOWN:graceful}` and `spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase` is `${SHUTDOWN_GRACE_PERIOD:30s}`. The connector stops accepting requests and drains those in flight before the context closes, and the drain is abandoned at the stated bound. The single pooled `DataSource` of `config/DataSourceConfig` therefore closes after the last in-flight request rather than underneath it. `ScannerApplicationTests.declaresABoundedGracefulShutdown` asserts both values. | (a) Leave both unset, which is Spring Boot's immediate shutdown. (b) Declare `graceful` with no phase bound, so a stuck request holds the process open. (c) Carry a longer bound. | The retired tree had no shutdown behaviour to port: its background paths never returned at all (`main.py:L41-48`, A2). Two delivered components hold resources across a request — the connection pool and the `SmartLifecycle` stream client of `task/TweetStreamClient` — and a bounded drain closes them in an order that does not fail a request already accepted. (b) makes shutdown unbounded, which a container orchestrator resolves with a kill. This pair was recorded under DL-271 while that entry also owned the connection-pool allowlist; the allowlist was withdrawn and these two keys are delivered, so they are recorded here under their own identifier. | A deployment that overrides `SHUTDOWN_GRACE_PERIOD` above the orchestrator's own termination grace period gains nothing, since the kill arrives first. The bound is a ceiling and not a guarantee: a request still running at 30 seconds is abandoned mid-flight, and a write it had begun is left to the transaction boundary to roll back. |
| DL-295 | The serialization the retired controllers called and never defined is carried by three net-new classes: `service/mapper/TweetMapper`, `service/mapper/ResponseMapper` and `service/mapper/SettingMapper`. `to_dict()` is invoked ten times across `api/tweets.py:L19,L30` and `api/responses.py:L18,L29,L47,L63`, and `backend/app/db/models.py` declares no such method on any model (defect A7). Each mapper converts one entity, or one closed projection, into one wire record and performs no read of its own. | (a) Declare a `toDto()` method on each entity. (b) Let Jackson serialise the entities directly and annotate the entity fields. (c) Generate the mappers with MapStruct. (d) Fold the conversion into each service. | (a) and (b) both put wire concerns on the persistence type, and (b) additionally serialises whatever the entity holds, including the lazy association `entity/Tweet.responses` declares. A separate mapper is the construct that carries the `Integer`-to-`String` identifier conversion of DL-023 and the null-preserving component types of DL-133 in one place. (c) adds an annotation processor for three classes. (d) repeats the conversion in every service that returns a record. | A mapper and its record can drift: adding a component to a DTO without adding the assignment leaves a silently null member. The controller tests read every member of every record they assert, which is what detects that. |
| DL-296 | In `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md`, the fifteen targets AAP section 0.7.3 enumerates carry the literal marker *No source construct — net-new* in the source column, and where the retired tree holds a call site, an import or a configuration value that fixes the target's signature, that site is named in a separate **Occasioned by** clause. The marker and the clause are both present on the same row. | (a) Mark only the targets with no occasioning site at all as net-new and give the others their occasioning site as the source construct, which is the shape AAP section 0.4.1 uses for `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` and `util/DelimitedStringListConverter`. (b) Carry the marker alone and drop the occasioning site. (c) Carry two distinct markers, one per shape, which is the form an earlier revision of the matrix used. | AAP section 0.4.1 records an occasioning source file for several of the fifteen while section 0.7.3 requires all fifteen to be marked as having no source construct; the two sections describe the same targets. Carrying both the marker and the clause satisfies section 0.7.3 literally and keeps the provenance section 0.4.1 records. (a) satisfies 0.4.1 and fails 0.7.3. (b) satisfies 0.7.3 and discards provenance the matrix exists to carry. (c) was the delivered form until this review round and it reports nine of the fifteen under a marker 0.7.3 does not name. | A reader who counts only rows whose source column reads *No source construct — net-new* now counts fifteen, where an earlier revision of the matrix reported eleven under that exact wording; the change is in the marking and not in the tree. The **Occasioned by** clause is prose and no check verifies that the site it names exists. |
